Unit 1 — Linear Algebra: Matrices & Systems 75
Matrix operations 22
EM-U1-Q01MCQeasy

Let A be a 3 × 2 matrix and B a 2 × 4 matrix. Which statement is TRUE?

  1. AB is defined and is a 3 × 4 matrix, while BA is not defined.
  2. BA is defined and is a 2 × 2 matrix, while AB is not defined.
  3. Both AB and BA are defined.
  4. Neither product is defined.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. The product XY is defined only when the number of columns of X equals the number of rows of Y, and the result then has the rows of X and the columns of Y.

Step by step.

  1. A is 3 × 2 and B is 2 × 4. For AB the inner dimensions are 2 = 2, so AB is defined and is 3 × 4.
  2. For BA the inner dimensions are 4 (columns of B) and 3 (rows of A). Since 4 ≠ 3, BA is not defined.
  3. Hence exactly one of the two products exists, and it is 3 × 4.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Students check only whether both matrices are non-square, or assume that if AB exists then BA must too. Matrix multiplication is not commutative and the two products can have different orders or fail to exist at all.

EM-U1-Q02MCQeasy

If A and B are n × n real matrices with AB = 0, which of the following must be TRUE?

  1. A = 0 or B = 0
  2. det(A) = 0 or det(B) = 0
  3. Both A and B are invertible
  4. A = B
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. The determinant is multiplicative: det(AB) = det(A)·det(B).

Step by step.

  1. Suppose, for contradiction, that det(A) ≠ 0 and det(B) ≠ 0. Then both are invertible.
  2. Then AB is invertible, so det(AB) ≠ 0.
  3. But AB = 0 gives det(AB) = 0, a contradiction. So at least one determinant is zero.

Answer: Option (B).

Common misconception. The error is believing that a zero product forces a zero factor, as it does for real numbers. For matrices, AB = 0 can hold with both factors non-zero, e.g. A = [[1,0],[0,0]], B = [[0,0],[1,0]]. The correct conclusion is only singularity, not zero-ness.

EM-U1-Q03MCQeasy

For square matrices A and B of the same order, (A + B)² equals

  1. A² + 2AB + B²
  2. A² + AB + BA + B²
  3. A² + B²
  4. A² + 2BA + B²
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Expand the product and use distributivity; do not assume commutativity.

Step by step.

  1. (A + B)² = (A + B)(A + B) = A·A + A·B + B·A + B·B.
  2. That is A² + AB + BA + B².
  3. The two middle terms can be merged only when AB = BA, which is not given.

Answer: Option (B).

Common misconception. Writing A² + 2AB + B² by analogy with the scalar binomial expansion. That step silently assumes commutativity, which fails for general matrices.

EM-U1-Q04MCQmedium

Let A be a 3 × 3 matrix with det(A) = 2. Then det(adj A) equals

  1. 2
  2. 4
  3. 8
  4. 1/2
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. For an n × n matrix, A · adj(A) = det(A) I, hence det(adj A) = det(A)^(n−1).

Step by step.

  1. Take determinants in A · adj(A) = det(A) I: det(A)·det(adj A) = det(A)ⁿ.
  2. With det(A) ≠ 0 this gives det(adj A) = det(A)^(n−1).
  3. Here n = 3 and det(A) = 2, so det(adj A) = 2² = 4.

Answer: Option (B) — 4.

Common misconception. Using the exponent n instead of n − 1, or applying the scalar rule det(kA) = k·det(A) here. The exponent depends on the order of the matrix, so for n = 3 the factor is det(A)².

EM-U1-Q05MCQmedium

Let A be a real 3 × 3 skew-symmetric matrix, i.e. Aᵀ = −A. Which of the following must hold?

  1. det(A) = 0
  2. det(A) > 0
  3. A is invertible
  4. trace(A) = 3
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Determinant under transpose, and the effect of a scalar multiple on a determinant of odd order.

Step by step.

  1. det(A) = det(Aᵀ) = det(−A).
  2. For a 3 × 3 matrix, det(−A) = (−1)³ det(A) = −det(A).
  3. So det(A) = −det(A), giving 2·det(A) = 0, i.e. det(A) = 0.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Assuming a skew-symmetric matrix is invertible because it looks 'structured'. In fact every real skew-symmetric matrix of odd order is singular; the determinant is zero because the eigenvalues are 0 or purely imaginary pairs. Also note trace(A) = 0 for any skew-symmetric matrix, not 3.

EM-U1-Q16MSQeasy

For real square matrices A and B of the same order, which statements are TRUE?

  1. (AB)ᵀ = BᵀAᵀ
  2. (A + B)ᵀ = Aᵀ + Bᵀ
  3. tr(AB) = tr(BA)
  4. If A and B are invertible then (AB)⁻¹ = A⁻¹B⁻¹
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Transpose reverses products but distributes over sums; trace is invariant under cyclic permutation; inverses reverse order.

Step by step.

  1. (AB)ᵀ = BᵀAᵀ — true: the (i,j) entry of (AB)ᵀ is the (j,i) entry of AB, which is (BᵀAᵀ)_ij.
  2. (A + B)ᵀ = Aᵀ + Bᵀ — true: transpose is additive.
  3. tr(AB) = Σᵢ (AB)_ii = Σᵢ Σ_k a_ik b_ki = tr(BA) — true.
  4. (AB)⁻¹ = B⁻¹A⁻¹, not A⁻¹B⁻¹ — false as written.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Option (D) is the classic slip: order must be reversed for both transpose and inverse. Only when A and B commute does A⁻¹B⁻¹ happen to coincide with the correct inverse.

EM-U1-Q20MSQeasy

Which of the following statements are TRUE for real square matrices?

  1. If A is 2 × 2 and A² = I, then A = I or A = −I.
  2. If A is invertible, then Aᵀ is invertible.
  3. If A and B are invertible n × n matrices, then AB is invertible.
  4. The inverse of an invertible upper-triangular matrix with non-zero diagonal entries is upper-triangular.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (B), (C) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Invertibility under transpose and products, plus the triangular structure of inverses.

Step by step.

  1. (A) False: A = [[0, 1], [1, 0]] satisfies A² = I but is neither I nor −I.
  2. (B) True: det(Aᵀ) = det(A) ≠ 0.
  3. (C) True: det(AB) = det(A)det(B) ≠ 0, so AB is invertible with inverse B⁻¹A⁻¹.
  4. (D) True: if U is upper-triangular with non-zero diagonal, solving UX = I by back substitution gives an upper-triangular X = U⁻¹.

Answer: Options (B), (C) and (D).

Common misconception. Option (A) — treating matrix squares like scalar squares. Over the reals, A² = I gives (A − I)(A + I) = 0, which does not force either factor to vanish because matrices have zero divisors.

EM-U1-Q21NATeasy

Let A be a 3 × 3 real matrix with det(A) = 5. Find det(2A).

Show answer & solution

Answer: 40

Detailed solution

Concept. Scaling an n × n matrix by k multiplies the determinant by kⁿ, not by k.

Step by step.

  1. Every one of the 3 rows is multiplied by 2, and each such multiplication scales the determinant by 2.
  2. Hence det(2A) = 2³ · det(A).
  3. det(2A) = 8 × 5 = 40.

Answer: 40

Common misconception. Writing det(2A) = 2·det(A) = 10. The factor 2 is applied to all n rows, so it is raised to the order of the matrix. Only for 1 × 1 matrices do the two rules coincide.

EM-U1-Q26MCQeasy

Let A be a 4 × 3 matrix, B a 3 × 5 matrix and C a 5 × 2 matrix. Which of the following products is defined, and what is its order?

  1. AC is defined and is 4 × 2.
  2. CB is defined and is 5 × 3.
  3. ABC is defined and is 4 × 2.
  4. BA is defined and is 3 × 3.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. The product XY is defined exactly when the number of columns of X equals the number of rows of Y, and the result has the row count of X and the column count of Y. Conformability chains through a product of three matrices.

Step by step.

  1. AC: A has 3 columns, C has 5 rows — not equal, so AC is undefined.
  2. CB: C has 2 columns, B has 3 rows — undefined.
  3. AB is defined, 4 × 5; the inner sizes agree: (4 × 3)(3 × 5).
  4. That product then multiplies C as (4 × 5)(5 × 2), which is conformable, so ABC is defined and is 4 × 2.
  5. BA: B has 5 columns, A has 4 rows — undefined.

Answer: Option (C)

Common misconception. Students check A against C and stop at the first mismatch, concluding that the triple product fails. The correct test is pairwise and left to right: A with B, then the result with C.

EM-U1-Q27MCQeasy

Let A be a 3 × 3 upper-triangular matrix whose diagonal entries are 2, −3, 4. Then det(A) equals

  1. −24
  2. 24
  3. 9
  4. 0
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. The determinant of a triangular matrix (upper or lower) is the product of its diagonal entries: all other terms of the Leibniz expansion contain a zero factor.

Step by step.

  1. A is upper triangular, so every term of det(A) except the diagonal product contains an entry below the diagonal, which is 0.
  2. det(A) = 2 · (−3) · 4 = −24.

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Adding the diagonal entries, 2 + (−3) + 4 = 3, and picking the nearest option. The rule is a product, not a sum — and the sign matters.

EM-U1-Q28MCQmedium

Let A be a 4 × 4 real matrix with det(A) = 3. Then det(2A⁻¹) equals

  1. 16/3
  2. 2/3
  3. 16
  4. 3/16
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Two determinant rules compose here: det(A⁻¹) = 1/det(A) and det(kA) = kⁿ det(A) for an n × n matrix.

Step by step.

  1. det(A⁻¹) = 1/3.
  2. Scaling A⁻¹ by 2 multiplies its determinant by 2⁴ = 16.
  3. det(2A⁻¹) = 16 · (1/3) = 16/3.

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Applying the scaling to the inverse first, (2A)⁻¹ = A⁻¹/2, or using 2 instead of 2⁴. Write the order n down before applying kⁿ; with n = 4 the factor is 16, not 2.

EM-U1-Q29MCQmedium

Let A and B be invertible n × n matrices. Then (AᵀB)⁻¹ equals

  1. (A⁻¹)ᵀ B⁻¹
  2. B⁻¹ (A⁻¹)ᵀ
  3. B⁻¹ A⁻¹
  4. A⁻¹ B⁻¹
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. The inverse of a product reverses the order: (XY)⁻¹ = Y⁻¹X⁻¹. Also (Aᵀ)⁻¹ = (A⁻¹)ᵀ, so it does not matter whether the transpose or the inverse is taken first.

Step by step.

  1. Put X = Aᵀ and Y = B. Then (XY)⁻¹ = Y⁻¹X⁻¹ = B⁻¹(Aᵀ)⁻¹.
  2. Since (Aᵀ)⁻¹ = (A⁻¹)ᵀ, this is B⁻¹(A⁻¹)ᵀ.
  3. Check: (AᵀB)·B⁻¹(A⁻¹)ᵀ = Aᵀ(BB⁻¹)(A⁻¹)ᵀ = Aᵀ(A⁻¹)ᵀ = (A⁻¹A)ᵀ = I.

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Option (A) keeps the original order and option (D) forgets the transpose. Both fail the multiplication check above as soon as A and B do not commute.

EM-U1-Q41MSQeasy

Let A and B be 2 × 2 real matrices with det(A) = 2 and det(B) = 3. Which statements are TRUE?

  1. det(AB) = 6
  2. det(A + B) = 5
  3. det(A⁻¹) = 1/2
  4. det(Aᵀ) = 3
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. The determinant is multiplicative and invariant under transpose; it is additive in neither the matrix nor, in general, the scalar.

Step by step.

  1. det(AB) = det(A)det(B) = 2 · 3 = 6 — true.
  2. det(A + B) has no rule in terms of det(A) and det(B); take A = I, B = 2I: det(A + B) = 9 ≠ 3 — false.
  3. det(A⁻¹) = 1/det(A) = 1/2 — true.
  4. det(Aᵀ) = det(A) = 2, not 3 — false.

Answer: Options (A) and (C)

Common misconception. Treating the determinant as linear, so that det(A + B) = det(A) + det(B). Linearity holds row by row, not for the whole matrix sum.

EM-U1-Q45MSQmedium

Let A, B, C be n × n real matrices. Which statements are TRUE?

  1. If A² = 0 then A is singular
  2. If A² = A then det(A) ∈ {0, 1}
  3. If A is invertible and AB = AC then B = C
  4. Every square matrix has an inverse
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. The determinant turns matrix multiplication into ordinary multiplication, so polynomial relations among matrices become relations among numbers.

Step by step.

  1. A² = 0 gives det(A)² = 0, hence det(A) = 0 and A is singular — true.
  2. A² = A gives det(A)² = det(A), so det(A) is a real root of t² = t, namely 0 or 1 — true.
  3. Multiply AB = AC on the left by A⁻¹ to get B = C — true.
  4. A square matrix is invertible only if det(A) ≠ 0; the zero matrix is a counterexample — false.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Common misconception. Cancelling A without invertibility. In (C) the hypothesis supplies A⁻¹; in general AB = AC does not imply B = C when A is singular.

EM-U1-Q46NATeasy

Let A = [[2, 1], [1, 1]]. Find the (1, 2) entry of A⁻¹. (Enter an integer; the value may be negative.)

Show answer & solution

Answer: -1

Detailed solution

Concept. For a 2 × 2 matrix [[a, b], [c, d]] with ad − bc ≠ 0, the inverse is (1/(ad − bc))·[[d, −b], [−c, a]] — the diagonal entries swap and the off-diagonal entries change sign.

Step by step.

  1. det(A) = 2·1 − 1·1 = 1.
  2. A⁻¹ = [[1, −1], [−1, 2]].
  3. The (1, 2) entry is −1.
  4. Check: A·A⁻¹ = [[2·1 − 1·1, −2 + 2], [1 − 1, −1 + 2]] = I.

Answer: −1

Common misconception. Reporting the (2, 1) entry, or forgetting the sign change on the off-diagonal entries and answering 1. The adjugate puts −b in position (1, 2) and −c in position (2, 1).

EM-U1-Q51MCQeasy

Let A be a 3 × 3 real matrix with AᵀA = I. Then det(A) is

  1. 1 or −1
  2. 1
  3. −1
  4. 0
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Taking determinants of both sides, det(AᵀA) = det(Aᵀ)·det(A) = det(A)², and det(I) = 1.

Step by step.

  1. Apply the product rule: det(AᵀA) = det(Aᵀ)·det(A).
  2. Use det(Aᵀ) = det(A), so the left side is det(A)².
  3. The right side is det(I) = 1, hence det(A)² = 1.
  4. Over ℝ that gives det(A) = 1 or det(A) = −1, and both occur: A = I and A = −I.

Answer: Option (A) — det(A) = ±1.

Common misconception. Reading AᵀA = I as a statement about orthogonality and concluding det(A) = 1. −I satisfies AᵀA = I too, and has determinant −1.

Takeaway. A condition on a product of matrices becomes an equation on determinants; solve that equation, do not assume its sign.

EM-U1-Q52MCQeasy

A and B are 3 × 3 real matrices with det(A) = 2 and det(B) = −3. Then det(ABᵀ) equals

  1. 6
  2. −5
  3. 5
  4. −6
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. The determinant is multiplicative, det(XY) = det(X)·det(Y), and a transpose does not change it, det(Bᵀ) = det(B).

Step by step.

  1. Split the product: det(ABᵀ) = det(A)·det(Bᵀ).
  2. Replace the transpose by the original determinant: det(Bᵀ) = det(B) = −3.
  3. Multiply: 2 × (−3) = −6.

Answer: Option (D) — −6.

Common misconception. Adding the two determinants (2 + (−3) = −1) instead of multiplying them, or dropping the sign carried by det(B).

Takeaway. Determinants turn products into products; the only care needed is with the sign.

EM-U1-Q53MCQmedium

For n × n real matrices A and B, which one of the following is always TRUE?

  1. tr(AB) = tr(A)·tr(B)
  2. tr(AB) = tr(BA)
  3. tr(A²) = (tr(A))²
  4. tr(Aᵀ) = −tr(A)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. The trace is the sum of the diagonal entries, and (AB)ᵢᵢ = Σₖ aᵢₖbₖᵢ while (BA)ᵢᵢ = Σₖ bᵢₖaₖᵢ. Summing over i in both cases adds every product aᵢₖbₖᵢ once.

Step by step.

  1. Write tr(AB) = Σᵢ Σₖ aᵢₖ bₖᵢ.
  2. Write tr(BA) = Σᵢ Σₖ bᵢₖ aₖᵢ.
  3. Rename the summation letters (i ↔ k) in the second sum: it becomes Σₖ Σᵢ aᵢₖ bₖᵢ, the same list of terms.
  4. So the two traces are equal for every pair of square matrices of the same order.

Answer: Option (B) — tr(AB) = tr(BA) always.

Common misconception. Assuming the trace is multiplicative like the determinant. It is not: with A = B = [[1, 0], [0, 0]], tr(AB) = 1 but tr(A)·tr(B) = 1 × 1 only by coincidence — take A = [[1, 1], [0, 0]], B = [[1, 0], [1, 0]] and tr(AB) = 2 while tr(A)·tr(B) = 1.

Takeaway. The trace is linear and cyclic (tr(ABC) = tr(BCA)) but never multiplicative.

EM-U1-Q54MSQmedium

A and B are n × n real matrices. Select every statement that is TRUE.

  1. det(AB) = det(BA)
  2. det(A + B) = det(A) + det(B)
  3. det(Aᵀ) = det(A)
  4. If A² = I then det(A) = 1.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. The determinant is multiplicative and blind to transposition; it is not additive.

Step by step.

  1. (A) det(AB) = det(A)det(B) = det(B)det(A) = det(BA), since the determinant is a real number and numbers commute.
  2. (B) fails already for A = B = I with n = 2: det(2I) = 4 but det(I) + det(I) = 2.
  3. (C) holds because expanding along a row or a column gives the same sum for A and Aᵀ.
  4. (D) fails: A = −I has A² = I and det(A) = (−1)ⁿ, which is −1 for odd n.

Answer: Options (A) and (C).

Common misconception. Transferring the scalar rule (a + b) to matrices, or forgetting that A² = I allows −I.

Takeaway. Determinant: multiplicative and transpose-invariant, never additive.

EM-U1-Q55NATeasy

Let A = [[1, 2], [0, 3]] and B = [[2, 0], [1, 1]]. The (2, 1) entry of AB is ______.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 3

Detailed solution

Concept. The (i, j) entry of AB is the dot product of row i of A with column j of B.

Step by step.

  1. Row 2 of A is [0, 3].
  2. Column 1 of B is [2, 1]ᵀ.
  3. Dot product: 0 × 2 + 3 × 1 = 3.
  4. Check against the full product AB = [[4, 2], [3, 3]].

Answer: 3.

Common misconception. Multiplying column 2 of A by row 1 of B, which computes the (2, 1) entry of BA = [[2, 4], [1, 5]] and gives 1.

Takeaway. Row of the left factor, column of the right factor — in that order.

EM-U1-Q56NATeasy

A is a 4 × 4 real matrix with det(A) = 2. Then det(adj A) is ______.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 8

Detailed solution

Concept. For an n × n matrix, A·adj(A) = det(A)·I, so det(adj A) = (det A)ⁿ⁻¹.

Step by step.

  1. Take determinants in A·adj(A) = det(A)·I: det(A)·det(adj A) = (det A)ⁿ.
  2. Divide by det(A) ≠ 0: det(adj A) = (det A)ⁿ⁻¹.
  3. Here n = 4 and det(A) = 2, so det(adj A) = 2³ = 8.

Answer: 8.

Common misconception. Using the exponent n instead of n − 1 and answering 16.

Takeaway. The adjugate costs one power of the determinant: (det A)ⁿ⁻¹.

EM-U1-Q57MCQhard

A is a 3 × 3 real matrix with A³ = I and A ≠ I. Which one of the following must be TRUE?

  1. det(A) = −1
  2. trace(A) = 3
  3. det(A) = 1
  4. A = Aᵀ
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. A matrix equation A³ = I passes to determinants: det(A)³ = 1. Over ℝ the only cube root of 1 is 1.

Step by step.

  1. Take determinants: det(A³) = det(A)³ = det(I) = 1.
  2. Since det(A) is real and its cube is 1, det(A) = 1.
  3. The condition A ≠ I does not change this — it only rules out the simplest example.
  4. It does rule out the other options: trace(A) = 3 would force all three eigenvalues to be 1, and with A³ = I (whose minimal polynomial has distinct roots) that makes A = I. A rotation by 120° on a plane, padded with a 1, satisfies A³ = I, has det(A) = 1, trace(A) = 0 and is not symmetric.

Answer: Option (C) — det(A) = 1.

Common misconception. Carrying over the A² = I habit and answering det(A) = ±1. The real cube roots of 1 are just 1; −1 is a square root, not a cube root.

Takeaway. For Aᵏ = I over ℝ, det(A) = 1 when k is odd and det(A) = ±1 when k is even.

Rank & nullity 21
EM-U1-Q06MCQmedium

A 4 × 6 real matrix A has rank 4. The nullity of A is

  1. 0
  2. 2
  3. 4
  4. 6
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. The rank–nullity theorem: for an m × n matrix, rank(A) + nullity(A) = n, the number of columns.

Step by step.

  1. Here m = 4, n = 6.
  2. nullity(A) = n − rank(A) = 6 − 4 = 2.
  3. Equivalently the solution space of Ax = 0 has dimension 2.

Answer: Option (B) — 2.

Common misconception. Using the number of rows (4 − 4 = 0) instead of the number of columns. The rank–nullity theorem counts the domain dimension n, never m.

EM-U1-Q07MCQeasy

Every row of a 3 × 3 real matrix A equals the same non-zero vector. The rank of A is

  1. 0
  2. 1
  3. 2
  4. 3
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. The rank is the maximum number of linearly independent rows.

Step by step.

  1. All three rows are equal to one non-zero vector v.
  2. Any two rows are scalar multiples of each other, so at most one row is independent.
  3. Since v ≠ 0, that one row is independent, so rank(A) = 1.

Answer: Option (B) — 1.

Common misconception. Answering 3 because the matrix 'has three rows', or 0 because the rows are repeated. Rank counts independent directions, not rows; repetition reduces rank to 1 but a non-zero vector still contributes rank 1.

EM-U1-Q08MCQmedium

Let A be a 5 × 5 real matrix with rank(A) = 3. Which statement is TRUE?

  1. rank(Aᵀ) = 3 and nullity(A) = 2
  2. rank(Aᵀ) = 2
  3. nullity(A) = 3
  4. A is invertible
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Row rank equals column rank, so rank(Aᵀ) = rank(A); and rank + nullity = n.

Step by step.

  1. rank(Aᵀ) = rank(A) = 3.
  2. nullity(A) = 5 − 3 = 2.
  3. A is singular because rank(A) = 3 < 5, so option (D) fails.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Believing rank(Aᵀ) may differ from rank(A) for non-symmetric matrices. Row rank and column rank are always equal, for every real matrix.

EM-U1-Q09MCQhard

Let A be 3 × 5 and B be 5 × 3 real matrices with AB = I₃. Which statement is TRUE?

  1. rank(A) = 3 and nullity(A) = 2
  2. rank(A) = 5
  3. nullity(B) = 2
  4. BA = I₅
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Rank is submultiplicative: rank(XY) ≤ min(rank X, rank Y). Also rank(A) ≤ min(m, n).

Step by step.

  1. rank(AB) = rank(I₃) = 3.
  2. 3 = rank(AB) ≤ rank(A), and rank(A) ≤ min(3, 5) = 3, so rank(A) = 3.
  3. Then nullity(A) = 5 − 3 = 2, which confirms option (A).
  4. Similarly rank(B) = 3 and nullity(B) = 3 − 3 = 0, so (C) fails. BA would be 5 × 5, not I₅ in general — only AB = I₃ is given, so (D) fails.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Concluding BA = I₅ from AB = I₃. A right inverse of a wide matrix is not a two-sided inverse; here B is a right inverse of A only, and BA is a non-identity projection of rank 3.

EM-U1-Q17MSQmedium

Let A be a 6 × 4 real matrix with rank(A) = 3. Which statements are TRUE?

  1. nullity(A) = 1
  2. The columns of A span ℝ³
  3. rank(Aᵀ) = 3
  4. The six rows of A are linearly dependent
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Rank–nullity counts columns; row rank equals column rank; the column space lives in ℝ^m, not ℝ^r.

Step by step.

  1. nullity(A) = 4 − 3 = 1 — (A) is true.
  2. The columns are vectors in ℝ⁶ (six entries each), spanning a 3-dimensional subspace of ℝ⁶. They do not span ℝ³ — (B) is false.
  3. Row rank equals column rank, so rank(Aᵀ) = 3 — (C) is true.
  4. Six rows live in ℝ⁴, so at most 4 can be independent; with rank 3 they are dependent — (D) is true.

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D).

Common misconception. Option (B): students match the rank 3 with ℝ³. Rank gives the dimension of the column space; the ambient space is still ℝ⁶ because the matrix has six rows.

EM-U1-Q22NATeasy

A 5 × 8 real matrix A has rank 5. Find the nullity of A. (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Answer: 3

Detailed solution

Concept. Rank–nullity: rank(A) + nullity(A) = n, where n is the number of columns.

Step by step.

  1. m = 5, n = 8, rank(A) = 5.
  2. nullity(A) = 8 − 5 = 3.
  3. So the solution space of Ax = 0 has dimension 3.

Answer: 3

Common misconception. Reporting 0 because the rank already equals the number of rows. Full row rank does not force trivial null space; only full column rank does.

EM-U1-Q25NAThard

Let A be a 4 × 4 real matrix with A² = A and rank(A) = 3. Find trace(A). (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Answer: 3

Detailed solution

Concept. A matrix satisfying A² = A is a projection: it is diagonalisable with eigenvalues only 0 and 1, so its trace counts the eigenvalues equal to 1, i.e. the rank.

Step by step.

  1. If λ is an eigenvalue with eigenvector v, then A²v = λ²v and also A²v = Av = λv, so λ² = λ, giving λ ∈ {0, 1}.
  2. A² = A implies A is diagonalisable (its minimal polynomial x² − x has distinct roots), so the algebraic multiplicity of each eigenvalue equals its geometric multiplicity.
  3. The multiplicity of λ = 1 is dim(Col A) = rank(A) = 3; the multiplicity of λ = 0 is nullity(A) = 1.
  4. trace(A) = 3·1 + 1·0 = 3.

Answer: 3

Common misconception. Guessing the trace from the diagonal entries of a particular example, or assuming A² = A forces A = I. Projections onto a proper subspace satisfy A² = A with trace strictly less than the order — here 3 < 4, exactly as expected.

EM-U1-Q30MCQeasy

Let A be a 3 × 7 real matrix. The nullity of A is always at least

  1. 0
  2. 3
  3. 4
  4. 7
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Rank–nullity: rank(A) + nullity(A) = n, the number of columns. The rank of an m × n matrix is at most min(m, n).

Step by step.

  1. Here m = 3 and n = 7, so rank(A) ≤ 3.
  2. nullity(A) = 7 − rank(A) ≥ 7 − 3 = 4.
  3. The bound is attained by any matrix of full row rank, e.g. [I₃ | 0].

Answer: Option (C)

Common misconception. Using m instead of n in rank–nullity, which gives 3 − 3 = 0. The null space lives in ℝ⁷, so the column count is the one that matters.

EM-U1-Q31MCQeasy

The dimension of the column space of a 4 × 6 real matrix is at most

  1. 4
  2. 6
  3. 10
  4. 24
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. The column space of an m × n matrix is a subspace of ℝᵐ, and its dimension is the rank, which is at most min(m, n).

Step by step.

  1. Each of the 6 columns is a vector in ℝ⁴, so any spanning set of them spans a subspace of dimension at most 4.
  2. Equivalently rank(A) ≤ min(4, 6) = 4.
  3. Equality holds whenever the matrix has full row rank.

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Answering 6 because there are six columns. Having six column vectors does not make them independent — the ambient space ℝ⁴ caps the dimension.

EM-U1-Q32MCQmedium

Let A be 4 × 3 with rank(A) = 3 and B be 3 × 5 with rank(B) = 2. The largest possible value of rank(AB) is

  1. 1
  2. 2
  3. 3
  4. 5
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. For conformable matrices, rank(AB) ≤ min(rank(A), rank(B)). The bound is sharp: it is attainable when the images line up.

Step by step.

  1. rank(AB) ≤ min(3, 2) = 2, so 2 is an upper bound.
  2. Attainability: rank(B) = 2 means the image of B is a 2-dimensional subspace S ⊆ ℝ³.
  3. rank(A) = 3 with 3 columns makes A injective, so it maps S to a subspace of the same dimension 2.
  4. Hence rank(AB) = 2 for such a pair, and the bound is achieved.

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Choosing 3 from A alone. The product can never create rank that B does not have; the smaller of the two ranks is the ceiling.

EM-U1-Q33MCQhard

Let A be a 3 × 3 real matrix with A² = 0 but A ≠ 0. Then rank(A) must be

  1. 1
  2. 2
  3. 3
  4. cannot be determined from the data
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. If A² = 0 then the image of A lies inside the kernel of A, because for every vector v, A(Av) = A²v = 0. Hence col(A) ⊆ ker(A).

Step by step.

  1. Taking dimensions: rank(A) ≤ nullity(A).
  2. Rank–nullity with n = 3 gives rank(A) + nullity(A) = 3, so 2·rank(A) ≤ 3.
  3. As a rank is an integer, rank(A) ≤ 1.
  4. A ≠ 0 forces rank(A) ≥ 1, so rank(A) = 1.
  5. Example: A = [[0, 1, 0], [0, 0, 0], [0, 0, 0]] has A² = 0 and rank 1.

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Reading A² = 0 as A = 0 and answering that the rank is 0, or assuming the rank can be 2. Nilpotency of index 2 forces the rank to be at most n/2, not zero.

EM-U1-Q42MSQmedium

Let A be an m × n real matrix of rank r. Which statements are TRUE?

  1. r ≤ min(m, n)
  2. rank(Aᵀ) = r
  3. r + nullity(A) = m
  4. If r = n then Ax = 0 has only the trivial solution
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. The rank is the common dimension of the row and column spaces; rank–nullity counts columns, not rows.

Step by step.

  1. r ≤ min(m, n): the column space lies in ℝᵐ and is spanned by n vectors — true.
  2. Row rank equals column rank, so rank(Aᵀ) = rank(A) = r — true.
  3. Rank–nullity is r + nullity(A) = n, not m — false.
  4. r = n means full column rank, so nullity(A) = 0 and Ax = 0 has only x = 0 — true.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Common misconception. Writing rank–nullity with m. The null space consists of vectors x ∈ ℝⁿ, so n is the constant that belongs there.

EM-U1-Q47NATmedium

Let A be a 3 × 5 real matrix with rank(A) = 3. Find the nullity of Aᵀ. (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Answer: 0

Detailed solution

Concept. Rank–nullity is applied to the matrix you actually have, counting its own columns. Also rank(Aᵀ) = rank(A).

Step by step.

  1. Aᵀ is 5 × 3, so it has 3 columns.
  2. rank(Aᵀ) = rank(A) = 3.
  3. nullity(Aᵀ) = 3 − 3 = 0.
  4. Sanity check: nullity(A) = 5 − 3 = 2, so the two nullities differ — the transposed matrix has different dimensions.

Answer: 0

Common misconception. Answering 2 by reusing nullity(A). A and Aᵀ have the same rank but different column counts, so their nullities differ whenever the matrix is not square.

EM-U1-Q50NAThard

Find the rank of A = [[1, 2, 3, 4], [2, 4, 6, 8], [1, 0, 1, 0], [0, 1, 1, 2]]. (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Answer: 2

Detailed solution

Concept. The rank is the number of non-zero rows in any row echelon form; equivalently the size of a largest set of independent rows.

Step by step.

  1. R₂ = 2R₁, so row 2 contributes nothing: R₂ ← R₂ − 2R₁ clears it.
  2. R₃ ← R₃ − R₁ gives (0, −2, −2, −4).
  3. Row 4 is (0, 1, 1, 2), and (0, −2, −2, −4) = −2·(0, 1, 1, 2), so rows 3 and 4 are dependent.
  4. The non-zero rows reduce to (1, 2, 3, 4) and (0, 1, 1, 2), which are independent, so rank(A) = 2.
  5. Check with rank–nullity: nullity(A) = 4 − 2 = 2.

Answer: 2

Common misconception. Reporting 3 because the first three rows 'look' different, or 4 because there are four rows. Always eliminate: a repeated or proportional row is invisible at a glance once entries are larger.

EM-U1-Q58MCQeasy

A is a 5 × 5 real matrix with rank(A) = 2. The dimension of the null space of Aᵀ is

  1. 0
  2. 2
  3. 5
  4. 3
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Row rank equals column rank, so rank(Aᵀ) = rank(A), and the rank-nullity theorem applied to the 5 × 5 matrix Aᵀ gives rank + nullity = 5.

Step by step.

  1. rank(Aᵀ) = rank(A) = 2.
  2. The null space of Aᵀ lives in ℝ⁵, so rank(Aᵀ) + nullity(Aᵀ) = 5.
  3. Hence nullity(Aᵀ) = 5 − 2 = 3.

Answer: Option (D) — 3.

Common misconception. Reporting 2 by confusing the null space of A with that of Aᵀ. They generally have different dimensions, because they live in different spaces.

Takeaway. Rank is transpose-invariant, but the two null spaces sit in different spaces and need their own rank-nullity count.

EM-U1-Q59MCQmedium

A and B are 4 × 4 real matrices with rank(A) = 2 and rank(B) = 3. Which one of the following is TRUE about rank(AB)?

  1. rank(AB) = 2
  2. rank(AB) ≤ 2
  3. rank(AB) = 3
  4. rank(AB) ≥ 3
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Every column of AB is A times a column of B, so the column space of AB sits inside the column space of A.

Step by step.

  1. Let B = [b₁ … b₄]. Then AB = [Ab₁ … Ab₄].
  2. Each Abⱼ is a linear combination of the columns of A.
  3. So col(AB) ⊆ col(A), and rank(AB) ≤ rank(A) = 2.
  4. The bound is tight: with B = I the product is A itself. It can also fall to 0 when the columns of B lie in the null space of A.

Answer: Option (B) — rank(AB) ≤ 2.

Common misconception. Writing rank(AB) = min(rank A, rank B) = 2 as an identity. That is only an upper bound; the product can have strictly smaller rank.

Takeaway. Multiplication cannot increase rank, and the cap is the rank of the left factor for columns and of the right factor for rows.

EM-U1-Q60MCQmedium

A is a 5 × 3 real matrix with rank(A) = 3. Which one of the following is TRUE?

  1. Both AᵀA and AAᵀ are invertible
  2. AAᵀ is invertible but AᵀA is not
  3. AᵀA is invertible but AAᵀ is not
  4. Neither AᵀA nor AAᵀ is invertible
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. For every real matrix, rank(AᵀA) = rank(AAᵀ) = rank(A). A square matrix is invertible exactly when its rank equals its order.

Step by step.

  1. AᵀA is 3 × 3 and rank(AᵀA) = rank(A) = 3, so it is invertible.
  2. AAᵀ is 5 × 5 and rank(AAᵀ) = rank(A) = 3 < 5, so it is singular.
  3. The mismatch is just the order: A has only three independent columns to spread over five rows.

Answer: Option (C) — AᵀA invertible, AAᵀ not.

Common misconception. Assuming AᵀA and AAᵀ behave alike because both have the same rank. Rank equality does not give invertibility when the orders differ.

Takeaway. Tall matrix: AᵀA (small, full rank) is invertible, AAᵀ (large) is not. Wide matrix: the reverse.

EM-U1-Q61MCQhard

A and B are 4 × 4 real matrices, each of rank 3. The smallest possible value of rank(AB) is

  1. 0
  2. 1
  3. 2
  4. 3
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Sylvester’s rank inequality: for n × n matrices, rank(AB) ≥ rank(A) + rank(B) − n.

Step by step.

  1. Apply the inequality with n = 4: rank(AB) ≥ 3 + 3 − 4 = 2.
  2. So 2 is a lower bound; the question is whether it is attained.
  3. Attainment: take A = diag(1, 1, 1, 0) and let B have columns e₁, e₂, e₄, e₄. Then rank(B) = 3, and AB keeps rows 1 and 2 and zeroes rows 3 and 4, so rank(AB) = 2.
  4. Hence the minimum is exactly 2.

Answer: Option (C) — 2.

Common misconception. Answering 0 because each factor has a one-dimensional null space. Two rank-3 maps on ℝ⁴ cannot cancel completely: ker(A) and col(B) are both 3-dimensional in a 4-dimensional space, so they must overlap in at least 2 dimensions.

Takeaway. Rank deficiency adds: def(AB) ≤ def(A) + def(B), equivalently Sylvester’s inequality. Deficiencies 1 + 1 can overlap into a deficiency of at most 2.

EM-U1-Q62MSQhard

Select every statement about the rank of real matrices that is TRUE (all products and sums below are defined).

  1. rank(A + B) ≤ rank(A) + rank(B)
  2. rank(AᵀA) = rank(A)
  3. rank(AB) = min(rank(A), rank(B))
  4. rank(A) + nullity(A) equals the number of columns of A
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Rank counts independent columns. Sums cannot create more independence than the two parts carry; the rank-nullity theorem fixes the bookkeeping; and the product rule is only an inequality.

Step by step.

  1. (A) col(A + B) ⊆ col(A) + col(B), so the dimension of the left side is at most the sum of the dimensions of the right side.
  2. (B) Ax = 0 ⇔ AᵀAx = 0: one direction is trivial, and the other follows from ‖Ax‖² = xᵀAᵀAx = 0. So the null spaces agree and the ranks agree.
  3. (C) fails: A = [[1, 0], [0, 0]] and B = [[0, 0], [0, 1]] each have rank 1, but AB = 0 has rank 0.
  4. (D) is the rank-nullity theorem for an m × n matrix: rank + nullity = n.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Treating rank(AB) = min(rank A, rank B) as an equality. It is an upper bound only, and the product can be strictly smaller or zero.

Takeaway. Rank is subadditive under sums, blind to A → AᵀA, and never multiplicative in general.

EM-U1-Q63NATeasy

A is a 7 × 4 real matrix with rank(A) = 3. The nullity of Aᵀ is ______.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 4

Detailed solution

Concept. Aᵀ is 4 × 7 and has the same rank as A. Rank-nullity applies to its seven columns.

Step by step.

  1. rank(Aᵀ) = rank(A) = 3.
  2. Aᵀ maps ℝ⁷ to ℝ⁴, so rank(Aᵀ) + nullity(Aᵀ) = 7.
  3. nullity(Aᵀ) = 7 − 3 = 4.

Answer: 4.

Common misconception. Using the column count 4 of A and answering 4 − 3 = 1. For Aᵀ the number of columns is 7.

Takeaway. Rank-nullity always counts the columns of the matrix you are applying it to — here Aᵀ, so 7.

EM-U1-Q64MSQmedium

A is a 5 × 5 real matrix with A² = A and rank(A) = 3. Select every statement that is TRUE.

  1. rank(I − A) = 2
  2. trace(A) = 3
  3. nullity(I − A) = 2
  4. (I − A)² = I − A
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. An idempotent matrix is a projection: every vector splits as x = Ax + (I − A)x, with Ax in the range and (I − A)x in the null space. Its eigenvalues are only 0 and 1.

Step by step.

  1. (D) (I − A)² = I − 2A + A² = I − 2A + A = I − A, so I − A is idempotent too.
  2. (A) For idempotent A, rank(A) + rank(I − A) = 5: the range of A and the null space of A (which is the range of I − A) meet only at 0 and span ℝ⁵. So rank(I − A) = 5 − 3 = 2.
  3. (B) Idempotent matrices are diagonalisable with eigenvalues in {0, 1}; the trace is the sum of the eigenvalues and also the number of 1s, which equals the rank. So trace(A) = 3.
  4. (C) is false: nullity(I − A) = 5 − rank(I − A) = 5 − 2 = 3, not 2.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Mixing up rank(I − A) with nullity(I − A). The nullity of I − A is the dimension of its kernel, which is the range of A, namely 3.

Takeaway. For an idempotent of order n: rank(A) = trace(A) and rank(A) + rank(I − A) = n.

Systems of linear equations 18
EM-U1-Q10MCQeasy

Let A be a 3 × 3 real matrix with det(A) = 0, and let b ≠ 0 lie in the column space of A. The system Ax = b has

  1. exactly one solution
  2. infinitely many solutions
  3. no solution
  4. exactly three solutions
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Consistency is decided by rank(A) = rank([A | b]); the number of solutions is decided by the nullity.

Step by step.

  1. b is in the column space, so the system is consistent and at least one solution exists.
  2. det(A) = 0 means rank(A) ≤ 2, so nullity(A) = 3 − rank(A) ≥ 1.
  3. A non-zero null vector v lets us add tv to any solution for every t ∈ ℝ, producing infinitely many distinct solutions.

Answer: Option (B).

Common misconception. Equating det(A) = 0 with inconsistency. A singular system is inconsistent only when the right-hand side lies outside the column space; when it lies inside, there are infinitely many solutions.

EM-U1-Q11MCQmedium

For the system x + y = 2, 2x + 2y = k to have infinitely many solutions, k must be

  1. 0
  2. 2
  3. 4
  4. any real number
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Two equations in two unknowns represent the same line exactly when one is a scalar multiple of the other, including the constant terms.

Step by step.

  1. The second equation is twice the left side of the first: 2(x + y) = 2x + 2y.
  2. For the two lines to coincide we need k = 2 × 2 = 4.
  3. If k = 4, the second equation carries no new information and y is free: x = 2 − y, infinitely many solutions.
  4. If k ≠ 4, the system is inconsistent (parallel distinct lines).

Answer: Option (C) — 4.

Common misconception. Matching only the coefficient rows and forgetting the augmented column. Consistency requires rank(A) = rank([A | b]), so the constant terms must scale together with the coefficients.

EM-U1-Q12MCQmedium

A homogeneous system of 3 linear equations in 5 unknowns over ℝ

  1. has only the trivial solution
  2. always has a non-trivial solution
  3. has exactly two solutions
  4. may have no solution at all
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. A homogeneous system is always consistent (it has x = 0), and it has a non-trivial solution exactly when the coefficient matrix has a non-trivial null space.

Step by step.

  1. The coefficient matrix is 3 × 5, so rank(A) ≤ 3.
  2. nullity(A) = 5 − rank(A) ≥ 5 − 3 = 2 > 0.
  3. A positive nullity means non-zero solutions exist, in fact a whole 2-dimensional (or larger) family.

Answer: Option (B).

Common misconception. Confusing this with the case of more equations than unknowns. With more unknowns than equations the non-trivial solution is guaranteed; with 5 equations in 3 unknowns it is not.

EM-U1-Q13MCQhard

The system x + 2y + 3z = 1, 2x + 4y + 6z = 2, 3x + 6y + 9z = 4 is

  1. consistent with a unique solution
  2. consistent with infinitely many solutions
  3. inconsistent
  4. consistent with exactly two solutions
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Compare rank(A) with rank([A | b]): equality means consistent, inequality means inconsistent.

Step by step.

  1. Row 2 is 2 × Row 1, and Row 3 is 3 × Row 1 in the coefficient part. So rank(A) = 1.
  2. Row 3 of the augmented matrix is [3, 6, 9 | 4]. Subtracting 3 × Row 1 [1, 2, 3 | 1] leaves [0, 0, 0 | 1].
  3. That row reads 0 = 1, impossible. Hence rank([A | b]) = 2 > rank(A) = 1, and the system is inconsistent.

Answer: Option (C).

Common misconception. Seeing that the coefficient rows are proportional and concluding 'infinitely many solutions'. Proportional coefficients give rank(A) = 1, but the right-hand side must scale the same way; here 4 ≠ 3 × 1, which is exactly what makes it inconsistent.

EM-U1-Q18MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about linear systems are TRUE?

  1. A homogeneous system with 3 equations and 5 unknowns always has a non-trivial solution.
  2. A system with more equations than unknowns is always inconsistent.
  3. If A is 4 × 4 with det(A) = 0, then Ax = b either has no solution or infinitely many.
  4. For b ≠ 0, the solution set of Ax = b is a subspace of ℝⁿ
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Consistency of a homogeneous system, the rank test for general systems, and the affine (not linear) nature of a non-homogeneous solution set.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True: rank ≤ 3 < 5, so nullity ≥ 2 and non-trivial solutions exist.
  2. (B) False: an overdetermined system can be perfectly consistent, e.g. x = 1, 2x = 2, 3x = 3.
  3. (C) True: det(A) = 0 makes A singular, so either the system is inconsistent or the nullity is positive, giving infinitely many solutions. A unique solution is impossible.
  4. (D) False: the solution set of Ax = b with b ≠ 0 does not contain 0, so it cannot be a subspace. It is a translate of the null space.

Answer: Options (A) and (C).

Common misconception. Option (D) is a frequent error: the homogeneous solution set N(A) is a subspace, but a coset x₀ + N(A) is not, precisely because it misses the origin.

EM-U1-Q23NATmedium

The system x + y + z = 3, x + 2y + 3z = 4, x + 3y + kz = 5 has infinitely many solutions for exactly one integer value of k. Find that value. (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Answer: 5

Detailed solution

Concept. A square system has infinitely many solutions exactly when the coefficient matrix is singular and the system is consistent.

Step by step.

  1. R₂ ← R₂ − R₁: y + 2z = 1.
  2. R₃ ← R₃ − R₁: 2y + (k − 1)z = 2.
  3. Eliminate y using step 1: 2(1 − 2z) + (k − 1)z = 2, so (k − 5)z = 0.
  4. If k = 5 the last row vanishes and z is free: y = 1 − 2z, x = 2 + z, giving infinitely many solutions.
  5. If k ≠ 5, then z = 0, y = 1, x = 2: a unique solution.

Answer: 5

Common misconception. Solving for the determinant and reporting every root as 'infinitely many'. A zero determinant gives singularity, but one must also verify the system stays consistent — here it does, at k = 5.

EM-U1-Q34MCQeasy

A homogeneous system of 4 linear equations in 3 unknowns has a coefficient matrix of rank 3. Its solution set is

  1. empty
  2. exactly one solution, the trivial one x = 0
  3. an infinite set of solutions
  4. exactly three solutions
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. A homogeneous system Ax = 0 is always consistent, since x = 0 works. Its solution set is the null space, whose dimension is nullity(A) = n − rank(A).

Step by step.

  1. n = 3 (three unknowns) and rank(A) = 3, so nullity(A) = 0.
  2. A null space of dimension 0 is {0}, so the only solution is x = 0.
  3. Note that four equations are irrelevant to the count: rank, not row count, decides.

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Believing that more equations than unknowns forces either inconsistency or infinitely many solutions. A homogeneous system is never inconsistent, and here the columns are independent, so the trivial solution is the only one.

EM-U1-Q35MCQmedium

Let A be a 3 × 5 real matrix of rank 3 and let Ax = b be consistent. The solution set of Ax = b is

  1. a single point
  2. a line
  3. a plane
  4. the empty set
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. If Ax = b is consistent with particular solution x₀, the full solution set is x₀ + ker(A) — an affine subspace whose dimension equals the nullity.

Step by step.

  1. nullity(A) = 5 − rank(A) = 5 − 3 = 2.
  2. So the solution set is x₀ plus a 2-dimensional subspace of ℝ⁵.
  3. A 2-dimensional affine subspace is a plane (a translate of a plane through the origin).

Answer: Option (C)

Common misconception. Answering 'a single point' because there are three equations, or 'a line' by confusing the nullity with the number of free variables minus one. Dimension of the solution set = number of unknowns − rank.

EM-U1-Q36MCQeasy

Using Cramer's rule on the system 2x + y = 5, x + 3y = 10, the value of x is

  1. 1
  2. 2
  3. 3
  4. 5
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Cramer's rule: when det(A) ≠ 0, xᵢ = det(Aᵢ)/det(A), where Aᵢ is A with column i replaced by b.

Step by step.

  1. A = [[2, 1], [1, 3]], so det(A) = 6 − 1 = 5.
  2. A₁ = [[5, 1], [10, 3]], so det(A₁) = 15 − 10 = 5.
  3. x = 5/5 = 1.
  4. Check: y = det([[2,5],[1,10]])/5 = (20 − 5)/5 = 3, and 2(1) + 3 = 5, 1 + 3(3) = 10 both hold.

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Replacing the wrong column — putting b into the y column gives det = 20 − 1 = 19 and a wrong x. The replacement column index must match the unknown being solved for.

EM-U1-Q37MCQmedium

Consider the system x + y + z = 1, x + 2y + 3z = 4, x + 4y + az = b, where a, b ∈ ℝ. The system has a unique solution for

  1. a ≠ 7, whatever b is
  2. a ≠ 0, whatever b is
  3. every real a and b
  4. no real a
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. For a square system, uniqueness is decided by the coefficient matrix alone: a unique solution exists exactly when det(A) ≠ 0, and then it exists for every right-hand side b.

Step by step.

  1. Expand the determinant of A = [[1,1,1],[1,2,3],[1,4,a]]:
    det(A) = 1(2a − 12) − 1(a − 3) + 1(4 − 2) = a − 7.
  2. So det(A) = 0 exactly when a = 7.
  3. For every a ≠ 7 the matrix is invertible and x = A⁻¹b is the unique solution, for any b.
  4. At a = 7 the rows (0,1,2) and (0,3,6) are proportional, so the system is either inconsistent or has infinitely many solutions, depending on b.

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Assuming that uniqueness depends on b as well. Row operations on [A | b] cannot change det(A), so the right-hand side never rescues a singular coefficient matrix into uniqueness.

EM-U1-Q43MSQmedium

Which of the following systems have infinitely many solutions?

  1. x + y = 1, 2x + 2y = 2
  2. x + y = 1, x − y = 0
  3. x + y + z = 2, x − y + z = 0, 2x + 2z = 2
  4. x + y = 1, x + y = 2
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. A consistent system has infinitely many solutions exactly when its coefficient matrix has nullity at least 1, i.e. when its rank is less than the number of unknowns.

Step by step.

  1. (A) The second equation is twice the first, so rank 1 with 2 unknowns and the system is consistent — infinitely many (a whole line) — true.
  2. (B) det([[1,1],[1,−1]]) = −2 ≠ 0, so the solution (1/2, 1/2) is unique — false.
  3. (C) The third equation is the sum of the first two, so the rank is 2 while there are 3 unknowns, and the system is consistent — infinitely many (a line) — true.
  4. (D) The two left sides are identical but the right sides differ, so the system is inconsistent — false.

Answer: Options (A) and (C)

Common misconception. Counting dependent equations as automatically giving infinitely many solutions. Dependence plus consistency gives infinitely many; dependence with contradictory right-hand sides (D) gives none.

EM-U1-Q48NAThard

The system x + 2y + z = 8, 2x − y + 3z = 9, x + y − z = 0 has a unique solution. For that solution, find x + 2y + 3z. (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Answer: 14

Detailed solution

Concept. Eliminate one variable at a time; then substitute back. Check uniqueness first by the determinant of the coefficient matrix.

Step by step.

  1. det([[1,2,1],[2,−1,3],[1,1,−1]]) = 1(1 − 3) − 2(−2 − 3) + 1(2 + 1) = −2 + 10 + 3 = 11 ≠ 0, so the solution is unique.
  2. From the third equation z = x + y. Substituting into the first: x + 2y + (x + y) = 8, i.e. 2x + 3y = 8.
  3. Into the second: 2x − y + 3(x + y) = 9, i.e. 5x + 2y = 9.
  4. Solving the 2 × 2 system: y = (8 − 2x)/3 and 5x + 2(8 − 2x)/3 = 9 give 15x + 16 − 4x = 27, so 11x = 11, x = 1.
  5. Then 2 + 3y = 8 gives y = 2, and z = 1 + 2 = 3.
  6. x + 2y + 3z = 1 + 4 + 9 = 14.

Answer: 14

Common misconception. Answering x + y + z = 6 — the sum of the solution — instead of the weighted expression asked for, or stopping after finding z. Substitute the triple back into all three original equations before reporting.

EM-U1-Q65MCQeasy

A system of 3 linear equations in 4 unknowns is consistent. Then the system has

  1. a unique solution
  2. no solution
  3. exactly two solutions
  4. infinitely many solutions
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. A consistent system with n unknowns has a unique solution exactly when the coefficient matrix has rank n; otherwise the solution set is infinite.

Step by step.

  1. Let the coefficient matrix be A, of size 3 × 4.
  2. rank(A) ≤ 3 < 4, so nullity(A) = 4 − rank(A) ≥ 1.
  3. A consistent system has solutions, and a non-zero null vector can be added to any solution to get another. So there are infinitely many.
  4. A solution set of a linear system is either empty, a single point, or infinite — never exactly two.

Answer: Option (D) — infinitely many solutions.

Common misconception. Answering "a unique solution" because there are fewer equations than unknowns, or "no solution" because the system looks underdetermined. Consistency is given here; underdetermination then forces infinitely many.

Takeaway. Fewer equations than unknowns plus consistency means free variables, hence infinitely many solutions.

EM-U1-Q66MCQeasy

A is a 3 × 3 real matrix with rank(A) = 2, and the augmented matrix [A | b] has rank 3. Then Ax = b has

  1. no solution
  2. a unique solution
  3. infinitely many solutions
  4. exactly two solutions
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Rouché–Capelli: the system is consistent exactly when rank(A) = rank([A | b]).

Step by step.

  1. rank(A) = 2 < 3 = rank([A | b]).
  2. The extra rank of the augmented matrix comes from the last column, so b is not a linear combination of the columns of A.
  3. Hence b ∉ col(A) and the system is inconsistent.

Answer: Option (A) — no solution.

Common misconception. Using rank(A) = 2 < 3 to conclude "infinitely many solutions". That conclusion needs consistency first; a rank gap between A and [A | b] means no solution at all.

Takeaway. Compare the two ranks before counting free variables: equal ranks give solutions, unequal ranks give none.

EM-U1-Q67MCQmedium

Consider the system x + y + 2z = 1, 2x + 3y + z = 3, 3x + 4y + 3z = 4. Which one of the following is TRUE?

  1. It has a unique solution.
  2. It has no solution.
  3. It has infinitely many solutions.
  4. It has exactly three solutions.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Add the first two equations and compare with the third: a dependent row means a free variable, and the solution set is a line.

Step by step.

  1. Add equation 1 and equation 2: (1 + 2)x + (1 + 3)y + (2 + 1)z = 1 + 3, that is 3x + 4y + 3z = 4.
  2. That is exactly equation 3, so the third row adds no information: rank(A) = rank([A | b]) = 2.
  3. The coefficient matrix is 3 × 3 with rank 2, so nullity = 1 and the solution set is a line.
  4. Consistency is guaranteed because equation 3 is a combination of the other two, so there are infinitely many solutions.

Answer: Option (C) — infinitely many solutions.

Common misconception. Stopping at "the third row is dependent, so no solution". Dependency gives infinitely many solutions; inconsistency is what gives none, and it needs a non-zero entry in the augmented column of a zero row.

Takeaway. A dependent row is consistent unless the augmented column breaks the dependency.

EM-U1-Q68MCQmedium

For how many real values of k does the system kx + y = 1, x + ky = 1 have infinitely many solutions?

  1. 0
  2. 1
  3. 2
  4. infinitely many
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Infinitely many solutions need both a singular coefficient matrix and consistency; each condition must be checked, in that order.

Step by step.

  1. The coefficient matrix [[k, 1], [1, k]] is singular only when k² − 1 = 0, that is k = 1 or k = −1.
  2. For k = 1 both equations read x + y = 1: one independent equation in two unknowns, so infinitely many solutions.
  3. For k = −1 the system is −x + y = 1 and x − y = 1. Adding them gives 0 = 2, so it is inconsistent.
  4. Every other k makes the matrix invertible and the solution unique. Hence exactly one value.

Answer: Option (B) — exactly one value, k = 1.

Common misconception. Counting both roots of k² = 1 and answering 2, without testing the degenerate case k = −1 for consistency.

Takeaway. Singularity is necessary, not sufficient: a singular system is either inconsistent or infinite.

EM-U1-Q69MSQmedium

Select every statement about real linear systems that is TRUE.

  1. If A is n × n with det(A) = 0, then Ax = b has either no solution or infinitely many solutions.
  2. A system of 4 equations in 3 unknowns can never have a unique solution.
  3. A consistent system with more unknowns than equations has infinitely many solutions.
  4. If A is n × n and Ax = 0 has only the trivial solution, then Ax = b has a unique solution for every b.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Three facts drive everything: consistency is rank(A) = rank([A | b]); the number of solutions is 0, 1 or infinite; and an invertible matrix settles the question for every right-hand side.

Step by step.

  1. (A) det(A) = 0 makes A singular, so either b ∉ col(A) (no solution) or b ∈ col(A) with a non-trivial null space (infinitely many).
  2. (B) is false: x = 1, y = 2, z = 3, x + y + z = 6 is a system of four equations in three unknowns with the unique solution (1, 2, 3). Extra equations only overdetermine the system; they do not stop it from being consistent.
  3. (C) With m equations and n > m unknowns, rank(A) ≤ m < n, so nullity ≥ 1 and any solution can be shifted by a null vector.
  4. (D) If Ax = 0 has only the trivial solution then nullity = 0, so rank(A) = n and A is invertible; then x = A⁻¹b is the unique solution for every b.

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D).

Common misconception. Believing that more equations than unknowns prevents a unique solution. Overdetermined systems can be perfectly consistent, and then the solution is unique.

Takeaway. Count rank, not equations; and test consistency before you count solutions.

EM-U1-Q70NATmedium

The system x + y + z = 6, 2x − y + z = 3, x + 2y − 3z = −4 has a unique solution. The value of x is ______.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 1

Detailed solution

Concept. Eliminate one variable at a time, keeping the number of equations in step with the number of unknowns that remain.

Step by step.

  1. From the first equation, z = 6 − x − y.
  2. Substitute into the second: 2x − y + 6 − x − y = 3, so x − 2y = −3.
  3. Substitute into the third: x + 2y − 3(6 − x − y) = −4, so 4x + 5y = 14.
  4. With x = 2y − 3: 4(2y − 3) + 5y = 14, 13y = 26, y = 2.
  5. Then x = 2(2) − 3 = 1 and z = 6 − 1 − 2 = 3.
  6. Check the third equation: 1 + 4 − 9 = −4.

Answer: 1 (with y = 2, z = 3).

Common misconception. Substituting back into the equation just used, which returns an identity instead of fixing a second variable. Always substitute into a different equation.

Takeaway. Back-substitute into a different equation each time, then check the answer in the equation you never used.

Gauss elimination 14
EM-U1-Q14MCQeasy

Applying Gauss–Jordan elimination to a system whose coefficient matrix is invertible transforms that coefficient matrix into

  1. an upper-triangular matrix
  2. the identity matrix
  3. a diagonal matrix that is not the identity
  4. its own transpose
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Gauss–Jordan elimination continues forward elimination with backward elimination, reducing the augmented matrix to reduced row-echelon form.

Step by step.

  1. Forward elimination alone produces an upper-triangular U — that is plain Gaussian elimination.
  2. Gauss–Jordan then eliminates above each pivot and scales every pivot to 1.
  3. For an invertible matrix every column has a pivot, so the coefficient block becomes I.

Answer: Option (B).

Common misconception. Confusing the two methods. Gaussian elimination stops at upper-triangular form; only Gauss–Jordan reaches the identity, after which the solution is read directly from the transformed right-hand side.

EM-U1-Q15MCQhard

Gaussian elimination is applied to A = [[1, 2, 3], [2, 4, 7], [3, 6, 10]]. The rank of A is

  1. 1
  2. 2
  3. 3
  4. 0
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Row operations preserve the row space, so the number of non-zero rows in the echelon form is the rank.

Step by step.

  1. R₂ ← R₂ − 2R₁: [2, 4, 7] − [2, 4, 6] = [0, 0, 1].
  2. R₃ ← R₃ − 3R₁: [3, 6, 10] − [3, 6, 9] = [0, 0, 1].
  3. R₃ ← R₃ − R₂: [0, 0, 0].
  4. The echelon form is [[1, 2, 3], [0, 0, 1], [0, 0, 0]], with two non-zero rows, so rank(A) = 2.

Answer: Option (B) — 2.

Common misconception. Reading rank off the first column only, or stopping after one elimination step. Rank is the count of pivots across all columns, obtained only after the elimination is complete.

EM-U1-Q19MSQhard

Which of the following statements about Gaussian elimination are TRUE?

  1. Partial pivoting can reduce the growth of round-off error.
  2. For a dense n × n matrix, forward elimination costs about n³/3 multiplications.
  3. If a zero pivot appears while a non-zero entry remains below it in that column, a row interchange can continue the elimination.
  4. An LU decomposition without row interchanges exists for every non-singular matrix.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Pivoting strategy, operation count, and the exact condition for a pivot-free LU factorisation.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True: choosing the largest-magnitude candidate as pivot keeps multipliers |l_ik| ≤ 1, bounding error growth.
  2. (B) True: the total is Σ_{k=1}^{n−1} (n−k)(n−k+1) ≈ n³/3 multiplications.
  3. (C) True: a zero pivot with a non-zero entry below is repairable by interchanging rows — this is exactly why partial pivoting is used.
  4. (D) False: LU without interchanges requires every leading principal minor to be non-zero. For example [[0, 1], [1, 0]] is non-singular but has no such factorisation.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Believing non-singularity alone guarantees LU. It guarantees a factorisation only with row interchanges (PA = LU); without pivoting a zero pivot can appear even for an invertible matrix.

EM-U1-Q24NATmedium

Gaussian elimination without row interchange is applied to A = [[2, 1, 1], [4, 3, 3], [8, 7, 9]]. Find U₃₃, the (3, 3) entry of the resulting upper-triangular matrix U. (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Answer: 2

Detailed solution

Concept. Forward elimination annihilates entries below each pivot using multipliers l_ik = a_ik / a_kk.

Step by step.

  1. Pivot a₁₁ = 2. R₂ ← R₂ − 2R₁: [4,3,3] − [4,2,2] = [0,1,1].
  2. R₃ ← R₃ − 4R₁: [8,7,9] − [8,4,4] = [0,3,5].
  3. Pivot a₂₂ = 1. R₃ ← R₃ − 3R₂: [0,3,5] − [0,3,3] = [0,0,2].
  4. So U = [[2,1,1],[0,1,1],[0,0,2]] and U₃₃ = 2.

Answer: 2

Common misconception. Subtracting multiples of the original first row again in the third step instead of using the updated second row. Each elimination must use the current pivot row, not the original R₁.

EM-U1-Q38MCQeasy

Which of the following is NOT an elementary row operation?

  1. Interchanging two rows
  2. Multiplying a row by a non-zero scalar
  3. Adding a scalar multiple of one row to another row
  4. Multiplying a row by 0
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. The three elementary row operations are row interchange, row scaling by a non-zero constant, and row replacement. Each must be reversible, because elimination has to preserve the solution set.

Step by step.

  1. Interchange is its own inverse.
  2. Scaling by k ≠ 0 is undone by scaling by 1/k.
  3. Row replacement is undone by subtracting the same multiple.
  4. Multiplying a row by 0 destroys the information in that row and cannot be undone — it is not an elementary operation.

Answer: Option (D)

Common misconception. Thinking that any 'row scaling' is allowed. The non-zero condition is essential: scaling by zero changes the solution set and reduces the rank.

EM-U1-Q39MCQmedium

Gauss–Jordan elimination (full reduction to reduced row echelon form) applied to a dense n × n system performs approximately how many arithmetic operations?

  1. n³/3
  2. n³/2
  3. n²
  4. n³
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Gaussian elimination costs about n³/3 operations for forward elimination; Gauss–Jordan additionally eliminates above each pivot, roughly doubling that work.

Step by step.

  1. Forward elimination removes entries below the pivots: about n³/3 operations.
  2. Back substitution in ordinary Gaussian elimination costs only n²/2.
  3. Gauss–Jordan also clears the entries above each pivot, adding about n³/6.
  4. Total n³/3 + n³/6 = n³/2.

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Quoting n³/3 for Gauss–Jordan as well. That figure belongs to plain Gaussian elimination followed by back substitution; the extra sweep above the diagonal is what pushes it to n³/2.

EM-U1-Q40MCQeasy

Gaussian elimination without pivoting is applied to a dense tridiagonal n × n system. Asymptotically, the number of arithmetic operations is

  1. Θ(n)
  2. Θ(n log n)
  3. Θ(n²)
  4. Θ(n³)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. The cost of elimination is governed by the number of non-zeros it has to touch. A tridiagonal matrix has only 3n − 2 non-zero entries, and elimination on it creates no fill-in outside the band.

Step by step.

  1. At step k only row k + 1 needs updating, and only in columns k + 1 and k + 2.
  2. So each of the n − 1 steps costs a constant number of operations.
  3. Total Θ(n) — this is the Thomas algorithm.

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Quoting Θ(n³) out of habit. The n³/3 figure assumes a dense matrix; banded structure is exactly what makes large tridiagonal systems cheap to solve.

EM-U1-Q44MSQhard

Which statements about pivoting in Gaussian elimination are TRUE?

  1. Partial pivoting selects the entry of largest magnitude in the current column, at or below the pivot row
  2. Scaled partial pivoting compares |aᵢₖ|/sᵢ, where sᵢ is the largest magnitude of an entry in row i
  3. Complete pivoting searches the remaining submatrix for the largest magnitude entry, using both row and column interchanges
  4. Partial pivoting guarantees an exact answer in finite-precision arithmetic
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Pivoting chooses the elimination order to keep multipliers small, which limits the growth of rounding error. It improves accuracy; it does not remove floating-point error.

Step by step.

  1. Partial pivoting: interchange rows so that the pivot is the largest magnitude entry in its column at or below the current row — true.
  2. Scaled partial pivoting divides each candidate by the largest entry in its own row, so rows of different scale are compared fairly — true.
  3. Complete piviting searches the whole remaining submatrix and may interchange columns as well, which is stronger but costs more comparisons — true.
  4. No pivoting strategy can make floating-point arithmetic exact; the guarantee is bounded growth of round-off, not exactness — false.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Common misconception. Believing pivoting eliminates round-off error. It only keeps the multipliers at most 1 in magnitude, which bounds error growth; the arithmetic is still inexact.

EM-U1-Q49NAThard

A 5 × 5 matrix with no zero pivots is reduced to row echelon form without any row interchange. Counting one operation per row update of the form Rᵢ ← Rᵢ − mRₖ, how many such updates are performed in total? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Answer: 10

Detailed solution

Concept. At elimination step k every row strictly below the pivot row is updated once. The count is therefore the number of pairs (k, i) with i > k.

Step by step.

  1. Step 1 updates rows 2–5: 4 updates.
  2. Step 2 updates rows 3–5: 3 updates.
  3. Step 3 updates rows 4–5: 2 updates.
  4. Step 4 updates row 5: 1 update.
  5. Total 4 + 3 + 2 + 1 = 10, i.e. n(n − 1)/2 = 5·4/2.

Answer: 10

Common misconception. Counting the inner loop over columns instead of rows, which gives the n³/3 ≈ 42 figure for arithmetic operations. Row updates and arithmetic operations are different counts; this question asks for the former.

EM-U1-Q71MCQeasy

Applying elementary row operations to a matrix does NOT change its

  1. determinant
  2. trace
  3. eigenvalues
  4. rank
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Each elementary row operation is left multiplication by an invertible matrix, and multiplying by an invertible matrix preserves rank.

Step by step.

  1. Let E be an elementary matrix. The operation replaces A by EA, with E invertible.
  2. Multiplication by an invertible matrix does not change the null space dimension, so rank(EA) = rank(A).
  3. The other three do change: swapping rows flips the sign of the determinant, adding a multiple of a row changes the trace, and EA is similar to A only in special cases, so the eigenvalues move in general.

Answer: Option (D) — the rank.

Common misconception. Confusing "row operations preserve the solution set of the system" with "row operations preserve everything". They preserve rank and the solution set, not the determinant, trace or eigenvalues.

Takeaway. Row operations preserve exactly what the row space and null space determine — rank and the solution set.

EM-U1-Q72MCQmedium

Gaussian elimination without row interchange is applied to a 3 × 3 matrix. Which one of the following is TRUE?

  1. A zero pivot can appear even when the matrix is non-singular, and a row interchange then repairs it.
  2. A zero pivot proves that the matrix is singular.
  3. Elimination without interchange can never meet a zero pivot.
  4. A zero pivot means the system has infinitely many solutions.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. The pivot at step k is the (k, k) entry after the previous eliminations. It can be zero purely because of the ordering of the equations.

Step by step.

  1. Take A = [[0, 1], [1, 0]]. It has det(A) = −1 ≠ 0, so it is non-singular.
  2. The first pivot is a₁₁ = 0, so elimination without interchange cannot proceed.
  3. Interchanging the two rows gives [[1, 0], [0, 1]] and the elimination runs at once.
  4. So a zero pivot signals "interchange needed", not "singular". Partial pivoting makes this automatic by choosing the largest available entry in the column.

Answer: Option (A) — a zero pivot can occur in a non-singular matrix and is repaired by an interchange.

Common misconception. Reading a zero pivot as a singularity test. The singularity test is a zero pivot with no non-zero entry below it in that column.

Takeaway. A zero pivot means the current ordering is unlucky; only a zero column below the pivot means the matrix is singular.

EM-U1-Q73MCQeasy

For a given matrix, which one of the following is unique?

  1. its row echelon form
  2. its reduced row echelon form
  3. both
  4. neither
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Every matrix has exactly one reduced row echelon form (RREF); its row echelon forms are not unique, because different sequences of row operations stop at different echelon shapes.

Step by step.

  1. Row echelon form fixes only the staircase: which columns hold pivots, and that entries below a pivot are zero. The pivot values themselves depend on the operations used.
  2. RREF additionally forces every pivot to be 1 and clears the entries above each pivot, which pins the matrix down completely.
  3. Example: A = [[1, 2], [2, 4]] has echelon forms [[1, 2], [0, 0]] and [[2, 4], [0, 0]], but only one RREF, [[1, 2], [0, 0]].

Answer: Option (B) — the reduced row echelon form.

Common misconception. Assuming row echelon form is canonical because "the" echelon form is usually computed by one standard procedure. Different procedures give different echelon forms of the same matrix.

Takeaway. RREF is the canonical form; it is the one you can use to read off rank, null space and consistency.

EM-U1-Q74MSQhard

Select every statement about elementary row operations and Gaussian elimination that is TRUE.

  1. Every elementary row operation is invertible, so applying one to the augmented matrix leaves the solution set unchanged.
  2. Interchanging two rows multiplies the determinant by −1.
  3. If A is reduced to a row echelon form U using only row replacements, then det(U) = det(A).
  4. Elementary column operations leave the solution set of Ax = b unchanged.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. The three row operations are exactly the invertible ones: swap, scale by a non-zero constant, and add a multiple of one row to another. Each has a known effect on the determinant, and none of them touches the solution set.

Step by step.

  1. (A) A swap is undone by swapping back, a scaling by c ≠ 0 by scaling by 1/c, and a replacement by subtracting the same multiple. Invertible left multiplication preserves the solution set.
  2. (B) A swap exchanges two rows of the determinant, and each exchange flips the sign.
  3. (C) A row replacement is adding a multiple of one row to another, which leaves the determinant untouched, so det(U) = det(A).
  4. (D) fails: a column operation changes the matrix that multiplies x, which is the same as renaming or rescaling the unknowns. The system 2x + y = 1, y = 0 has the solution (0.5, 0), while its column-swapped form x + 2y = 1, x = 0 has the solution (0, 0.5) — a different point.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Treating column operations as harmless because they also preserve rank. Rank yes, solution set no: the unknowns are attached to columns.

Takeaway. Row operations act on equations, column operations act on unknowns. Only the first kind is safe inside an elimination.

EM-U1-Q75NAThard

A matrix A is carried to an upper-triangular matrix U by Gaussian elimination in which exactly one row interchange was performed and, separately, two rows were multiplied by 2 and by 3. If det(U) = 12, then det(A) is ______.

Show answer & solution

Answer: −2

Detailed solution

Concept. Each operation has a fixed effect on the determinant: an interchange multiplies it by −1, scaling a row by c multiplies it by c, and a row replacement leaves it unchanged.

Step by step.

  1. Collect the factors introduced on the way from A to U: (−1) for the interchange, 2 and 3 for the two scalings.
  2. Their product is (−1) × 2 × 3 = −6, so det(U) = −6 · det(A).
  3. Therefore det(A) = det(U) / (−6) = 12 / (−6) = −2.

Answer: −2.

Common misconception. Forgetting the sign of the interchange and answering 2, or applying the scaling factors in the wrong direction and answering −72.

Takeaway. Track the determinant multiplier of every operation as you perform it: −1 per swap, c per scaling, 1 per replacement.

Unit 2 — Linear Algebra: Eigenvalues & Vectors 75
Characteristic equations 20
EM-U2-Q01MCQeasy

Let A = [[2, 1], [1, 2]]. Which option gives the characteristic equation of A together with its eigenvalues?

  1. λ² − 4λ + 3 = 0, eigenvalues 1 and 3
  2. λ² − 4λ + 5 = 0, eigenvalues 2 ± i
  3. λ² − 2λ + 3 = 0, eigenvalues 1 and 2
  4. λ² + 4λ + 3 = 0, eigenvalues −1 and −3
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. The characteristic polynomial of a square matrix is p(λ) = det(A − λI), and its roots are the eigenvalues. For a 2 × 2 matrix this always collapses to λ² − tr(A)λ + det(A), which is the fastest route and the one worth memorising.

Step by step.

  1. Trace: tr(A) = 2 + 2 = 4. Determinant: det(A) = (2)(2) − (1)(1) = 3.
  2. So p(λ) = λ² − 4λ + 3.
  3. Factorise: λ² − 4λ + 3 = (λ − 1)(λ − 3), so the roots are 1 and 3.
  4. Cross-check by the direct expansion: det(A − λI) = (2 − λ)² − 1 = λ² − 4λ + 3. Same polynomial.
  5. Sanity checks that must hold: 1 + 3 = 4 = tr(A) and 1 × 3 = 3 = det(A).

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Writing the constant term as −det(A) instead of +det(A). The 2 × 2 form is λ² − tr(A)λ + det(A), with a plus before the determinant. Option (D) is the same slip compounded with a sign error on the trace.

EM-U2-Q02MCQeasy

A real 3 × 3 matrix A has eigenvalues 2, 2, 5. Which statement is certainly TRUE?

  1. A is diagonalizable.
  2. A is not diagonalizable.
  3. tr(A) = 9 and det(A) = 20
  4. tr(A) = 20 and det(A) = 9
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Two coefficients of the characteristic polynomial are always readable from the eigenvalues, counted with algebraic multiplicity: the coefficient of λⁿ⁻¹ gives tr(A) = Σλᵢ, and the constant term gives det(A) = Πλᵢ. Both are symmetric functions, so the order in which the eigenvalues are listed does not matter.

Step by step.

  1. tr(A) = 2 + 2 + 5 = 9. The repeated 2 is counted twice.
  2. det(A) = 2 × 2 × 5 = 20.
  3. Diagonalizability is not settled by the multiset alone. diag(2, 2, 5) is diagonalizable, while [[2, 1, 0], [0, 2, 0], [0, 0, 5]] has the same eigenvalues and is not, because λ = 2 has geometric multiplicity 1 against algebraic multiplicity 2.
  4. So (A) and (B) are each possible but neither is forced, and only (C) is guaranteed.

Answer: Option (C)

Common misconception. Two errors pull in opposite directions: reading a repeated eigenvalue as proof that the matrix cannot be diagonalized, and reading it as proof that it can. A repeated eigenvalue only makes diagonalizability a question to check, not a conclusion.

EM-U2-Q07MCQmedium

The characteristic polynomial of a 3 × 3 invertible matrix A is p(λ) = λ³ − 6λ² + 11λ − 6. What is the characteristic polynomial of A⁻¹?

  1. λ³ − (11/6)λ² + λ − 1/6
  2. λ³ − (11/6)λ² + λ + 1/6
  3. λ³ − 6λ² + 11λ − 6
  4. λ³ + 6λ² + 11λ + 6
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Inverting a matrix inverts every eigenvalue and leaves every eigenvector in place. So the spectrum of A⁻¹ is {1/λᵢ}, and its characteristic polynomial is rebuilt from the three symmetric functions of those reciprocals — sum, pairwise sum and product — not by transforming the old coefficients one at a time.

Step by step.

  1. Factor: λ³ − 6λ² + 11λ − 6 = (λ − 1)(λ − 2)(λ − 3), so the eigenvalues of A are 1, 2, 3.
  2. The constant term is −6, and for a 3 × 3 matrix the constant term is −det(A), so det(A) = 6 — consistent with 1 × 2 × 3 = 6.
  3. Eigenvalues of A⁻¹: 1, 1/2, 1/3.
  4. Sum: 1 + 1/2 + 1/3 = 11/6.
  5. Pairwise sum: 1·(1/2) + 1·(1/3) + (1/2)(1/3) = 1/2 + 1/3 + 1/6 = 1.
  6. Product: 1 × (1/2) × (1/3) = 1/6.
  7. Assemble: q(μ) = μ³ − (11/6)μ² + μ − 1/6.
  8. Check against the closed form: q(μ) = −μ³ p(1/μ)/det(A) reproduces exactly this polynomial.

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Answering (C) on the assumption that a matrix and its inverse share a characteristic polynomial. They share eigenvectors, not eigenvalues: 1 and 1/2 are different numbers.

EM-U2-Q09MCQmedium

Let A be a real square matrix. Which statement is FALSE?

  1. A and Aᵀ have the same characteristic polynomial.
  2. If λ is an eigenvalue of A, then λ² is an eigenvalue of A².
  3. If A is invertible and λ is an eigenvalue of A, then 1/λ is an eigenvalue of A⁻¹.
  4. If λ is an eigenvalue of A, then λ is an eigenvalue of A + Aᵀ.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Spectral statements split into two families. Polynomial and inverse rules act on each eigenvalue independently, so they hold always. Additive rules do not: A + B generally has nothing to do with the eigenvalues of A and B, because the two matrices usually have different eigenvectors.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true: det(Aᵀ − λI) = det((A − λI)ᵀ) = det(A − λI), so the polynomials coincide.
  2. (B) is true: from Av = λv we get A²v = λ²v.
  3. (C) is true: Av = λv with λ ≠ 0 gives A⁻¹v = (1/λ)v. Invertibility rules out λ = 0, so the division is legitimate.
  4. (D) is false. Take A = [[0, 1], [0, 0]]. Its only eigenvalue is 0. But A + Aᵀ = [[0, 1], [1, 0]] has eigenvalues 1 and −1, so 0 is not an eigenvalue of A + Aᵀ. The claimed implication fails.

Answer: Option (D)

Common misconception. Assuming eigenvalues are additive under matrix addition. They are multiplicative only in the special case AB = BA, and additive only for shifts of the form A + cI, where the eigenvector is unchanged.

EM-U2-Q19MSQmedium

The characteristic polynomial of a 3 × 3 real matrix A is p(λ) = λ³ − 6λ² + 11λ − 6. Which statements are TRUE?

  1. det(A) = 6
  2. tr(A) = 6
  3. 0 is an eigenvalue of A.
  4. A is invertible.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Read the coefficients of λⁿ + c₁λⁿ⁻¹ + … + cₙ as spectral data: c₁ = −tr(A) and cₙ = (−1)ⁿ det(A). The eigenvalues themselves are the roots, and a zero root is equivalent to a zero determinant.

Step by step.

  1. Factor: p(λ) = (λ − 1)(λ − 2)(λ − 3), so the eigenvalues are 1, 2, 3.
  2. det(A) = 1 × 2 × 3 = 6. True.
  3. tr(A) = 1 + 2 + 3 = 6, matching −c₁ = 6. True.
  4. p(0) = −6 ≠ 0, so 0 is not a root and not an eigenvalue. False.
  5. det(A) = 6 ≠ 0, so A is invertible. True.

Answer: (A), (B) and (D)

Common misconception. Reading p(0) = −6 as "the determinant is −6" and then concluding singularity from the sign. For a 3 × 3 matrix the constant term is −det(A), so the sign flip is expected and says nothing about invertibility.

EM-U2-Q21NATeasy

The characteristic polynomial of a 3 × 3 real matrix A is p(λ) = λ³ − 7λ² + 14λ − 8. Find det(A).

Show answer & solution

Answer: 8

Detailed solution

Concept. For an n × n matrix, the constant term of the characteristic polynomial equals (−1)ⁿ det(A), and the product of the eigenvalues also equals det(A). Either route gives the same number.

Step by step.

  1. Factor: λ³ − 7λ² + 14λ − 8 = (λ − 1)(λ − 2)(λ − 4). Check: sum = 7, pairwise sum = 2 + 4 + 8 = 14, product = 8.
  2. The eigenvalues are 1, 2, 4, so det(A) = 1 × 2 × 4 = 8.
  3. Cross-check with the coefficient rule: n = 3 is odd, so the constant term −8 equals (−1)³ det(A) = −det(A), giving det(A) = 8.

Answer: 8

Common misconception. Answering −8 by quoting the constant term directly. For odd n the constant term is −det(A); for even n it is +det(A). The parity of n decides the sign.

EM-U2-Q26MCQeasy

The characteristic equation of the matrix A = [[3, 1], [0, 2]] is

  1. λ² − 5λ + 6 = 0
  2. λ² + 5λ + 6 = 0
  3. λ² − 5λ − 6 = 0
  4. λ² − 6λ + 5 = 0
Show answer & solution
