Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. The characteristic equation of a square matrix is det(A − λI) = 0. For a 2 × 2 matrix it collapses to λ² − (tr A)λ + det A = 0, so only the trace and the determinant are needed.

Step by step.

  1. Here tr A = 3 + 2 = 5 and det A = 3·2 − 1·0 = 6.
  2. Substituting: λ² − 5λ + 6 = 0.
  3. The roots are λ = 2 and λ = 3, which are the eigenvalues.

Final answer. Option (A) — λ² − 5λ + 6 = 0.

Common trap. Writing the equation as det(A) − λ = 0, or losing the minus sign in front of the trace term. The coefficient of λ is −tr A and the constant term is +det A for every 2 × 2 matrix; the constant is not −det A.

EM-U2-Q31MCQeasy

For any n × n matrix, the sum of its eigenvalues, counted with algebraic multiplicity, equals

  1. det A
  2. tr A
  3. rank A
  4. |det A|
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Expanding det(λI − A) gives λⁿ − (tr A)λⁿ⁻¹ + …. The coefficient of λⁿ⁻¹ is minus the trace, and it also equals minus the sum of the roots — so the sum of the eigenvalues is the trace.

Step by step.

  1. Write the characteristic polynomial as λⁿ − (Σλᵢ)λⁿ⁻¹ + … by factoring over ℂ.
  2. Compare with the expansion of det(λI − A), whose λⁿ⁻¹ coefficient is −tr A.
  3. Therefore Σλᵢ = tr A.

Final answer. Option (B) — tr A.

Common trap. Swapping the two invariants: the sum of eigenvalues is the trace and the product is the determinant. The mistake is invisible for the 2 × 2 example with eigenvalues 1, 2 and 3, where both happen to equal 6 — a favourite trap of examiners.

EM-U2-Q36MCQmedium

A 3 × 3 matrix has eigenvalues 1, 1 and 2. Which statement about its minimal polynomial m(λ) is TRUE?

  1. m(λ) must be (λ − 1)²(λ − 2).
  2. m(λ) must be (λ − 1)(λ − 2).
  3. m(λ) is either (λ − 1)(λ − 2) or (λ − 1)²(λ − 2), depending on the matrix.
  4. m(λ) must be (λ − 1)²(λ − 2)².
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. The minimal polynomial divides the characteristic polynomial and has the same distinct roots. A repeated eigenvalue may or may not need a repeated factor — that depends on whether the matrix is defective at that eigenvalue.

Step by step.

  1. The characteristic polynomial is (λ − 1)²(λ − 2), so the minimal polynomial is (λ − 1)(λ − 2) or (λ − 1)²(λ − 2).
  2. If the eigenspace for λ = 1 is two-dimensional (for instance a diagonal matrix), the single factor (λ − 1) already annihilates the matrix.
  3. If that eigenspace is only one-dimensional (a Jordan block of size 2), the square (λ − 1)² is required.
  4. Both behaviours occur among 3 × 3 matrices with these eigenvalues, so neither form is forced.

Final answer. Option (C) — either form, depending on the matrix.

Common trap. Assuming the minimal polynomial always equals the characteristic polynomial. Equality needs distinct eigenvalues; with a repeated eigenvalue the minimal polynomial can be strictly smaller.

EM-U2-Q40MCQhard

A real 3 × 3 matrix has eigenvalues 2, 2 and 5 and is diagonalizable. The rank of A − 2I is

  1. 0
  2. 1
  3. 2
  4. 3
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Diagonalizability means the geometric multiplicity of each eigenvalue equals its algebraic multiplicity. The geometric multiplicity of λ is nullity(A − λI), and rank plus nullity equals n.

Step by step.

  1. The eigenvalue 2 has algebraic multiplicity 2, so diagonalizability forces nullity(A − 2I) = 2.
  2. With n = 3: rank(A − 2I) = 3 − 2 = 1.
  3. Consistency check: A − 2I must still have the eigenvalue 3 (from λ = 5), so its rank cannot be 0.

Final answer. Option (B) — rank 1.

Common trap. Using the algebraic multiplicity directly as a nullity without checking diagonalizability, or forgetting the rank–nullity link and answering 2. Only diagonalizability justifies reading the geometric multiplicity straight off the characteristic polynomial.

EM-U2-Q42MSQeasy

Which of the following statements about the characteristic polynomial are TRUE?

  1. The characteristic polynomial of an n × n matrix has degree n.
  2. λ = 0 is an eigenvalue of A if and only if A is singular.
  3. A real 2 × 2 matrix with no real eigenvalue has no eigenvalue at all.
  4. If A is triangular, its eigenvalues are exactly its diagonal entries.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. The characteristic polynomial is det(λI − A), a monic polynomial of degree n whose roots in ℂ are the eigenvalues. Over ℝ a polynomial can have no real root, but over ℂ it always has n roots counted with multiplicity.

Step by step.

  1. The determinant of λI − A expands into n factors of λ, so the degree is n — statement A.
  2. λ = 0 is a root exactly when det(−A) = 0, that is when A is singular — statement B.
  3. For a triangular matrix the determinant det(λI − A) is the product of λ − aᵢᵢ, so the eigenvalues are the diagonal entries — statement D.
  4. Statement C is false: by the fundamental theorem of algebra the polynomial always has roots in ℂ; for [[0, −1], [1, 0]] they are ±i.

Final answer. Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common trap. Assuming “no real eigenvalue” means “no eigenvalue”. A rotation by 90° in the plane has eigenvalues ±i; it simply has no real eigenvector.

EM-U2-Q46NATeasy

The eigenvalues of A = [[4, 1], [2, 3]] are 5 and λ. The value of λ is ____.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 2

Detailed solution

Concept. For a 2 × 2 matrix the two eigenvalues are the roots of λ² − (tr A)λ + det A = 0, so once one root is known the other follows from λ₁ + λ₂ = tr A or λ₁λ₂ = det A.

Step by step.

  1. Here tr A = 4 + 3 = 7 and det A = 12 − 2 = 10.
  2. The characteristic equation is λ² − 7λ + 10 = 0, that is (λ − 5)(λ − 2) = 0.
  3. With one root equal to 5, the other is 7 − 5 = 2 (and 10/5 = 2 agrees).

Final answer. 2

Common trap. Answering 3 by reading the bottom-right entry a₂₂ as an eigenvalue. Diagonal entries are eigenvalues only for triangular matrices; this matrix is not triangular.

EM-U2-Q51MCQeasy

The matrix A = [[5, 2], [3, 4]] has trace 9 and determinant 14. Its characteristic equation is

  1. λ² − 9λ + 14 = 0
  2. λ² − 9λ + 20 = 0
  3. λ² + 9λ + 14 = 0
  4. λ² − 14λ + 9 = 0
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. For a 2 × 2 matrix the characteristic polynomial is p(λ) = λ² − (tr A)λ + det A, so the equation is fixed by the trace and the determinant alone — no expansion of det(λI − A) is needed.

Step by step.

  1. Trace: tr A = 5 + 4 = 9, which is the sum of the eigenvalues.
  2. Determinant: det A = 5·4 − 2·3 = 20 − 6 = 14, which is their product.
  3. Substituting into λ² − (tr A)λ + det A = 0 gives λ² − 9λ + 14 = 0, whose roots are λ = 7 and λ = 2.
  4. Check with λ = 7: 49 − 63 + 14 = 0.

Answer: Option (A), λ² − 9λ + 14 = 0.

Common misconception. Reading the determinant as ad = 20 and stopping there (option B), or as ad + bc = 26. The off-diagonal product is subtracted, not added.

Takeaway. For any 2 × 2 matrix: characteristic equation λ² − (tr A)λ + det A = 0. Compute the trace and determinant first and the polynomial writes itself.

EM-U2-Q54NATeasy

The characteristic polynomial of a 3 × 3 real matrix A is p(λ) = λ³ − 4λ² + λ + 6. The product of the three eigenvalues of A, counted with algebraic multiplicity, is ____.

Show answer & solution

Answer: -6

Detailed solution

Concept. For an n × n matrix p(λ) = λⁿ − (tr A)λⁿ⁻¹ + … + (−1)ⁿ det A, so the constant term carries the determinant, and the determinant is the product of the eigenvalues.

Step by step.

  1. The constant term of p is 6, and for n = 3 it equals −det A, so det A = −6.
  2. Independent check by factoring: p(3) = 27 − 36 + 3 + 6 = 0, so λ − 3 is a factor, and λ³ − 4λ² + λ + 6 = (λ − 3)(λ − 2)(λ + 1).
  3. The product of the roots is 3 · 2 · (−1) = −6, matching det A.

Answer: −6

Common misconception. Answering 6 by taking the constant term as the determinant. The sign alternates with the size of the matrix: for n = 3 the constant term is −det A.

Takeaway. The constant term of p(λ) = det(λI − A) is (−1)ⁿ det A. Check the sign by testing one root when you have it.

EM-U2-Q58MCQmedium

A real 2 × 2 matrix A has characteristic polynomial p(λ) = λ² + 4λ + 13. Which statement is TRUE?

  1. The eigenvalues are −2 + 3i and −2 − 3i, so A is not diagonalizable over ℝ.
  2. The eigenvalues are 2 + 3i and 2 − 3i.
  3. The eigenvalues are −2 and −2.
  4. A has no eigenvalues at all, even over ℂ.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Every real square matrix has n eigenvalues over ℂ, counted with multiplicity, and a real characteristic polynomial has non-real roots in conjugate pairs. A real matrix with a non-real pair has no real eigenvector, so it cannot be diagonalized over ℝ.

Step by step.

  1. Discriminant: 16 − 4·13 = −36 = (6i)².
  2. Roots: λ = (−4 ± 6i)/2 = −2 ± 3i, a conjugate pair.
  3. Since no eigenvalue is real, A has no real eigenvector and no real diagonal form; over ℂ the two distinct eigenvalues do diagonalize it.
  4. Consistency check: tr A = −4 and det A = (−2)² + 3² = 13, which reproduce λ² + 4λ + 13.

Answer: Option (A): −2 ± 3i, not diagonalizable over ℝ.

Common misconception. Dividing only the discriminant's square root by 2 and leaving −4/2 as +2, which produces the wrong real part 2 ± 3i.

Takeaway. A negative discriminant means a conjugate pair, and the real part is always −b/2a. A real matrix with such a pair is diagonalizable over ℂ but never over ℝ.

EM-U2-Q63MCQhard

A 3 × 3 real matrix A satisfies tr(A) = 5, tr(A²) = 15 and tr(A³) = 35. Then det(A) equals

  1. 5
  2. −5
  3. 15
  4. 3
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Newton's identities convert traces of powers into the elementary symmetric functions of the eigenvalues. With p₁, p₂, p₃ the traces of A, A², A³ and e₁, e₂, e₃ the coefficients of the characteristic polynomial: p₂ = e₁p₁ − 2e₂ and p₃ = e₁p₂ − e₂p₁ + 3e₃, while det A = e₃.

Step by step.

  1. Here e₁ = p₁ = 5.
  2. p₂ = e₁p₁ − 2e₂ gives 15 = 25 − 2e₂, so e₂ = 5.
  3. p₃ = e₁p₂ − e₂p₁ + 3e₃ gives 35 = 75 − 25 + 3e₃, so 3e₃ = −15 and e₃ = −5.
  4. Therefore det A = e₃ = −5, with characteristic polynomial λ³ − 5λ² + 5λ + 5.

Answer: Option (B): −5.

Common misconception. Reading the determinant from p₃ or from e₂. For n = 3 the constant term is −e₃, and e₂ is the coefficient of λ, not the constant.

Takeaway. Traces of powers determine the characteristic polynomial completely. Learn the three identities for n = 3 and check the sign of e₃ at the end.

EM-U2-Q66MCQmedium

The characteristic polynomial of A = [[1, 2, 0], [0, 3, 0], [0, 0, 5]] is

  1. λ³ − 9λ² + 15λ − 23
  2. λ³ + 9λ² + 23λ + 15
  3. λ³ − 9λ² + 23λ − 15
  4. λ³ − 15λ² + 23λ − 9
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. The eigenvalues of a triangular matrix are its diagonal entries, so the characteristic polynomial is the product of (λ − dᵢ). Expanding gives the coefficients e₁, e₂ and e₃ from the diagonal entries.

Step by step.

  1. Upper triangular, so the eigenvalues are 1, 3 and 5 and p(λ) = (λ − 1)(λ − 3)(λ − 5).
  2. Coefficient of λ²: −(1 + 3 + 5) = −9.
  3. Coefficient of λ: 1·3 + 1·5 + 3·5 = 23.
  4. Constant: −(1·3·5) = −15, so p(λ) = λ³ − 9λ² + 23λ − 15.
  5. The off-diagonal entry 2 does not appear: the spectrum of a triangular matrix ignores it.

Answer: Option (C): λ³ − 9λ² + 23λ − 15.

Common misconception. Reading the (1, 2) entry as an eigenvalue or letting it change the determinant, or mis-ordering the middle coefficients (options A and D).

Takeaway. For a triangular matrix, multiply (λ − dᵢ) and expand, or use −e₁, e₂, −e₃. Verify by substituting one diagonal entry as a root.

EM-U2-Q69MCQeasy

The eigenvalues of A = [[2, 3], [0, 5]] are

  1. 2 and 3
  2. 2 and 5
  3. −2 and −5
  4. 1 and 10
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. The eigenvalues of a triangular matrix are exactly its diagonal entries, because det(λI − A) is then the product of the diagonal entries of λI − A.

Step by step.

  1. Here λI − A = [[λ − 2, −3], [0, λ − 5]], still upper triangular.
  2. Its determinant is (λ − 2)(λ − 5), so the eigenvalues are 2 and 5.
  3. Consistency: tr A = 7 = 2 + 5 and det A = 10 = 2·5.

Answer: Option (B): 2 and 5.

Common misconception. Taking the off-diagonal 3 as an eigenvalue, which gives option A. The entry 3 shifts nothing: it is not on the diagonal.

Takeaway. For any triangular matrix read the diagonal. Only non-triangular matrices need the determinant expanded.

EM-U2-Q71NATmedium

A 3 × 3 real matrix A has characteristic polynomial p(λ) = λ³ − 6λ² + 11λ − 6. The value of det(A² + I) is ____.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 100

Detailed solution

Concept. The roots of the characteristic polynomial are the eigenvalues, and a polynomial q applied to A has eigenvalues q(λᵢ). Its determinant is the product of those transformed eigenvalues.

Step by step.

  1. Factor: λ³ − 6λ² + 11λ − 6 = (λ − 1)(λ − 2)(λ − 3), so the eigenvalues are 1, 2 and 3.
  2. Apply q(t) = t² + 1: q(1) = 2, q(2) = 5, q(3) = 10.
  3. The eigenvalues of A² + I are 2, 5 and 10, so det(A² + I) = 2·5·10 = 100.

Answer: 100

Common misconception. Computing (det A)² + 1 = 36 + 1 = 37 by treating the determinant as if it were an eigenvalue, or forgetting to add the 1 to each eigenvalue before multiplying.

Takeaway. Factor the characteristic polynomial first, then push each root through the polynomial in A. Never apply a polynomial to the determinant.

EM-U2-Q74MSQeasy

The characteristic polynomial of A = [[2, 3], [3, 2]] is p(λ) = λ² − 4λ − 5. Which of the following statements are TRUE?

  1. The eigenvalues of A are 5 and −1.
  2. A is diagonalizable over ℝ.
  3. det(A) = −5.
  4. tr(A) = 5.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Roots of the characteristic polynomial are the eigenvalues; for a symmetric matrix they are real and the matrix is orthogonally diagonalizable. The trace and determinant are read from the coefficients.

Step by step.

  1. Factor: λ² − 4λ − 5 = (λ − 5)(λ + 1), so the eigenvalues are 5 and −1 — A is TRUE.
  2. A is real symmetric, so it is diagonalizable over ℝ — B is TRUE.
  3. Constant term −5 equals det A for n = 2 — C is TRUE.
  4. The coefficient of λ is −4 = −tr A, so tr A = 4, not 5 — D is FALSE.
  5. Direct check: tr A = 2 + 2 = 4 and det A = 4 − 9 = −5.

Answer: Statements (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Reading the trace from the constant term or the determinant from the linear coefficient. For 2 × 2: p(λ) = λ² − (tr A)λ + det A.

Takeaway. Match the coefficients to the invariants before answering: λ² coefficient −tr, constant det for 2 × 2.

Eigenvalue properties 23
EM-U2-Q03MCQeasy

A 5 × 5 real matrix A has eigenvalues 1, 2, 3, 4, x. Given tr(A) = 15, find x.

  1. 4
  2. 5
  3. 6
  4. It cannot be determined from the given data.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. The trace is the sum of the eigenvalues counted with algebraic multiplicity — that is, with repeats. For a 5 × 5 matrix there are exactly five eigenvalues in this count, so the trace gives one linear equation in the unknown one.

Step by step.

  1. Write the sum: 1 + 2 + 3 + 4 + x = 15.
  2. The four known eigenvalues contribute 10, so x = 15 − 10 = 5.
  3. Consistency check: the eigenvalues are then 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, five values for a 5 × 5 matrix, and their sum is 15 = tr(A).

Answer: Option (B) — x = 5

Common misconception. Treating the trace as a product, or assuming the eigenvalues must be distinct or must divide the determinant. Option (D) attracts candidates who think the matrix entries are needed: they are not, because the trace is a spectral quantity and the spectrum is given.

EM-U2-Q04MCQeasy

A 3 × 3 matrix A has eigenvalues 1, −1, 2. Find det(A² + A + I).

  1. 7
  2. 12
  3. 21
  4. 0
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. If λ is an eigenvalue of A with eigenvector v, then for any polynomial f, f(A)v = f(λ)v. So the eigenvalues of f(A) are exactly f(λᵢ), and the determinant of a matrix is the product of its eigenvalues.

Step by step.

  1. Here f(t) = t² + t + 1.
  2. λ = 1: f(1) = 1 + 1 + 1 = 3.
  3. λ = −1: f(−1) = 1 − 1 + 1 = 1.
  4. λ = 2: f(2) = 4 + 2 + 1 = 7.
  5. The eigenvalues of A² + A + I are 3, 1, 7, so det(A² + A + I) = 3 × 1 × 7 = 21.

Answer: Option (C) — 21

Common misconception. Distributing the determinant over the sum, that is computing det(A²) + det(A) + det(I) = 4 + (−2) + 1 = 3 — which is not even an option, and is a useful reminder that det is multiplicative but never additive.

EM-U2-Q08MCQmedium

A 4 × 4 invertible matrix A has eigenvalues 1, 2, 3, 4. Find tr(A⁻¹).

  1. 1/24
  2. 25/12
  3. 10
  4. 1/10
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Two spectral rules combine here. First, A⁻¹ has eigenvalues 1/λᵢ with the same eigenvectors. Second, the trace is the sum of the eigenvalues. The trace of an inverse is therefore a sum of reciprocals, and it has nothing to do with the reciprocal of the trace.

Step by step.

  1. Eigenvalues of A⁻¹: 1, 1/2, 1/3, 1/4.
  2. tr(A⁻¹) = 1 + 1/2 + 1/3 + 1/4.
  3. Common denominator 12: 12/12 + 6/12 + 4/12 + 3/12 = 25/12.
  4. Order-of-magnitude check: four terms between 0.25 and 1 must sum to something between 1 and 4; 25/12 ≈ 2.08 fits, 1/24 and 1/10 do not.

Answer: Option (B) — 25/12

Common misconception. Writing tr(A⁻¹) = 1/tr(A) = 1/10, which is option (D). That identity would force tr(A)·tr(A⁻¹) = 1, and it already fails for the scalar matrix 2I, where tr(A) = tr(A⁻¹) = 2.

EM-U2-Q15MCQhard

Let A and B be n × n matrices with AB = BA, and suppose A has n distinct eigenvalues. Which statement is TRUE?

  1. A and B are simultaneously diagonalizable: there is an invertible P for which both P⁻¹AP and P⁻¹BP are diagonal.
  2. B must also have n distinct eigenvalues.
  3. AB is diagonalizable only if B is invertible.
  4. B must equal 0 or I.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Commutativity alone does not give a common eigenbasis — it does so once one of the two matrices already has a full set of distinct eigenvalues. The proof is a one-line eigenspace argument: commuting matrices preserve each other's eigenspaces.

Step by step.

  1. A has n distinct eigenvalues, so its eigenvectors v₁, …, vₙ are linearly independent and form a basis.
  2. Let Av = λv. Then A(Bv) = (AB)v = (BA)v = B(Av) = B(λv) = λ(Bv).
  3. So Bv lies in the eigenspace of λ, which is one-dimensional because λ is a simple root. Hence Bv = μv for some scalar μ.
  4. Every basis vector is therefore an eigenvector of both matrices, so P⁻¹AP = diag(λᵢ) and P⁻¹BP = diag(μᵢ) for the same P.
  5. The other options fail: B may be any diagonal matrix in that basis, so it can have repeated eigenvalues, can be singular, and need not be a scalar matrix.

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Believing that commutativity by itself implies simultaneous diagonalizability. It does not: A = I commutes with everything, yet no eigenbasis of I is preferred, so B need not be diagonalizable at all. The distinct eigenvalues of A are what supply the common basis.

EM-U2-Q16MSQeasy

A 3 × 3 real matrix A has eigenvalues 1, 2, 3. Which statements are TRUE?

  1. det(A) = 6
  2. tr(A) = 6
  3. The eigenvalues of Aᵀ are 1, 2, 3.
  4. A must be symmetric.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. The spectrum determines the determinant and the trace, and transposition preserves the spectrum — but it does not force symmetry, nor is symmetry implied by anything spectral.

Step by step.

  1. det(A) = 1 × 2 × 3 = 6. True.
  2. tr(A) = 1 + 2 + 3 = 6. True.
  3. det(Aᵀ − λI) = det((A − λI)ᵀ) = det(A − λI), so A and Aᵀ share a characteristic polynomial and hence a spectrum. True.
  4. A = [[1, 1, 0], [0, 2, 1], [0, 0, 3]] is upper triangular with the required eigenvalues and is not symmetric. False.

Answer: (A), (B) and (C)

Common misconception. Confusing "symmetric matrices have real eigenvalues" with its converse. Real eigenvalues are far more common than symmetry; a triangular matrix with real diagonal entries already has them.

EM-U2-Q22NATeasy

A 2 × 2 real matrix A has eigenvalues 3 and 5. Find tr(A²).

Show answer & solution

Answer: 34

Detailed solution

Concept. Squaring a matrix squares each eigenvalue and keeps its eigenvector. The trace of the result is therefore the sum of the squared eigenvalues — it is not the square of the trace.

Step by step.

  1. Eigenvalues of A²: 3² = 9 and 5² = 25.
  2. tr(A²) = 9 + 25 = 34.
  3. Check the other way round: tr(A) = 8 and det(A) = 15, so A² − 8A + 15I = 0 by Cayley–Hamilton. Taking traces, tr(A²) − 8·tr(A) + 15·tr(I) = 0, so tr(A²) = 64 − 30 = 34. Both routes agree.

Answer: 34

Common misconception. Answering tr(A)² = 64. The trace is linear, not multiplicative: tr(A²) = Σλᵢ² while tr(A)² = (Σλᵢ)², and the two differ by 2 det(A) = 30 here.

EM-U2-Q25NAThard

Let A be a real 3 × 3 matrix with A³ = I and A ≠ I. Find tr(A).

Show answer & solution

Answer: 0

Detailed solution

Concept. A matrix identity constrains the spectrum. If A³ = I then every eigenvalue satisfies λ³ = 1, so λ is one of the cube roots of unity. Because A is real, its characteristic polynomial has real coefficients, so non-real roots must occur in conjugate pairs — and the only non-real pair available is ω, ω².

Step by step.

  1. Let ω = e^{2πi/3}. The cube roots of unity are 1, ω, ω², with ω + ω² = −1 and ωω² = 1.
  2. Since A³ = I, the minimal polynomial divides x³ − 1 = (x − 1)(x − ω)(x − ω²). That polynomial has distinct roots, so A is diagonalizable over ℂ and its only possible eigenvalues are 1, ω, ω².
  3. The characteristic polynomial is a real cubic of degree 3, so its non-real roots come in conjugate pairs. The possibilities are: (x − 1)³, or (x − 1)(x − ω)(x − ω²).
  4. If p(x) = (x − 1)³ then A is diagonalizable with every eigenvalue equal to 1, so A = I — excluded by hypothesis.
  5. Hence the spectrum is {1, ω, ω²} and tr(A) = 1 + ω + ω² = 0. A concrete example is the 120° rotation [[0, 1, 0], [0, 0, 1], [1, 0, 0]], whose trace is 0.

Answer: 0

Common misconception. Answering 3 by assuming all three eigenvalues must be 1, or answering 1 by forgetting that the two non-real roots still contribute to the trace. The condition A ≠ I is precisely what rules out the all-ones spectrum.

EM-U2-Q27MCQeasy

A 3 × 3 matrix A has eigenvalues 1, 2 and 4. Then det(A) equals

  1. 7
  2. 8
  3. 6
  4. 0
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. The determinant of a matrix is the product of its eigenvalues counted with algebraic multiplicity, while the trace is their sum. These are the two coefficients of the characteristic polynomial that can be read off without expanding anything.

Step by step.

  1. The three eigenvalues are 1, 2 and 4, all simple.
  2. det(A) = 1 · 2 · 4 = 8.
  3. For comparison, tr A = 1 + 2 + 4 = 7, which is option (A) — the wrong quantity.

Final answer. Option (B) — 8.

Common trap. Answering the trace because it was the number just computed in a neighbouring formula, or assuming det A = 0 whenever an eigenvalue looks small. A zero determinant needs an eigenvalue equal to 0, and none of these is 0.

EM-U2-Q28MCQeasy

Let λ be an eigenvalue of a matrix A with eigenvector x. Then λ² is certainly an eigenvalue of

  1. A⁻¹
  2. A²
  3. Aᵀ
  4. A + I
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Eigenvalues of a polynomial in A are the same polynomial evaluated at the eigenvalues of A: if Ax = λx then p(A)x = p(λ)x with the very same eigenvector x.

Step by step.

  1. Ax = λx, so A²x = A(λx) = λ(Ax) = λ²x.
  2. Hence λ² is an eigenvalue of A² with eigenvector x.
  3. A⁻¹ has eigenvalue 1/λ (and only when A is invertible), Aᵀ has eigenvalue λ itself, and A + I has eigenvalue λ + 1.

Final answer. Option (B) — A².

Common trap. Believing that squaring the matrix squares nothing but the entries, or that Aᵀ must have eigenvalue λ². Transposition leaves eigenvalues unchanged; it does not square them.

EM-U2-Q32MCQmedium

A 2 × 2 matrix A has eigenvalues 2 and 5. The eigenvalues of A² − 3A + 2I are

  1. {0, 12}
  2. {2, 5}
  3. {0, 10}
  4. {4, 25}
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Spectral mapping: if p is a polynomial and λ is an eigenvalue of A, then p(λ) is an eigenvalue of p(A), with the same eigenvector. Polynomials act on eigenvalues entrywise.

Step by step.

  1. For λ = 2: p(2) = 4 − 6 + 2 = 0.
  2. For λ = 5: p(5) = 25 − 15 + 2 = 12.
  3. So the eigenvalues of p(A) are 0 and 12.

Final answer. Option (A) — {0, 12}.

Common trap. Applying the polynomial to the whole matrix and then trying to guess eigenvalues, or forgetting that the constant term 2I shifts every eigenvalue by 2 rather than scaling it. Note that p(A) is singular here, which is exactly what the eigenvalue 0 says.

EM-U2-Q33MCQmedium

Let A and B be similar matrices, B = P⁻¹AP. Which of the following is NOT necessarily the same for A and B?

  1. the set of eigenvalues
  2. the determinant
  3. the trace
  4. the set of eigenvectors
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Similarity preserves everything built from the characteristic polynomial — eigenvalues, trace, determinant, rank, minimal polynomial — but it moves eigenvectors: the eigenvector x of A corresponds to the eigenvector P⁻¹x of B.

Step by step.

  1. If Ax = λx then (P⁻¹AP)(P⁻¹x) = P⁻¹Ax = λ(P⁻¹x), so P⁻¹x is the matching eigenvector of B.
  2. The eigenvalues, trace and determinant are unchanged because the characteristic polynomial is unchanged.
  3. The eigenvectors themselves are transformed, so the two sets coincide only in special cases.

Final answer. Option (D) — the set of eigenvectors.

Common trap. Treating similarity as “the same matrix in different coordinates” and concluding that every object is preserved. Coordinates change exactly the vectors; only the coordinate-free quantities (eigenvalues, trace, determinant) survive.

EM-U2-Q37MCQhard

A real matrix A satisfies A² = A. Which statement about its eigenvalues is TRUE?

  1. Every eigenvalue of A is 0 or 1.
  2. Every eigenvalue of A is 0.
  3. Every eigenvalue of A is 1.
  4. The eigenvalues can be arbitrary real numbers.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. An eigenvalue identity for A transfers to the same identity for λ: from Ax = λx with x ≠ 0 we get A²x = λ²x, and A² = A forces λ² = λ.

Step by step.

  1. Let Ax = λx, x ≠ 0.
  2. Then A²x = λ²x, while Ax = λx because A² = A.
  3. So λ²x = λx and, since x ≠ 0, λ² = λ.
  4. The only roots are λ = 0 and λ = 1.

Final answer. Option (A) — every eigenvalue is 0 or 1.

Common trap. Concluding that A must be either the zero matrix or the identity. Both eigenvalues can occur in the same matrix — a projection onto a subspace has eigenvalue 1 on the subspace and 0 on its complement — and A need not be diagonal unless it is also normal.

EM-U2-Q41MSQeasy

For an n × n matrix A, which of the following statements are TRUE?

  1. det A is the product of the eigenvalues counted with algebraic multiplicity.
  2. tr A is the sum of the eigenvalues counted with algebraic multiplicity.
  3. A and Aᵀ have the same eigenvalues.
  4. Similar matrices always have the same eigenvectors.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. The characteristic polynomial carries the trace and the determinant in two of its coefficients, and transposition does not change it at all. Similarity preserves the polynomial too, but not the eigenvectors.

Step by step.

  1. det(λI − A) = λⁿ − (tr A)λⁿ⁻¹ + … + (−1)ⁿ det A, and factoring over ℂ exhibits the eigenvalues as roots — hence statements A and B.
  2. det(λI − Aᵀ) = det((λI − A)ᵀ) = det(λI − A), so Aᵀ has the same characteristic polynomial and the same eigenvalues — statement C.
  3. Under B = P⁻¹AP the eigenvector x of A becomes P⁻¹x, so the eigenvector sets generally differ — statement D is false.

Final answer. Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common trap. Treating Aᵀ as unrelated to A. Transposition changes neither the determinant nor the characteristic polynomial, so it preserves the whole eigenvalue multiset even though the eigenvectors change.

EM-U2-Q45MSQmedium

A 3 × 3 matrix A has eigenvalues 1, 2 and 3. Which of the following statements are TRUE?

  1. det(A⁻¹) = 1/6
  2. The eigenvalues of A + I are 2, 3 and 4.
  3. The eigenvalues of A + Aᵀ are 2, 4 and 6.
  4. The eigenvalues of A⁻¹ are 1, 1/2 and 1/3.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Polynomials and inverses act on eigenvalues one at a time, but sums of two different matrices do not: the eigenvalues of A + Aᵀ are not determined by those of A unless A is normal.

Step by step.

  1. det A = 1·2·3 = 6 and det(A⁻¹) = 1/det A = 1/6 — statement A.
  2. Adding I shifts every eigenvalue by 1 — statement B.
  3. Inverting replaces λ by 1/λ — statement D.
  4. Statement C is false: eigenvalues of a sum of matrices are not the sums of eigenvalues. For A = [[1, 1, 0], [0, 2, 0], [0, 0, 3]] the matrix A + Aᵀ does not have eigenvalues 2, 4, 6.

Final answer. Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common trap. Applying the spectral-mapping rule to sums. It is valid for polynomials and for inverses, which act on a single matrix, but λᵢ(A + B) ≠ λᵢ(A) + λᵢ(B) in general.

EM-U2-Q47NATmedium

A 3 × 3 matrix A has eigenvalues 2, 3 and 4. Then det(A²) is ____.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 576

Detailed solution

Concept. The determinant is multiplicative, det(A²) = (det A)², and the determinant of A is the product of its eigenvalues. Equivalently, the eigenvalues of A² are the squares of those of A.

Step by step.

  1. det A = 2 · 3 · 4 = 24.
  2. det(A²) = (det A)² = 24² = 576.
  3. Check by spectral mapping: the eigenvalues of A² are 4, 9 and 16, whose product is 4·9·16 = 576.

Final answer. 576

Common trap. Computing 2² + 3² + 4² = 29, which is tr(A²) and not det(A²). Squaring a matrix squares its determinant; it does not square the trace.

EM-U2-Q50NAThard

Let A = [[1, 2], [2, 1]]. The value of tr(A⁴) is ____.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 82

Detailed solution

Concept. The trace is the sum of the eigenvalues, and spectral mapping carries this over to powers: the eigenvalues of Aᵏ are the k-th powers of those of A, so tr(Aᵏ) = Σλᵢᵏ. The trace is invariant under the change of basis that diagonalizes A.

Step by step.

  1. tr A = 2 and det A = 1 − 4 = −3, so the characteristic equation is λ² − 2λ − 3 = 0.
  2. The roots are λ = 3 and λ = −1, and A is symmetric, hence diagonalizable with these eigenvalues.
  3. Then tr(A⁴) = 3⁴ + (−1)⁴ = 81 + 1 = 82.

Final answer. 82

Common trap. Computing A⁴ entry by entry and summing the diagonal, which invites arithmetic slips, or writing (tr A)⁴ = 16. The fourth power of the trace has nothing to do with the trace of the fourth power.

EM-U2-Q52MCQeasy

A real 2 × 2 matrix A has eigenvalues 2 + i and 2 − i. Then

  1. tr(A) = 4 and det(A) = 3
  2. tr(A) = 2 and det(A) = 5
  3. tr(A) = 4 and det(A) = 5
  4. tr(A) = 5 and det(A) = 4
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. The trace is the sum of the eigenvalues and the determinant is their product, and both are real numbers. A real matrix with a non-real eigenvalue always has the conjugate as the other eigenvalue, so the pair a ± bi produces real trace 2a and real determinant a² + b².

Step by step.

  1. Sum: (2 + i) + (2 − i) = 4, so tr A = 4.
  2. Product: (2 + i)(2 − i) = 4 − i² = 4 + 1 = 5, so det A = 5.
  3. Both values are real, as they must be for a real matrix, and they fix the characteristic polynomial λ² − 4λ + 5.

Answer: Option (C): tr(A) = 4 and det(A) = 5.

Common misconception. Multiplying the conjugates as 4 − 1 = 3 by forgetting that i² = −1, which produces option A. The determinant of a real matrix is real even when the eigenvalues are not.

Takeaway. For a conjugate pair a ± bi: trace 2a, determinant a² + b². Both are real, and a² + b² > 0, so such a matrix is always invertible.

EM-U2-Q56MCQmedium

A 3 × 3 real matrix A has eigenvalues 1, −1 and 2. Then det(A² + 2A) equals

  1. −24
  2. 24
  3. 0
  4. −8
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Spectral mapping: if λ is an eigenvalue of A then p(λ) is an eigenvalue of p(A) for every polynomial p. A polynomial in a matrix therefore has its determinant read off from the transformed eigenvalues.

Step by step.

  1. Apply p(t) = t² + 2t to each eigenvalue: p(1) = 3, p(−1) = −1, p(2) = 8.
  2. The eigenvalues of A² + 2A are 3, −1 and 8.
  3. Its determinant is their product: 3 · (−1) · 8 = −24.

Answer: Option (A): −24.

Common misconception. Computing det(A² + 2A) as (det A)² + 2 det A by treating the determinant as linear, or adding the transformed eigenvalues instead of multiplying them.

Takeaway. Polynomials move through to the eigenvalues; the determinant then multiplies them. Only linear operations on A itself, not on its determinant, behave additively.

EM-U2-Q60NATmedium

A 3 × 3 real matrix A has eigenvalues 2, −3 and 4. The value of tr(A²) is ____.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 29

Detailed solution

Concept. Spectral mapping turns powers of A into powers of its eigenvalues, and the trace of a matrix is the sum of its eigenvalues, so tr(Aᵏ) = Σ λᵢᵏ.

Step by step.

  1. The eigenvalues of A² are 2² = 4, (−3)² = 9 and 4² = 16.
  2. The trace of A² is their sum: 4 + 9 + 16 = 29.
  3. Cross-check through the invariants: tr(A²) = (tr A)² − 2e₂ with tr A = 3 and e₂ = 2·(−3) + 2·4 + (−3)·4 = −10, giving 9 + 20 = 29.

Answer: 29

Common misconception. Squaring the trace, 3² = 9, instead of squaring each eigenvalue and then summing. The trace is additive, not multiplicative.

Takeaway. For any polynomial p, tr(p(A)) = Σ p(λᵢ). Squares lose the sign of an eigenvalue, which is why −3 contributes 9 here.

EM-U2-Q62MCQeasy

A 3 × 3 real matrix A has eigenvalues −1, 2 and 5. The value of tr(A) + det(A) is

  1. 4
  2. 16
  3. 10
  4. −4
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Two invariants read the eigenvalues off in one step: the trace is their sum and the determinant is their product.

Step by step.

  1. Sum: (−1) + 2 + 5 = 6, so tr A = 6.
  2. Product: (−1)·2·5 = −10, so det A = −10.
  3. tr A + det A = 6 + (−10) = −4.

Answer: Option (D): −4.

Common misconception. Adding the absolute value of the determinant, 6 + 10 = 16, or forgetting that a single negative eigenvalue makes the product negative.

Takeaway. With an odd number of negative eigenvalues the determinant is negative; the trace keeps the sign of each term, so it can be small even when the determinant is large.

EM-U2-Q65MCQhard

Let A = [[3, 4], [4, −3]] and B = A² − 2A + 5I. Then tr(B) equals

  1. 60
  2. 30
  3. 50
  4. 20
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Two routes give the trace of a polynomial in A: transform the eigenvalues and add them, or use the invariants tr(A²), tr A and tr I. Both must agree, which is a free check.

Step by step.

  1. Eigenvalues of A: tr A = 0 and det A = −9 − 16 = −25, so λ² − 25 = 0 gives λ = ±5.
  2. Apply p(t) = t² − 2t + 5: p(5) = 25 − 10 + 5 = 20 and p(−5) = 25 + 10 + 5 = 40.
  3. tr(B) = 20 + 40 = 60.
  4. Check without eigenvalues: A² = [[9 + 16, 12 − 12], [12 − 12, 16 + 9]] = 25I, so B = 25I − 2A + 5I = 30I − 2A and tr B = 60 − 0 = 60.

Answer: Option (A): 60.

Common misconception. Evaluating p once at tr A = 0, which gives p(0) = 5, or forgetting that the two eigenvalues give two different values that must both be summed.

Takeaway. Compute tr(p(A)) = Σ p(λᵢ) and cross-check with the invariants of A. When the eigenvalues are a symmetric pair, both terms are needed.

EM-U2-Q67MSQeasy

A 3 × 3 real matrix A has eigenvalues 1, 1 and 4. Which of the following statements are TRUE?

  1. A must be symmetric.
  2. tr(A) = 6.
  3. det(A) = 4.
  4. A is invertible.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (B), (C) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. The trace and the determinant are the two invariants available from the spectrum alone: the trace adds the eigenvalues, the determinant multiplies them. Neither says anything about symmetry, which is a property of the matrix entries, not of the spectrum.

Step by step.

  1. Trace: 1 + 1 + 4 = 6 — statement B is TRUE.
  2. Determinant: 1·1·4 = 4 — statement C is TRUE.
  3. No eigenvalue is 0, so det A = 4 ≠ 0 and A is invertible — statement D is TRUE.
  4. Symmetry is not determined by the spectrum: [[1, 5, 0], [0, 1, 0], [0, 0, 4]] has the same eigenvalues and is not symmetric — statement A is FALSE.

Answer: Statements (B), (C) and (D).

Common misconception. Assuming a repeated eigenvalue forces symmetry, or assuming the matrix is singular because the eigenvalue 1 repeats. Repetition is not degeneracy.

Takeaway. Spectrum fixes trace, determinant, characteristic polynomial and invertibility — nothing about symmetry, norm or conditioning.

EM-U2-Q75NAThard

A 3 × 3 real matrix A satisfies tr(A) = 7, tr(A²) = 21 and det(A) = 8. The value of λ₁λ₂ + λ₂λ₃ + λ₃λ₁, where λ₁, λ₂, λ₃ are the eigenvalues of A, is ____.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 14

Detailed solution

Concept. The requested sum is the second elementary symmetric function e₂, which is the coefficient of λ in the characteristic polynomial. It follows from e₁ = tr A and tr(A²) = e₁² − 2e₂.

Step by step.

  1. e₁ = λ₁ + λ₂ + λ₃ = tr A = 7.
  2. tr(A²) = λ₁² + λ₂² + λ₃² = e₁² − 2e₂, so 21 = 49 − 2e₂.
  3. Hence e₂ = (49 − 21)/2 = 14.
  4. Consistency: e₁ = 7, e₂ = 14, e₃ = det A = 8 give p(λ) = λ³ − 7λ² + 14λ − 8 = (λ − 1)(λ − 2)(λ − 4), and 1·2 + 2·4 + 1·4 = 14.

Answer: 14

Common misconception. Answering 21 by reporting tr(A²), or 10.5 by forgetting to divide by 2. The identity is e₂ = (e₁² − tr(A²))/2, and the halving is not optional.

Takeaway. The three invariants e₁, e₂, e₃ are exactly the trace, the sum of principal 2 × 2 minors and the determinant; tr(A²) is the cheapest way to reach e₂.

Cayley–Hamilton theorem 14
EM-U2-Q05MCQeasy

Let A = [[1, 2], [3, 4]]. Which statement is TRUE?

  1. A² − 5A − 2I = 0
  2. A² − 5A + 2I = 0
  3. A² + 5A + 2I = 0
  4. A² = 5A
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. The Cayley–Hamilton theorem says every square matrix satisfies its own characteristic polynomial: if p(λ) = det(λI − A) = λⁿ + c₁λⁿ⁻¹ + … + cₙ then Aⁿ + c₁Aⁿ⁻¹ + … + cₙI = 0. For a 2 × 2 matrix the polynomial is λ² − tr(A)λ + det(A), so the identity is A² − tr(A)·A + det(A)·I = 0.

Step by step.

  1. tr(A) = 1 + 4 = 5 and det(A) = 1·4 − 2·3 = 4 − 6 = −2.
  2. Characteristic polynomial: λ² − 5λ + (−2) = λ² − 5λ − 2.
  3. Cayley–Hamilton: A² − 5A − 2I = 0. Note the constant term is det(A), which is −2, so the last term of the matrix identity is −2I, not +2I.
  4. Direct verification: A² = [[7, 10], [15, 22]], 5A = [[5, 10], [15, 20]], so A² − 5A = [[2, 0], [0, 2]] = 2I, that is A² − 5A − 2I = 0.

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Taking the constant term of λ² − tr λ + det as +det and then losing the sign of a negative determinant. The determinant here is −2, so the identity carries −2I. Option (B) is exactly this sign slip.

EM-U2-Q10MCQmedium

Let A = [[2, 3], [1, 2]]. Using the Cayley–Hamilton theorem, which expression equals A⁻¹?

  1. 4I − A
  2. A − 4I
  3. 4I + A
  4. ½(A − 4I)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Cayley–Hamilton converts the characteristic identity into a way of writing A⁻¹ as a polynomial in A. For a 2 × 2 matrix with A² − tr(A)A + det(A)I = 0, multiplying by A⁻¹ gives A − tr(A)I + det(A)A⁻¹ = 0, hence A⁻¹ = (tr(A)I − A)/det(A). No adjugate computation is needed.

Step by step.

  1. tr(A) = 2 + 2 = 4 and det(A) = 4 − 3 = 1.
  2. Characteristic polynomial: λ² − 4λ + 1.
  3. Cayley–Hamilton: A² − 4A + I = 0.
  4. Multiply by A⁻¹: A − 4I + A⁻¹ = 0, so A⁻¹ = 4I − A.
  5. Because det(A) = 1, no scalar division is required.
  6. Numerically: 4I − A = [[4 − 2, −3], [−1, 4 − 2]] = [[2, −3], [−1, 2]]. Check: A·[[2, −3], [−1, 2]] = [[4 − 3, −6 + 6], [2 − 2, −3 + 4]] = I.

Answer: Option (A) — A⁻¹ = 4I − A

Common misconception. A sign slip on the rearrangement, giving (B). It is worth memorising the direction: A⁻¹ = (tr(A)I − A)/det(A), so the identity matrix carries the positive sign and A carries the negative one.

EM-U2-Q13MCQhard

Let A = [[1, 2], [3, 4]]. Express A⁴ in the form αA + βI.

  1. 145A + 54I
  2. 145A − 54I
  3. 54A + 145I
  4. 27A + 10I
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Cayley–Hamilton reduces every power of A to a linear combination of A and I for a 2 × 2 matrix, because A² = tr(A)·A − det(A)·I. Higher powers are then obtained by repeatedly substituting this relation — each step is pure algebra, no matrix multiplication of full powers required.

Step by step.

  1. tr(A) = 5, det(A) = 4 − 6 = −2, so the characteristic polynomial is λ² − 5λ − 2 and A² = 5A + 2I.
  2. A³ = A·A² = 5A² + 2A = 5(5A + 2I) + 2A = 27A + 10I.
  3. A⁴ = A·A³ = 27A² + 10A = 27(5A + 2I) + 10A = 135A + 54I + 10A = 145A + 54I.
  4. Numerical check: A² = [[7, 10], [15, 22]], A⁴ = (A²)² = [[199, 290], [435, 634]]. And 145A + 54I = [[145 + 54, 290], [435, 580 + 54]] = [[199, 290], [435, 634]]. They agree.

Answer: Option (A) — A⁴ = 145A + 54I

Common misconception. Stopping one step early and giving A³, which is option (D). Option (C) simply swaps the two coefficients, which the numerical check rules out immediately.

EM-U2-Q17MSQeasy

Let A = [[1, 2], [3, 4]]. Which statements are TRUE?

  1. The characteristic equation is λ² − 5λ − 2 = 0.
  2. A⁻¹ = ½(A − 5I)
  3. A⁻¹ = ½(5I − A)
  4. A is singular.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A) and (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. The 2 × 2 characteristic polynomial is λ² − tr(A)λ + det(A), and Cayley–Hamilton turns it into A⁻¹ = (tr(A)I − A)/det(A) when the determinant is nonzero.

Step by step.

  1. tr(A) = 5, det(A) = 4 − 6 = −2, so the polynomial is λ² − 5λ − 2. True.
  2. A² − 5A − 2I = 0. Multiply by A⁻¹: A − 5I − 2A⁻¹ = 0, so 2A⁻¹ = A − 5I and A⁻¹ = ½(A − 5I). True.
  3. ½(5I − A) = −A⁻¹, the negative of the inverse. Check: A⁻¹ = [[−2, 1], [1.5, −0.5]] while ½(5I − A) = ½[[4, −2], [−3, 1]] = [[2, −1], [−1.5, 0.5]]. False.
  4. det(A) = −2 ≠ 0, so A is invertible. False.

Answer: (A) and (B)

Common misconception. Losing the minus sign while rearranging A − 5I − 2A⁻¹ = 0. Isolate the inverse first and then divide by its coefficient — the sign of det(A) enters through the constant term, not through the rearrangement.

EM-U2-Q23NATmedium

Let A = [[1, 2], [3, 4]]. Find the (2, 2) entry of A³.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 118

Detailed solution

Concept. Cayley–Hamilton reduces A³ to a linear combination of A and I. Reading a single entry off that combination is much cheaper than cubing the matrix, and the entry depends only on the diagonal of A once the reduction is done.

Step by step.

  1. tr(A) = 5, det(A) = 4 − 6 = −2, so A² = 5A + 2I.
  2. A³ = A·A² = 5A² + 2A = 5(5A + 2I) + 2A = 27A + 10I.
  3. The (2, 2) entry of 27A + 10I is 27 × a₂₂ + 10 = 27 × 4 + 10 = 108 + 10 = 118.
  4. Verify by direct multiplication: A² = [[7, 10], [15, 22]] and A³ = A·A² = [[1·7 + 2·15, 1·10 + 2·22], [3·7 + 4·15, 3·10 + 4·22]] = [[37, 54], [81, 118]]. The (2, 2) entry is 118. Agreement.

Answer: 118

Common misconception. Answering 27 × 4 = 108 by keeping only the A-term and dropping the 10I. The identity term contributes to every diagonal entry.

EM-U2-Q30MCQeasy

The Cayley–Hamilton theorem states that every square matrix

  1. satisfies its own characteristic equation.
  2. is similar to a diagonal matrix.
  3. has distinct eigenvalues.
  4. commutes with its transpose.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Cayley–Hamilton: if p(λ) = det(λI − A) is the characteristic polynomial of A, then p(A) = 0. It is an identity in the matrix ring, not a statement about eigenvalues alone.

Step by step.

  1. Let p(λ) = λⁿ + c₁λⁿ⁻¹ + … + cₙ.
  2. Then Aⁿ + c₁Aⁿ⁻¹ + … + cₙI = 0, the zero matrix.
  3. This lets every power Aᵏ with k ≥ n be reduced to a combination of I, A, …, Aⁿ⁻¹.

Final answer. Option (A) — it satisfies its own characteristic equation.

Common trap. Reading the theorem as “the eigenvalues satisfy the characteristic equation”, which is only the definition of an eigenvalue. The theorem's content is that the matrix itself, substituted into its own polynomial, gives the zero matrix.

EM-U2-Q35MCQmedium

Let A = [[2, 1], [0, 3]]. Using the Cayley–Hamilton theorem, A⁻¹ equals

  1. (1/6)·[[3, −1], [0, 2]]
  2. (1/6)·[[2, −1], [0, 3]]
  3. [[3, −1], [0, 2]]
  4. (1/5)·[[3, −1], [0, 2]]
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Cayley–Hamilton gives A² − (tr A)A + (det A)I = 0 for a 2 × 2 matrix. Multiplying by A⁻¹ turns it into an explicit formula, A⁻¹ = ((tr A)I − A)/det A, with no elimination needed.

Step by step.

  1. Here tr A = 5 and det A = 6, so A² − 5A + 6I = 0.
  2. Multiply by A⁻¹: A − 5I + 6A⁻¹ = 0.
  3. Hence A⁻¹ = (5I − A)/6 = ([[5, 0], [0, 5]] − [[2, 1], [0, 3]])/6 = (1/6)[[3, −1], [0, 2]].

Final answer. Option (A) — (1/6)·[[3, −1], [0, 2]].

Common trap. Dropping the 1/det A factor, which gives option (C), or dividing by the trace instead of the determinant. Multiplying the answer back by A is the fastest check: (1/6)[[2,1],[0,3]]·[[3,−1],[0,2]] = I.

EM-U2-Q39MCQhard

A 3 × 3 matrix has characteristic polynomial p(λ) = λ³ − 2λ² + λ − 5. Then A³ equals

  1. 2A² − A + 5I
  2. 2A² + A − 5I
  3. A² − 2A + 5I
  4. 5I − 2A² + A
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Cayley–Hamilton says p(A) = 0. Solving that matrix equation for the highest power is the standard way to write any high power of A in terms of I, A, A².

Step by step.

  1. p(A) = A³ − 2A² + A − 5I = 0.
  2. Move every other term to the right: A³ = 2A² − A + 5I.
  3. Every later power is then reducible as well, for instance A⁴ = 2A³ − A² + 5A.

Final answer. Option (A) — A³ = 2A² − A + 5I.

Common trap. Copying the coefficients with their original signs. Moving a term across the equals sign flips it, so −2A² becomes +2A² and +A becomes −A; option (B) is exactly that sign slip.

EM-U2-Q43MSQeasy

Which of the following statements about the Cayley–Hamilton theorem are TRUE?

  1. Every square matrix satisfies its own characteristic equation.
  2. For an invertible matrix it expresses A⁻¹ as a polynomial in A.
  3. It implies that Aⁿ is a linear combination of I, A, …, Aⁿ⁻¹.
  4. It holds only for diagonalizable matrices.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Cayley–Hamilton is an identity valid for every square matrix over any commutative ring — no diagonalizability, no symmetry, no distinctness of eigenvalues is needed.

Step by step.

  1. Write p(λ) = λⁿ + c₁λⁿ⁻¹ + … + cₙ; the theorem gives Aⁿ + c₁Aⁿ⁻¹ + … + cₙI = 0 — statement A.
  2. If cₙ = (−1)ⁿ det A ≠ 0, multiply by A⁻¹ and solve for A⁻¹ — statement B.
  3. Rearranging the same identity gives Aⁿ = −c₁Aⁿ⁻¹ − … − cₙI, and induction reduces every higher power — statement C.
  4. The identity was never derived from a diagonal form, so statement D is false; the theorem also holds for defective matrices such as [[1, 1], [0, 1]].

Final answer. Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common trap. Believing the theorem needs a diagonal form because that is how it is usually first illustrated. The proof only needs the adjugate identity adj(λI − A)(λI − A) = det(λI − A)I.

EM-U2-Q48NATmedium

Let A = [[1, 2], [3, 4]]. The Cayley–Hamilton theorem gives A² = aA + bI. The value of a + b is ____.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 7

Detailed solution

Concept. Cayley–Hamilton for a 2 × 2 matrix reads A² − (tr A)A + (det A)I = 0, so the coefficients a and b are the trace and the determinant respectively — with the sign of b coming from the constant term +det A.

Step by step.

  1. tr A = 1 + 4 = 5 and det A = 1·4 − 2·3 = −2.
  2. The characteristic equation is λ² − 5λ − 2 = 0, so A² − 5A − 2I = 0.
  3. Hence A² = 5A + 2I, giving a = 5 and b = 2.
  4. Therefore a + b = 7.

Final answer. 7

Common trap. Taking b = det A = −2 and answering 3. The constant term of λ² − (tr A)λ + det A is +det A, so the sign flips when the equation is solved for A².

EM-U2-Q55MCQeasy

For A = [[4, 3], [1, 2]], the Cayley–Hamilton theorem gives

  1. A² = 6A + 5I
  2. A² = 5A − 6I
  3. A² = 6I − 5A
  4. A² = 6A − 5I
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Cayley–Hamilton: a matrix satisfies its own characteristic equation. For a 2 × 2 matrix that equation is A² − (tr A)A + (det A)I = 0, which rearranges into a formula for A².

Step by step.

  1. Trace: tr A = 4 + 2 = 6; determinant: det A = 8 − 3 = 5.
  2. Characteristic equation: λ² − 6λ + 5 = 0, so A² − 6A + 5I = 0.
  3. Therefore A² = 6A − 5I. Check: A² = [[19, 18], [6, 7]] and 6A − 5I = [[24 − 5, 18], [6, 12 − 5]] = [[19, 18], [6, 7]].

Answer: Option (D): A² = 6A − 5I.

Common misconception. Swapping the signs of the two terms, which gives A² = 6A + 5I, or moving the constant to the wrong side and getting A² = 6I − 5A.

Takeaway. Memorise the rearrangement A² = (tr A)A − (det A)I for 2 × 2 matrices; it saves computing A² by hand.

EM-U2-Q59MCQmedium

A 2 × 2 real matrix A has tr(A) = 4 and det(A) = 3. Using the Cayley–Hamilton theorem, A³ equals

  1. 13A + 12I
  2. 12A − 13I
  3. 13A − 12I
  4. 16A − 12I
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Cayley–Hamilton reduces every power of A to a linear combination of A and I. Reducing A² first and then multiplying by A gives the next power without any matrix multiplication of numbers.

Step by step.

  1. Characteristic equation: A² − 4A + 3I = 0, so A² = 4A − 3I.
  2. Multiply by A: A³ = 4A² − 3A.
  3. Substitute A²: A³ = 4(4A − 3I) − 3A = 16A − 12I − 3A = 13A − 12I.
  4. Sanity check with a matrix having these invariants, A = [[3, 0], [0, 1]]: A³ = [[27, 0], [0, 1]] and 13A − 12I = [[39 − 12, 0], [0, 13 − 12]] = [[27, 0], [0, 1]].

Answer: Option (C): A³ = 13A − 12I.

Common misconception. Writing A³ = 4A² + 3A by dropping the sign of the constant term, or stopping at 16A − 12I after forgetting to subtract 3A.

Takeaway. For powers beyond the characteristic degree, reduce and multiply: A² = (tr A)A − (det A)I, then A³ = A·A² with A² already replaced.

EM-U2-Q70MCQmedium

A 2 × 2 real matrix A has characteristic polynomial p(λ) = λ² − 3λ + 1. Using the Cayley–Hamilton theorem, A⁻¹ equals

  1. A − 3I
  2. 3A − I
  3. I − 3A
  4. 3I − A
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Cayley–Hamilton gives A² − 3A + I = 0. Multiplying by A⁻¹ (legal because the constant term 1 is non-zero, so det A = 1 ≠ 0) turns the relation into one that isolates A⁻¹.

Step by step.

  1. Cayley–Hamilton: A² − 3A + I = 0.
  2. Multiply by A⁻¹: A − 3I + A⁻¹ = 0.
  3. Therefore A⁻¹ = 3I − A.
  4. Check with A = [[1, 0], [0, 1]]-type reasoning: any matrix with p(λ) = λ² − 3λ + 1 has det A = 1, and A(3I − A) = 3A − A² = 3A − (3A − I) = I.

Answer: Option (D): A⁻¹ = 3I − A.

Common misconception. Solving for A instead of A⁻¹, or dropping the sign of the middle term and writing A⁻¹ = A − 3I.

Takeaway. The identity A⁻¹ = −(A + c₁I)/c₀ for p(λ) = λ² + c₁λ + c₀ inverts a 2 × 2 matrix with no adjugate.

EM-U2-Q72MSQmedium

Let A = [[2, 1], [1, 2]]. Which of the following statements are TRUE?

  1. A² = 4A − 3I.
  2. A⁻¹ = (4I − A)/3.
  3. A³ = 13A − 12I.
  4. A² + 4A + 3I = 0.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Cayley–Hamilton for A gives A² − 4A + 3I = 0, and every other statement is a consequence: rearrange it for A², multiply by A⁻¹ for the inverse, multiply by A for A³.

Step by step.

  1. Trace 4 and determinant 3 give A² − 4A + 3I = 0, i.e. A² = 4A − 3I — statement A is TRUE.
  2. Multiply by A⁻¹: A − 4I + 3A⁻¹ = 0, so A⁻¹ = (4I − A)/3 — statement B is TRUE.
  3. A³ = A·A² = A(4A − 3I) = 4A² − 3A = 4(4A − 3I) − 3A = 13A − 12I — statement C is TRUE.
  4. Statement D has the wrong signs: the relation is A² − 4A + 3I = 0, not A² + 4A + 3I = 0 — FALSE. Check with A = I: 1 − 4 + 3 = 0 but 1 + 4 + 3 = 8 ≠ 0.

Answer: Statements (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Sign slips on the middle coefficient. The characteristic equation is λ² − (tr)λ + det = 0, so the trace enters negatively and the determinant positively.

Takeaway. One Cayley–Hamilton relation answers every power and inverse question for a 2 × 2 matrix. Verify any claimed relation by substituting a simple matrix with the same invariants.

Diagonalization 18
EM-U2-Q06MCQeasy

Which statement is TRUE?

  1. Every real 3 × 3 matrix with three distinct real eigenvalues is diagonalizable over ℝ.
  2. A real matrix is diagonalizable over ℝ if and only if it is symmetric.
  3. A matrix with a repeated eigenvalue is never diagonalizable.
  4. A real n × n matrix is diagonalizable over ℝ if and only if AᵀA = AAᵀ.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Diagonalizability is about having enough eigenvectors: A is diagonalizable over ℝ exactly when the geometric multiplicity of every real eigenvalue equals its algebraic multiplicity and the eigenvalues are all real. Distinct eigenvalues are the easiest sufficient condition, because eigenvectors belonging to distinct eigenvalues are automatically linearly independent.

Step by step.

  1. Three distinct real eigenvalues give three linearly independent eigenvectors in ℝ³, hence a basis of eigenvectors, hence A = PDP⁻¹. So the first statement is true.
  2. "Symmetric implies diagonalizable" is true (the spectral theorem), but the converse fails: [[1, 1], [0, 2]] is diagonalizable with distinct eigenvalues and is not symmetric. So (B) is false.
  3. I has the repeated eigenvalue 1 and is already diagonal, so (C) is false.
  4. AᵀA = AAᵀ characterises normal matrices, which are unitarily diagonalizable over ℂ — not a characterisation of real diagonalizability. So (D) is false.

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Collapsing "symmetric implies diagonalizable" into an "if and only if". Symmetry is a strong extra hypothesis, not a necessary condition; most diagonalizable matrices are not symmetric.

EM-U2-Q11MCQmedium

Let A = [[1, 1], [0, 1]]. Which statement is TRUE?

  1. 1 has algebraic multiplicity 2 and geometric multiplicity 1, so A is not diagonalizable.
  2. A is diagonalizable because both its eigenvalues are real.
  3. A is not diagonalizable because its eigenvalues are complex.
  4. A is diagonalizable because A = I + N with N nilpotent.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. A matrix is diagonalizable exactly when, for every eigenvalue, the geometric multiplicity (the dimension of its eigenspace) equals the algebraic multiplicity (its multiplicity as a root of the characteristic polynomial). The geometric multiplicity is the nullity of A − λI, computed as n − rank(A − λI).

Step by step.

  1. A is upper triangular, so det(A − λI) = (1 − λ)²: the only eigenvalue is 1, with algebraic multiplicity 2.
  2. A − I = [[0, 1], [0, 0]], which has rank 1.
  3. Geometric multiplicity = nullity(A − I) = 2 − 1 = 1. Equivalently, the eigenspace is span{(1, 0)}, one-dimensional.
  4. 1 < 2, so there is no basis of eigenvectors and A is not diagonalizable. It is a single Jordan block J₂(1).

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Reading "real eigenvalues" as "diagonalizable", which is option (B). Real eigenvalues suffice only when they are distinct. Option (D) is the subtler trap: A = I + N with N² = 0 is correct as an identity, but a nilpotent perturbation does not add eigenvectors, so it does not make the matrix diagonalizable.

EM-U2-Q12MCQmedium

A 3 × 3 real matrix A is diagonalizable and has eigenvalues 1, 1, 2. Find rank(A − I).

  1. 0
  2. 1
  3. 2
  4. 3
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Diagonalizability lets you replace A by a similar diagonal matrix D without changing any rank: rank(A − I) = rank(D − I). Rank–nullity then converts the answer into the geometric multiplicity of 1, which is what diagonalizability pins down.

Step by step.

  1. Similarity: A = PDP⁻¹ with D = diag(1, 1, 2).
  2. Then A − I = P(D − I)P⁻¹ and D − I = diag(0, 0, 1).
  3. rank(diag(0, 0, 1)) = 1, so rank(A − I) = 1.
  4. Independent check via nullity: since A is diagonalizable, the geometric multiplicity of λ = 1 equals its algebraic multiplicity, 2. So nullity(A − I) = 2 and rank(A − I) = 3 − 2 = 1.

Answer: Option (B) — 1

Common misconception. Answering 2 from the algebraic multiplicity of 1, or answering 0 from the fact that 1 is an eigenvalue and so A − I is singular. Singular only tells you the rank is at most 2; diagonalizability is what pins it to exactly 1.

EM-U2-Q14MCQhard

Let A = [[2, 1], [1, 2]]. Find the (1, 1) entry of A¹⁰.

  1. 29524
  2. 29525
  3. 59049
  4. 1024
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Diagonalize once and every power follows: if A = PDP⁻¹ then Aⁿ = PDⁿP⁻¹. Equivalently, expand in the eigenvectors. Here the two eigenvalues are 3 and 1, so Aⁿ is a combination of 3ⁿ and 1ⁿ — the second eigenvalue is easy to forget and is exactly what makes the trap answer 3¹⁰ wrong.

Step by step.

  1. tr(A) = 4, det(A) = 4 − 1 = 3, so λ² − 4λ + 3 = (λ − 3)(λ − 1): eigenvalues 3 and 1.
  2. Eigenvectors: λ = 3 gives (1, 1); λ = 1 gives (1, −1).
  3. Write A = 2I + B with B = [[0, 1], [1, 0]] and B² = I. Since 2I and B commute, the binomial theorem applies: Aⁿ = Σ C(n, k) 2ⁿ⁻ᵏ Bᵏ.
  4. Split by the parity of k: Aⁿ = ((3ⁿ + 1)/2)I + ((3ⁿ − 1)/2)B.
  5. Check on n = 1: ((3 + 1)/2)I + ((3 − 1)/2)B = 2I + B = A. Correct.
  6. At n = 10: 3¹⁰ = 59049, so the (1, 1) entry is (59049 + 1)/2 = 29525.

Answer: Option (B) — 29525

Common misconception. Using only the dominant eigenvalue and answering 3¹⁰ = 59049, option (C). The eigenvalue 1 contributes a constant offset of 1/2 to the diagonal, and dropping it doubles the answer.

EM-U2-Q18MSQmedium

Which of the following real matrices are diagonalizable over ℝ?

  1. [[1, 0], [0, 2]]
  2. [[1, 1], [0, 1]]
  3. [[0, −1], [1, 0]]
  4. [[2, 1], [1, 2]]
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Over ℝ, diagonalizability needs two things at once: every eigenvalue must be real, and every eigenvalue must have geometric multiplicity equal to its algebraic multiplicity. Checking both is the only reliable test; either alone is insufficient.

Step by step.

  1. [[1, 0], [0, 2]] is already diagonal, with eigenvalues 1 and 2. Diagonalizable.
  2. [[1, 1], [0, 1]] has the single eigenvalue 1 with algebraic multiplicity 2; A − I = [[0, 1], [0, 0]] has rank 1, so the geometric multiplicity is 1. Not diagonalizable.
  3. [[0, −1], [1, 0]] has characteristic polynomial λ² + 1, so its eigenvalues are i and −i. It is diagonalizable over ℂ but not over ℝ, since no real eigenvector exists — it is a quarter-turn. Not diagonalizable over ℝ.
  4. [[2, 1], [1, 2]] is symmetric, so by the spectral theorem it is orthogonally diagonalizable; directly, λ² − 4λ + 3 gives distinct real eigenvalues 3 and 1. Diagonalizable.

Answer: (A) and (D)

Common misconception. Marking (C) because it is diagonalizable over ℂ. The field matters: a rotation has no real eigenvectors at all, so over ℝ it fails outright.

EM-U2-Q20MSQhard

Let A = [[0, 1, 0], [0, 0, 1], [1, 0, 0]]. Which statements are TRUE?

  1. A is diagonalizable over ℂ.
  2. A is diagonalizable over ℝ.
  3. tr(A) = 0
  4. A³ = I
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. A is the permutation matrix of the 3-cycle (1 2 3), so it acts by permuting the standard basis. Its characteristic polynomial is λ³ − 1, whose roots are the three distinct cube roots of unity — distinct roots are exactly what guarantees diagonalizability over the field containing them.

Step by step.

  1. det(A − λI) = −(λ³ − 1), so the eigenvalues are 1, ω = e^{2πi/3} and ω². They are pairwise distinct. Diagonalizable over ℂ: True.
  2. Two of the eigenvalues are non-real, and a real matrix with a non-real eigenvalue has no real eigenvector for it, so only one real eigenvector exists in ℝ³. Not diagonalizable over ℝ: False.
  3. tr(A) = 0 + 0 + 0 = 0 from the diagonal, and also 1 + ω + ω² = 0 from the spectrum. True.
  4. A cyclically shifts the basis, so A³ = I; equivalently Cayley–Hamilton gives A³ − I = 0. True.

Answer: (A), (C) and (D)

Common misconception. Marking (B) because A satisfies A³ = I and "everything about it looks real". The matrix has real entries, but its eigenvectors do not: over ℝ a rotation by 120° fixes only one line.

EM-U2-Q24NATmedium

Let A = [[1, 1], [0, 2]]. Find the (1, 2) entry of A¹⁰.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 1023

Detailed solution

Concept. A triangular matrix is not diagonal, but its powers are easy once you notice that the diagonal entries decouple and the off-diagonal entry is driven by a geometric sum. Equivalently, A is diagonalizable with distinct eigenvalues 1 and 2, and Aⁿ = PDⁿP⁻¹ gives the same closed form.

Step by step.

  1. Write A = [[1, 0], [0, 2]] + [[0, 1], [0, 0]] = D + N with N² = 0 and DN = N, so D and N commute and the binomial theorem applies.
  2. Aⁿ = Dⁿ + nDⁿ⁻¹N, since all terms with N² or higher vanish.
  3. Dⁿ = diag(1, 2ⁿ) and nDⁿ⁻¹N = [[0, n·2ⁿ⁻¹], [0, 0]], so Aⁿ = [[1, n·2ⁿ⁻¹], [0, 2ⁿ]].
  4. Check at n = 2: [[1, 2·2], [0, 4]] = [[1, 4], [0, 4]], and A² = [[1, 1 + 2], [0, 4]] = [[1, 3], [0, 4]]. The closed form gives 2·2¹ = 4, so refine it: Aⁿ = [[1, 2ⁿ − 1], [0, 2ⁿ]]. Check again: n = 2 gives 3. Correct.
  5. At n = 10: the (1, 2) entry is 2¹⁰ − 1 = 1024 − 1 = 1023.

Answer: 1023

Common misconception. Answering 2¹⁰ = 1024 from the diagonal alone, or 10 × 2⁹ = 5120 from the naive binomial term. The correct off-diagonal is the geometric sum 1 + 2 + … + 2⁹ = 2¹⁰ − 1.

EM-U2-Q29MCQeasy

A real n × n matrix is diagonalizable over ℝ if and only if

  1. it is invertible.
  2. it has n linearly independent eigenvectors.
  3. it has n distinct eigenvalues.
  4. it is symmetric.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Diagonalizability is exactly the existence of a basis of eigenvectors. Writing A = PDP⁻¹ needs P to be invertible, and P is built from eigenvectors — so the columns of P must be n linearly independent eigenvectors.

Step by step.

  1. If A = PDP⁻¹ with D diagonal, the columns of P are eigenvectors and P is invertible, so they are linearly independent.
  2. Conversely, n linearly independent eigenvectors fill an invertible P and give A = PDP⁻¹.
  3. Having n distinct eigenvalues is sufficient but not necessary; being symmetric is sufficient but not necessary; invertibility is neither.

Final answer. Option (B) — n linearly independent eigenvectors.

Common trap. Confusing the sufficient condition (distinct eigenvalues) with the exact criterion. A repeated eigenvalue still allows diagonalization provided its eigenspace is large enough; for example the identity matrix is diagonal though its only eigenvalue is repeated n times.

EM-U2-Q34MCQmedium

Let A = [[1, 1], [0, 1]]. Which statement is TRUE?

  1. A is diagonalizable because it is upper triangular.
  2. A has the repeated eigenvalue 1 and is not diagonalizable.
  3. A is not diagonalizable because it is singular.
  4. A has two distinct eigenvalues.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. A triangular matrix has its diagonal entries as eigenvalues, so here λ = 1 twice. Diagonalizability then depends on the size of the eigenspace: A is diagonalizable if and only if the geometric multiplicity of each eigenvalue equals its algebraic multiplicity.

Step by step.

  1. The only eigenvalue is λ = 1, with algebraic multiplicity 2.
  2. Solve (A − I)x = 0: A − I = [[0, 1], [0, 0]], so x₂ = 0 and x = (1, 0)ᵗ only.
  3. The eigenspace is one-dimensional, so the geometric multiplicity is 1 < 2 and no basis of eigenvectors exists.

Final answer. Option (B) — repeated eigenvalue 1, not diagonalizable.

Common trap. Assuming triangular always means diagonalizable. Triangular guarantees that the eigenvalues are visible on the diagonal, not that enough eigenvectors exist; the defect shows up precisely in the off-diagonal entry.

EM-U2-Q38MCQhard

A real 3 × 3 matrix has three distinct eigenvalues, one of which is 2 + i. Then

  1. A is diagonalizable over ℝ.
  2. A is diagonalizable over ℂ but not over ℝ.
  3. A is not diagonalizable over either field.
  4. A cannot have a non-real eigenvalue.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Three distinct eigenvalues always give three linearly independent eigenvectors, so the matrix is diagonalizable over ℂ. But a real matrix with a non-real eigenvalue λ has the conjugate λ̄ as well, and the eigenvectors are non-real, so no real eigenbasis exists.

Step by step.

  1. Real coefficients force the conjugate 2 − i to be an eigenvalue too, so the three eigenvalues are 2 + i, 2 − i and one real number.
  2. They are distinct, hence the three eigenvectors are linearly independent over ℂ and A is diagonalizable over ℂ.
  3. The eigenvectors belonging to 2 ± i are not real multiples of real vectors, so a real basis of eigenvectors cannot be assembled.
  4. Over ℝ the matrix is only block-diagonalizable, into 1 × 1 and 2 × 2 rotation-scaling blocks.

Final answer. Option (B) — diagonalizable over ℂ only.

Common trap. Believing that a real matrix can only have real eigenvalues, or that “diagonalizable” is field-independent. The field matters: [[0, −1], [1, 0]] is diagonalizable over ℂ and not over ℝ.

EM-U2-Q44MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about diagonalizability over ℝ are TRUE?

  1. A real n × n matrix is diagonalizable if and only if it has n linearly independent eigenvectors.
  2. A matrix with n distinct eigenvalues is diagonalizable.
  3. A matrix with a repeated eigenvalue is diagonalizable only if it is symmetric.
  4. Every invertible matrix is diagonalizable.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A) and (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. The exact criterion is an eigenbasis: n linearly independent eigenvectors. Distinct eigenvalues guarantee one eigenvector per eigenvalue and hence a basis; repeated eigenvalues and invertibility say nothing on their own.

Step by step.

  1. Statements A and B are the criterion and its most common sufficient condition.
  2. For C, take A = [[2, 0, 0], [0, 2, 0], [1, 0, 3]]: the eigenvalue 2 is repeated, nullity(A − 2I) = 2 so it is diagonalizable, yet A is not symmetric.
  3. For D, [[1, 1], [0, 1]] is invertible with determinant 1 but has only a one-dimensional eigenspace.

Final answer. Options (A) and (B).

Common trap. Using symmetry as if it were necessary for diagonalization. Symmetry is sufficient (indeed it gives an orthonormal eigenbasis), but non-symmetric matrices with a repeated eigenvalue are diagonalizable whenever each eigenspace is big enough.

EM-U2-Q49NATmedium

For A = [[2, 1], [0, 2]], the dimension of the eigenspace of A corresponding to λ = 2 is ____.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 1

Detailed solution

Concept. The eigenspace for λ is the null space of A − λI, and its dimension is nullity(A − λI) = n − rank(A − λI). That geometric multiplicity is what decides diagonalizability.

Step by step.

  1. A − 2I = [[0, 1], [0, 0]].
  2. This matrix has rank 1, so its null space has dimension 2 − 1 = 1.
  3. Concretely the null space is spanned by (1, 0)ᵗ only, so the geometric multiplicity is 1 while the algebraic multiplicity is 2 — the matrix is defective.

Final answer. 1

Common trap. Reporting 2 from the algebraic multiplicity, since λ = 2 is a double root of (λ − 2)². A repeated eigenvalue only produces a two-dimensional eigenspace when the matrix is diagonalizable at that eigenvalue.

EM-U2-Q53MCQeasy

A real 3 × 3 matrix A has three distinct eigenvalues 1, 2 and 5. Which statement is certainly TRUE?

  1. A is symmetric.
  2. A is diagonalizable over ℝ: some invertible real P gives P⁻¹AP = diag(1, 2, 5).
  3. A is singular.
  4. A has only two linearly independent eigenvectors.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Eigenvectors belonging to distinct eigenvalues are linearly independent. With n distinct eigenvalues a real n × n matrix therefore owns n independent eigenvectors, which is exactly the condition for diagonalizability.

Step by step.

  1. Distinct eigenvalues give linearly independent eigenvectors: x₁, x₂, x₃ for λ = 1, 2, 5 are independent.
  2. Three independent eigenvectors in ℝ³ form a basis, so P = [x₁ x₂ x₃] is invertible and P⁻¹AP = diag(1, 2, 5).
  3. The other options are not forced: A need not be symmetric (only real matrices with an orthonormal eigenbasis are), and det A = 1·2·5 = 10 ≠ 0, so A is certainly not singular.

Answer: Option (B): distinct real eigenvalues make A diagonalizable over ℝ.

Common misconception. Believing that real eigenvalues are enough. A repeated eigenvalue with a short eigenspace destroys diagonalizability; distinctness is the hypothesis that rules that out.

Takeaway. Distinct eigenvalues ⇒ diagonalizable. The converse fails, so a repeated eigenvalue only means the eigenspace dimensions must be checked.

EM-U2-Q57MSQmedium

Let A = [[1, 2, 0], [0, 1, 0], [0, 0, 3]]. Which of the following statements are TRUE?

  1. The eigenvalues of A are 1, 1 and 3.
  2. The dimension of the eigenspace of A for λ = 1 is 2.
  3. A is not diagonalizable over ℝ.
  4. A is diagonalizable over ℝ because it is upper triangular.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. A triangular matrix displays its eigenvalues on the diagonal, but triangular form says nothing about diagonalizability. That needs the geometric multiplicity of every repeated eigenvalue to equal its algebraic multiplicity, i.e. nullity(A − λI) = algebraic multiplicity.

Step by step.

  1. Upper triangular, so the eigenvalues are the diagonal entries 1, 1, 3 — statement A is TRUE.
  2. For λ = 1: A − I = [[0, 2, 0], [0, 0, 0], [0, 0, 2]], which has two non-zero rows, so rank(A − I) = 2 and nullity = 3 − 2 = 1.
  3. The algebraic multiplicity of λ = 1 is 2 but its geometric multiplicity is 1, so the eigenspaces cannot supply three independent eigenvectors — statement B is FALSE and statement C is TRUE.
  4. Upper triangularity only reveals the eigenvalues; the (1, 2) entry 2 blocks the eigenspace — statement D is FALSE.

Answer: Statements (A) and (C).

Common misconception. Treating a triangular matrix as automatically diagonalizable. Diagonal matrices are triangular, but the converse fails as soon as a repeated diagonal entry has an off-diagonal coupling.

Takeaway. For a repeated eigenvalue compute nullity(A − λI) every time. Triangular form answers the eigenvalue question and nothing else.

EM-U2-Q61MCQmedium

A real 2 × 2 matrix A satisfies A² = A. Which statement is TRUE?

  1. A must be the identity matrix or the zero matrix.
  2. A is diagonalizable over ℝ and its eigenvalues are only 0 and 1.
  3. A has 2 as an eigenvalue.
  4. A is not diagonalizable unless A = I.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. If a polynomial p annihilates A then the minimal polynomial of A divides p. Here A² − A = 0, i.e. A(A − I) = 0, so the minimal polynomial divides λ(λ − 1), whose roots are distinct — and distinct roots of the minimal polynomial are exactly what diagonalizability means.

Step by step.

  1. Every eigenvalue of A satisfies λ² = λ, so λ ∈ {0, 1}.
  2. The minimal polynomial divides λ(λ − 1) and has no repeated factor, so A is diagonalizable over ℝ with 0 and 1 as its only possible eigenvalues.
  3. A need not be I or 0: A = [[1, 0], [0, 0]] satisfies A² = A and is neither.
  4. No eigenvalue can be 2, since 2² ≠ 2.

Answer: Option (B).

Common misconception. Concluding A = I or 0 from A² = A, which confuses the scalar equation x² = x with the matrix equation: a projection onto a line is a counterexample.

Takeaway. An annihilating polynomial with distinct roots proves diagonalizability outright. Check the roots of the minimal polynomial before hunting for eigenvectors.

EM-U2-Q64MSQhard

Let A be a real n × n matrix. Which of the following statements are TRUE?

  1. If A is diagonalizable over ℝ, then every eigenvalue of A is real.
  2. A real symmetric matrix is diagonalizable over ℝ by an orthogonal matrix.
  3. If A has n linearly independent eigenvectors, then A is diagonalizable.
  4. If A² is diagonalizable, then A is diagonalizable.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Diagonalizability over ℝ means P⁻¹AP = D with P and D real. The eigenvalues of A are then the diagonal entries of D, so they are real; conversely n independent eigenvectors are exactly the columns of such a P.

Step by step.

  1. Statement A is TRUE: the diagonal entries of the real diagonal form D are the eigenvalues, so all of them are real. A matrix with a non-real pair cannot have a real diagonal form.
  2. Statement B is TRUE: the spectral theorem for real symmetric matrices gives an orthonormal eigenbasis, so QᵀAQ = D with Q orthogonal.
  3. Statement C is TRUE: those n eigenvectors are a basis, so P⁻¹AP is diagonal.
  4. Statement D is FALSE: A = [[0, 1], [0, 0]] has A² = 0, which is diagonal, yet A itself has only a one-dimensional eigenspace.

Answer: Statements (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Accepting D because squaring seems to preserve structure. Squaring destroys information: a non-zero nilpotent matrix squares to zero, which is as diagonal as a matrix gets.

Takeaway. Two independent tests of diagonalizability: the minimal polynomial has distinct roots, or there are n independent eigenvectors. Both are one-way; no implication survives a polynomial in A.

EM-U2-Q68NATeasy

A real 3 × 3 matrix A has characteristic polynomial (λ − 2)²(λ − 5) and is diagonalizable over ℝ. The rank of A is ____.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 3

Detailed solution

Concept. A diagonalizable matrix is similar to a diagonal matrix holding its eigenvalues, and rank is preserved under similarity, so rank(A) equals the number of non-zero eigenvalues counted with geometric multiplicity.

Step by step.

  1. The eigenvalues are 2, 2 and 5, none of them zero, so det A = 2·2·5 = 20 ≠ 0.
  2. A matrix with non-zero determinant has full rank, so rank(A) = 3.
  3. Equivalently: A = PDP⁻¹ with D = diag(2, 2, 5) invertible, and rank is a similarity invariant, so rank A = rank D = 3.

Answer: 3

Common misconception. Answering 2 by counting the distinct eigenvalues, or 1 by subtracting the repeated eigenvalue. Rank counts non-zero eigenvalues with multiplicity, not distinct values.

Takeaway. Zero is the only eigenvalue that lowers the rank. Read invertibility straight off the spectrum before doing any elimination.

EM-U2-Q73MCQhard

A real 4 × 4 matrix A satisfies A² = −4I. Which statement is TRUE?

  1. A has eigenvalues 2, 2, −2 and −2.
  2. A is nilpotent.
  3. A has no real eigenvalue; it is diagonalizable over ℂ but not over ℝ.
  4. A = 2I.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. The polynomial t² + 4 annihilates A, so the minimal polynomial divides (t − 2i)(t + 2i). Distinct roots over ℂ give diagonalizability there; the absence of real roots forbids a real diagonal form.

Step by step.

  1. Any eigenvalue satisfies λ² = −4, so λ = ±2i and no eigenvalue is real.
  2. A matrix diagonalizable over ℝ would have real eigenvalues (they are the diagonal entries), so A is not diagonalizable over ℝ.
  3. The minimal polynomial divides t² + 4, whose two roots ±2i are distinct, so A is diagonalizable over ℂ.
  4. The other options fail: A = 2I gives A² = 4I, not −4I; a nilpotent matrix has all eigenvalues 0, contradicting λ² = −4.

Answer: Option (C).

Common misconception. Concluding that a matrix with no real eigenvalues is not diagonalizable at all. Over ℂ the two distinct eigenvalues do the job; it is only the real form that is impossible.

Takeaway. An annihilating polynomial with distinct roots settles diagonalizability over the field containing those roots. Read the field off the roots before answering.

Unit 3 — Calculus: Limits, Continuity & Differentiability 75
Indeterminate forms & L’Hôpital 21
EM-U3-Q01MCQeasy

The value of limx→0 sin(3x)/x is

  1. 1
  2. 1/3
  3. 3
  4. 0
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. At x = 0 both numerator and denominator vanish, so the expression is the indeterminate form 0/0. For sin(kx)/x the limit is k.

Step by step.

  1. Write sin(3x)/x = 3 · sin(3x)/(3x).
  2. Put u = 3x. As x → 0, also u → 0, and sin u / u → 1.
  3. So the limit is 3 · 1 = 3.

Answer: 3 — option (C).

Common misconception. Reading the coefficient as the reciprocal, or applying L'Hôpital's rule once and forgetting the chain-rule factor 3 from the derivative of sin 3x. Both give 1/3, which is option (B).

EM-U3-Q02MCQeasy

The value of limx→0 (1 − cos x)/x² is

  1. 1/2
  2. 1
  3. 0
  4. 2
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. The form is 0/0. Two standard routes: the half-angle identity 1 − cos x = 2 sin²(x/2), or one application of L'Hôpital's rule.

Step by step.

  1. Use 1 − cos x = 2 sin²(x/2), so (1 − cos x)/x² = 2 sin²(x/2)/x².
  2. Write it as (1/2) · [sin(x/2)/(x/2)]².
  3. Since sin u / u → 1, the bracket tends to 1 and the limit is 1/2.

Answer: 1/2 — option (A).

Common misconception. Stopping at the first derivative: L'Hôpital once gives sin x / (2x) → 1/2, but a second application on the wrong form gives cos x / 2 → 1/2 only by luck here — students who differentiate the numerator twice but the denominator once get 1. The quadratic vanishing of 1 − cos x is what makes the answer finite and equal to 1/2.

EM-U3-Q07MCQmedium

The value of limx→0 (ex − 1 − x)/x² is

  1. 1
  2. 1/2
  3. 0
  4. It does not exist
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. The numerator vanishes to second order at the origin, so one application of L'Hôpital's rule still leaves 0/0. The Taylor expansion ex = 1 + x + x²/2 + O(x³) settles it at once.

Step by step.

  1. First application: (ex − 1)/(2x), still 0/0 at x = 0.
  2. Second application: ex/2.
  3. At x = 0 this equals 1/2. Equivalently, ex − 1 − x = x²/2 + O(x³).

Answer: 1/2 — option (B).

Common misconception. Applying L'Hôpital once and stopping when the expression still reads 0/0 — that gives the invalid value 0 by substitution, or 1 if the numerator's derivative is taken as ex and the denominator's as 2x without re-checking. L'Hôpital may be repeated only while the quotient remains indeterminate.

EM-U3-Q12MCQhard

The value of limx→0 (1/x² − 1/sin²x) is

  1. −1/6
  2. −1/3
  3. 0
  4. 1/3
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. This is the indeterminate form ∞ − ∞. Combine over a common denominator to reach 0/0, then compare the series expansions of sin x and x.

Step by step.

  1. Combine: (sin²x − x²)/(x² sin²x).
  2. Expand: sin x = x − x³/6 + O(x⁵), so sin²x = x² − x⁴/3 + O(x⁶).
  3. Numerator = −x⁴/3 + O(x⁶); denominator = x⁴ + O(x⁶).
  4. Ratio → −1/3.

Answer: −1/3 — option (B).
