Common misconception. Treating ∞ − ∞ as 0, or cancelling 1/x² against 1/sin²x because sin x ≈ x. The two terms differ only at fourth order, so the leading behaviour cancels and the residual is −1/3, not 0.

EM-U3-Q16MSQeasy

Which of the following limits equal 1?

  1. limx→0 sin x / x
  2. limx→0 tan x / x
  3. limx→0 (ex − 1)/x
  4. limx→0 (1 − cos x)/x²
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Each option is a 0/0 form. The three standard limits sin x/x → 1, tan x/x → 1 and (ex − 1)/x → 1 all follow from the definition of the derivative at 0.

Step by step.

  1. (A) sin x / x → 1 is the fundamental trigonometric limit.
  2. (B) tan x / x = (sin x / x)(1/cos x) → 1 · 1 = 1.
  3. (C) (ex − 1)/x is the derivative of ex at 0, which is 1.
  4. (D) (1 − cos x)/x² → 1/2, not 1 — by the half-angle identity or two applications of L'Hôpital's rule.

Answer: (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Treating every 0/0 limit with a vanishing denominator as 1. The numerator of (D) vanishes to second order, which halves the answer; the order of vanishing decides the value, not the form alone.

EM-U3-Q21NATeasy

The value of limx→0 (e2x − 1)/x is ____.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 2

Detailed solution

Concept. The form is 0/0. Either recognise (eu − 1)/u → 1 with u = 2x, or differentiate once.

Step by step.

  1. Write (e2x − 1)/x = 2 · (e2x − 1)/(2x).
  2. With u = 2x → 0, (eu − 1)/u → 1.
  3. So the limit is 2 · 1 = 2. By L'Hôpital: 2e2x/1 → 2.

Answer: 2

Common misconception. Reporting 1 by pattern-matching the standard limit (ex − 1)/x → 1 without noticing the factor 2 in the exponent. The rate of the exponential is what survives in the limit.

EM-U3-Q24NATmedium

The value of limx→0⁺ x ln x is ____.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 0

Detailed solution

Concept. This is the indeterminate form 0 · ∞, which is not covered by L'Hôpital's rule until it is rewritten as a quotient: x ln x = ln x / (1/x), a ∞/∞ form.

Step by step.

  1. Rewrite: x ln x = ln x / x⁻¹, now −∞/∞ as x → 0⁺.
  2. L'Hôpital: derivative of ln x is 1/x; derivative of x⁻¹ is −x⁻².
  3. The quotient becomes (1/x)/(−1/x²) = −x → 0.
  4. Hence the limit is 0.

Answer: 0

Common misconception. Reporting −∞ from ln x → −∞ and forgetting that the x factor dominates it, or applying L'Hôpital's rule directly to a product. A product form must first be converted to a quotient; x beats ln x as x → 0⁺.

EM-U3B-Q01MCQeasy

The value of limx→0 tan(3x)/(2x) is

  1. 2/3
  2. 3
  3. 0
  4. 3/2
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Both numerator and denominator vanish at x = 0, so the expression is the indeterminate form 0/0. The standard limit limu→0 tan u / u = 1 settles it.

Step by step.

  1. Write tan(3x)/(2x) = (3/2) · tan(3x)/(3x).
  2. Put u = 3x. As x → 0, also u → 0, and tan u / u → 1.
  3. So the limit is (3/2) · 1 = 3/2.

Answer: 3/2 — option (D).

Common misconception. Reading the coefficient of x in the numerator as the answer gives 3 (option B); treating the factor 2 in the denominator as a multiplier of the numerator gives 6, and its reciprocal gives 2/3 (option A). The chain-rule factor from tan 3x must be divided by the coefficient in the denominator, not multiplied.

EM-U3B-Q02MCQeasy

The value of limx→0 sin(5x)/sin(2x) is

  1. 2/5
  2. 5/2
  3. 1
  4. 0
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Both numerator and denominator tend to 0, so the form is 0/0. Dividing numerator and denominator by the same factor x turns each piece into the standard limit limu→0 sin u / u = 1.

Step by step.

  1. Divide numerator and denominator by x: sin(5x)/sin(2x) = [sin(5x)/(5x)] · [2x/sin(2x)] · (5/2).
  2. As x → 0, the first factor tends to 1 and the second to 1.
  3. So the limit is 5/2.

Answer: 5/2 — option (B).

Common misconception. Inverting the ratio gives 2/5 (option A). A second trap is to cancel sin 5x against sin 2x as if they were like algebraic factors; they are not, and only the limiting ratio 5/2 is meaningful.

EM-U3B-Q07MCQmedium

The value of limx→0 (x − sin x)/x³ is

  1. 0
  2. 1/6
  3. 1/3
  4. 1/2
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. The form is 0/0. Applying L'Hôpital's rule three times, or expanding sin x about 0, both give the same answer; the expansion shows why the leading terms cancel.

Step by step.

  1. Expand: sin x = x − x³/6 + x⁵/120 − ….
  2. Subtract x: x − sin x = x³/6 − x⁵/120 + ….
  3. Divide by x³: 1/6 − x²/120 + …, so the limit is 1/6.
  4. Check by L'Hôpital: lim (1 − cos x)/(3x²), lim sin x/(6x), lim cos x/6, giving 1/6 at x = 0.

Answer: 1/6 — option (B).

Common misconception. Stopping at one application of L'Hôpital's rule gives (1 − cos x)/(3x²), which is still 0/0, and reading the surviving numerator as the answer gives 0 (option A). A second trap is to use the first neglected term of sin x alone as if it were the whole remainder, which halves or doubles the answer.

EM-U3B-Q11MCQmedium

The value of limx→0 (tan x − sin x)/x³ is

  1. 0
  2. 1/3
  3. 1/2
  4. 1
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. The form is 0/0. Factoring exposes the two standard limits sin x / x → 1 and tan x / x → 1 without any differentiation.

Step by step.

  1. tan x − sin x = sin x (1/cos x − 1) = sin x · (1 − cos x)/cos x.
  2. (tan x − sin x)/x³ = [sin x / x] · [(1 − cos x)/x²] · [1/cos x].
  3. As x → 0: sin x / x → 1, (1 − cos x)/x² → 1/2 and 1/cos x → 1, giving 1 · (1/2) · 1 = 1/2.

Answer: 1/2 — option (C).

Common misconception. Cancelling sin x to leave tan x − sin x ≈ 0 and reporting 0 (option A). The two functions agree only to first order; their difference is cubic, and the coefficient is the whole point of the question. Applying L'Hôpital's rule once and stopping yields another 0/0 form, which invites the wrong guess 1/3 (option B).

EM-U3B-Q16MSQeasy

Which of the following limits exist and are finite?

  1. limx→0 sin x / x
  2. limx→0 1/x²
  3. limx→0 (√(1 + x) − 1)/x
  4. limx→0 |x|/x
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. A two-sided limit exists exactly when the left-hand and right-hand limits exist and agree; it is finite when that common value is a real number. Each option must be tested on its own.

Step by step.

  1. (A) sin x / x → 1. Finite. Correct.
  2. (B) 1/x² → +∞ from both sides, so the limit is not finite — the function has a pole at 0. Incorrect.
  3. (C) Rationalising, (√(1 + x) − 1)/x = 1/(√(1 + x) + 1), which tends to 1/2. Finite. Correct.
  4. (D) The right-hand limit is +1 and the left-hand limit is −1. The two differ, so the limit does not exist. Incorrect.

Answer: (A) and (C).

Common misconception. Treating "the limit does not exist" and "the limit is infinite" as the same failure. Option (B) fails by diverging; option (D) fails by disagreeing on the two sides. Only (D) is the genuine non-existence case, and neither counts as finite.

EM-U3B-Q21NATeasy

The value of limx→0 (1 − cos 4x)/x² is ____.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 8

Detailed solution

Concept. The form is 0/0. The standard limit (1 − cos x)/x² → 1/2 generalises to (1 − cos kx)/x² → k²/2 for a constant k.

Step by step.

  1. Multiply numerator and denominator by 16: (1 − cos 4x)/x² = 16 · (1 − cos 4x)/(4x)².
  2. Put u = 4x. As x → 0, u → 0, so the bracket tends to 1/2.
  3. Hence the limit is 16 · (1/2) = 8.
  4. Check with the double-angle identity 1 − cos 4x = 2 sin²(2x), which gives 2 sin²(2x)/x² → 2 · 4 = 8 directly.

Answer: 8 (exact integer).

Common misconception. Using (1 − cos 4x)/x² ≈ 1/2, which forgets the factor k and gives 4. The factor appears squared because the argument is squared in the denominator; for cos 4x the limit is k²/2, not k/2.

EM-U3B-Q24NATmedium

The value of limx→0 (sin 3x − 3 sin x)/x³ is ____.

Show answer & solution

Answer: −4

Detailed solution

Concept. The form is 0/0. Expanding both sines about 0 to the cubic term isolates the leading behaviour of the difference.

Step by step.

  1. sin 3x = 3x − (3x)³/6 + … = 3x − 4.5x³ + ….
  2. 3 sin x = 3x − 3x³/6 + … = 3x − 0.5x³ + ….
  3. Subtract: sin 3x − 3 sin x = (−4.5 + 0.5)x³ + … = −4x³ + ….
  4. Divide by x³: −4 + O(x²), so the limit is −4.
  5. Cross-check by L'Hôpital's rule three times: (3 cos 3x − 3 cos x)/(3x²), (−9 sin 3x + 3 sin x)/(6x), (−27 cos 3x + 3 cos x)/6, giving (−27 + 3)/6 = −4 at x = 0.

Answer: −4 (exact integer).

Common misconception. Reporting 0 because sin 3x and 3 sin x agree to first order — the leading terms cancel, and the sign of the surviving cubic term is what the question tests. A second slip is to drop the minus sign, which comes from sin 3x growing much faster than 3 sin x.

EM-U3C-Q01MCQeasy

The value of limx→0 (1 − cos 2x)/(1 − cos 3x) is

  1. 4/9
  2. 9/4
  3. 2/3
  4. 1
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Both numerator and denominator tend to 0, so the form is 0/0. The standard limit (1 − cos θ)/θ² → 1/2 reduces every such quotient to the squares of the two coefficients.

Step by step.

  1. Divide numerator and denominator by x²: [(1 − cos 2x)/x²] / [(1 − cos 3x)/x²].
  2. Write the numerator as 4 · (1 − cos 2x)/(2x)² and the denominator as 9 · (1 − cos 3x)/(3x)².
  3. As x → 0, each bracket tends to 1/2, so the ratio tends to (4 · 1/2)/(9 · 1/2) = 4/9.

Answer: 4/9 — option (A).

Common misconception. Cancelling the cosines to get the ratio of the coefficients 2/3 (option C), or inverting it to 9/4 (option B). The quadratic behaviour of 1 − cos θ is what fixes the answer, and the squares of the coefficients 2 and 3 give the ratio 4 : 9, not 2 : 3.

EM-U3C-Q02MCQeasy

The value of limx→0 (e3x − 1)/sin 2x is

  1. 3/2
  2. 2/3
  3. 3
  4. 1
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Two standard limits do the work: (eu − 1)/u → 1 and (sin u)/u → 1. Both are statements about a quotient whose argument tends to 0, so the arguments must be matched exactly before the limits are applied.

Step by step.

  1. Multiply and divide by the arguments: [(e3x − 1)/(3x)] · [(2x)/sin 2x] · (3x)/(2x).
  2. The first bracket tends to 1 and the second to 1.
  3. The remaining factor is (3x)/(2x) = 3/2, so the limit is 3/2.

Answer: 3/2 — option (A).

Common misconception. Reporting the ratio of the coefficients inside the two functions in the wrong order, 2/3 (option B), or taking only the numerator coefficient 3 (option C) as if the denominator were sin x. The denominator argument is 2x, so its coefficient belongs in the answer.

EM-U3C-Q06MCQmedium

The value of limx→0 (cos x − 1 + x²/2)/x⁴ is

  1. 1/24
  2. 1/12
  3. 1/6
  4. 0
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. The numerator vanishes to order four at the origin, so only the fourth-order term of the cosine expansion survives. The Maclaurin series cos x = 1 − x²/2 + x⁴/24 − x⁶/720 + … is the quickest route.

Step by step.

  1. Substitute the series: cos x − 1 + x²/2 = x⁴/24 − x⁶/720 + ….
  2. Divide by x⁴: 1/24 − x²/720 + ….
  3. Letting x → 0 leaves 1/24.

Check by L'Hôpital. Four applications give lim (cos x)/24 = 1/24.

Answer: 1/24 — option (A).

Common misconception. Stopping after two applications of L'Hôpital and reporting 0 (option D), which only shows that the numerator vanishes faster than x². Another slip is using x²/2 twice and reaching 1/12 (option B).

EM-U3C-Q07MCQmedium

The value of limx→0 (1/sin x − 1/x) is

  1. 0
  2. 1/6
  3. 1/2
  4. The limit does not exist
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. The expression is the indeterminate form ∞ − ∞, which must be turned into a single quotient before any limit rule applies. After combining, the order of vanishing of numerator and denominator decides the answer.

Step by step.

  1. Combine over a common denominator: 1/sin x − 1/x = (x − sin x)/(x sin x).
  2. The form is now 0/0. Use x − sin x = x³/6 − x⁵/120 + … and x sin x = x² − x⁴/6 + ….
  3. Dividing, (x − sin x)/(x sin x) = (x³/6 + …)/(x² + …) = x/6 + ….
  4. As x → 0 the quotient tends to 0.

Answer: 0 — option (A).

Common misconception. Applying L'Hôpital to the separate terms, or noticing that both pieces blow up and concluding the difference must blow up too. The two singularities cancel: 1/sin x and 1/x differ only by a term of order x. Reporting 1/2 (option C) comes from confusing this with lim (1/x² − 1/sin²x), whose value is not zero.

EM-U3C-Q12MCQhard

The value of limx→0 ((1 + x)1/x − e)/x is

  1. −e/2
  2. e/2
  3. −e
  4. 0
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Write the variable base as an exponential and expand its logarithm. The limit measures the first-order gap between (1 + x)1/x and its limiting value e.

Step by step.

  1. (1 + x)1/x = exp[ ln(1 + x)/x ].
  2. ln(1 + x) = x − x²/2 + x³/3 − …, so ln(1 + x)/x = 1 − x/2 + x²/3 − ….
  3. Hence (1 + x)1/x = e · exp(−x/2 + x²/3 − …) = e(1 − x/2 + O(x²)).
  4. Subtracting e and dividing by x leaves −e/2 + O(x), so the limit is −e/2.

Answer: −e/2 — option (A).

Common misconception. Replacing (1 + x)1/x by e outright and reporting 0 (option D). That substitution is only valid when the numerator is not itself vanishing; here the numerator is e · (−x/2), of the same order as the denominator, so the leading correction decides the answer.

EM-U3C-Q16MSQmedium

Consider L = limx→0 (sin x − x)/x³. Which of the following statements are TRUE?

  1. The quotient has the indeterminate form 0/0 at x = 0.
  2. One application of L'Hôpital's rule replaces it by limx→0 (cos x − 1)/(3x²).
  3. L = −1/6.
  4. L = 0.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. L'Hôpital's rule converts 0/0 into the quotient of derivatives, and it may be applied repeatedly as long as the new quotient is again 0/0. The value itself is best confirmed by the sine series.

Step by step.

  1. Option (A). sin 0 − 0 = 0 and 0³ = 0, so the form is 0/0 — TRUE.
  2. Option (B). Differentiating numerator and denominator gives (cos x − 1)/(3x²) — TRUE.
  3. That quotient is still 0/0; differentiating twice more gives (−sin x)/(6x) → −1/6.
  4. Option (C). The series sin x = x − x³/6 + … gives (sin x − x)/x³ = −1/6 + O(x²) → −1/6 — TRUE.
  5. Option (D). L = −1/6 ≠ 0 — FALSE.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Stopping at the first derivative and concluding the limit is 0 because the numerator looks "smaller" than x³ (option D). Numerator and denominator vanish to the same order here, so the limit is a nonzero constant.

EM-U3C-Q21NATeasy

The value of limx→0 (ln(1 + x) − x)/x² is ____.

Show answer & solution

Answer: -0.5

Detailed solution

Concept. The numerator vanishes to second order, so the limit is the coefficient of x² in the expansion of ln(1 + x).

Step by step.

  1. ln(1 + x) = x − x²/2 + x³/3 − ….
  2. Subtract x: ln(1 + x) − x = −x²/2 + x³/3 − ….
  3. Divide by x²: −1/2 + x/3 − …, which tends to −1/2.

Check by L'Hôpital. (1/(1 + x) − 1)/(2x) = (−x/(1 + x))/(2x) = −1/(2(1 + x)) → −1/2.

Answer: −0.5 (exact decimal).

Common misconception. Reporting +0.5 by losing the sign of the quadratic term, or 0 from the single substitution ln(1 + x) ≈ x. That approximation is only first order and is exactly what has to be refined here.

Continuity analysis 18
EM-U3-Q03MCQeasy

Let f(x) = (x² − 9)/(x − 3) for x ≠ 3 and f(3) = k. Then f is continuous at x = 3 exactly when k equals

  1. 0
  2. 3
  3. 9
  4. 6
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Continuity at a point requires limx→a f(x) = f(a). When the formula is a 0/0 quotient, the limit is found by cancelling the common factor — this is a removable discontinuity.

Step by step.

  1. Factor the numerator: x² − 9 = (x − 3)(x + 3).
  2. For x ≠ 3, cancel: f(x) = x + 3.
  3. So limx→3 f(x) = 3 + 3 = 6, and continuity needs k = 6.

Answer: 6 — option (D).

Common misconception. Substituting x = 3 into x + 3 but reporting 3 (the root) or 9 (the constant term of the numerator). The value of the function at the point must match the limit, and the limit is the value of the cancelled expression.

EM-U3-Q08MCQmedium

Let f(x) = x sin(1/x) for x ≠ 0 and f(0) = 0. Then f is

  1. continuous at 0 but not differentiable at 0
  2. differentiable at 0 but not continuous at 0
  3. both continuous and differentiable at 0
  4. neither continuous nor differentiable at 0
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Continuity at 0 is a squeeze: |x sin(1/x)| ≤ |x|. Differentiability at 0 is a limit of a difference quotient, and the oscillating factor makes that limit fail.

Step by step.

  1. Squeeze: 0 ≤ |x sin(1/x)| ≤ |x| and |x| → 0, so f(x) → 0 = f(0): continuous at 0.
  2. Difference quotient at 0: (f(h) − f(0))/h = sin(1/h).
  3. As h → 0, sin(1/h) oscillates in [−1, 1] and has no limit, so f′(0) does not exist.

Answer: continuous but not differentiable at 0 — option (A).

Common misconception. Assuming continuity implies differentiability, or judging by the oscillation of sin(1/x) alone and declaring the function discontinuous. The factor x kills the oscillation in the function values but not in the slopes, so the function is continuous and not differentiable.

EM-U3-Q13MCQhard

Let a > 0, f(x) = xa sin(1/x) for x ≠ 0 and f(0) = 0. Which statement is TRUE?

  1. f is continuous at 0 for every a > 0, and differentiable at 0 for every a > 1.
  2. f is continuous at 0 for every a > 1, and differentiable at 0 for every a > 0.
  3. f is continuous and differentiable at 0 for every a > 0.
  4. f is neither continuous nor differentiable at 0 for any a.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Continuity needs xa sin(1/x) → 0; differentiability needs the quotient xa−1 sin(1/x) to have a limit. The two conditions differ by exactly one power of x.

Step by step.

  1. For continuity, |xa sin(1/x)| ≤ |x|a → 0 whenever a > 0.
  2. The difference quotient at 0 is (f(h) − f(0))/h = ha−1 sin(1/h).
  3. This tends to 0 exactly when the exponent a − 1 is positive, i.e. a > 1.
  4. At a = 1 it is sin(1/h), which has no limit, so a > 1 is required.

Answer: continuous for every a > 0, differentiable for every a > 1 — option (A).

Common misconception. Assuming the same threshold for both properties. The exponent needed for differentiability is one higher than for continuity, which is why the boundary case a = 1 — the function x sin(1/x) — is continuous but not differentiable at 0.

EM-U3-Q17MSQeasy

Let f be continuous on the closed interval [a, b]. Which of the following must be TRUE?

  1. f is bounded on [a, b].
  2. f attains a maximum and a minimum on [a, b].
  3. f is differentiable on (a, b).
  4. f takes every value between f(a) and f(b).
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Three consequences of continuity on a closed bounded interval: boundedness, the extreme value theorem and the intermediate value theorem. Differentiability is not one of them.

Step by step.

  1. (A) Boundedness follows from the extreme value theorem, which already produces the maximum and minimum.
  2. (B) This is the extreme value theorem, and it needs the interval closed and bounded.
  3. (C) False: f(x) = |x| is continuous on [−1, 1] but not differentiable at 0.
  4. (D) This is the intermediate value theorem.

Answer: (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Believing continuity is 'almost differentiability'. Continuity is a much weaker hypothesis: |x| is the standard counterexample, and the Weierstrass function is continuous everywhere yet differentiable nowhere.

EM-U3-Q19MSQmedium

For which of the following functions is the discontinuity at the stated point removable?

  1. (x² − 4)/(x − 2) at x = 2
  2. 1/x at x = 0
  3. sin x / x at x = 0
  4. |x|/x at x = 0
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. A discontinuity is removable exactly when the two-sided limit exists and is finite but differs from the function value (or the function is undefined there). A jump or an infinite limit is not removable.

Step by step.

  1. (A) (x² − 4)/(x − 2) = x + 2 for x ≠ 2, so the limit is 4: removable.
  2. (B) 1/x has one-sided limits ±∞: an infinite discontinuity, not removable.
  3. (C) sin x / x → 1: removable, since defining the value as 1 makes the function continuous.
  4. (D) |x|/x equals 1 for x > 0 and −1 for x < 0: a jump, not removable.

Answer: (A) and (C).

Common misconception. Calling every point where a function is undefined a removable discontinuity. Removability is about the existence of a finite two-sided limit; 1/x and |x|/x both fail that test.

EM-U3-Q22NATmedium

Let f(x) = (3x² + ax + a + 3)/(x² + x − 2). The function has a removable discontinuity at x = −2 exactly when a equals ____.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 15

Detailed solution

Concept. The denominator vanishes at x = −2 (it factors as (x + 2)(x − 1)). For the discontinuity to be removable the numerator must vanish there too, cancelling the factor x + 2.

Step by step.

  1. Denominator at x = −2: 4 − 2 − 2 = 0, so the point is a candidate discontinuity.
  2. Numerator at x = −2: 3 · 4 − 2a + a + 3 = 15 − a.
  3. Removability needs 15 − a = 0, so a = 15.
  4. Check: the numerator becomes 3x² + 15x + 18 = 3(x + 2)(x + 3), the denominator (x + 2)(x − 1), and the ratio tends to 3 · 1/(−3) = −1 as x → −2 — a finite limit, so the discontinuity is removable.

Answer: 15

Common misconception. Setting the numerator's value at x = −2 equal to the denominator's, or cancelling a as if it were a common factor. The condition is that both numerator and denominator vanish at the point, which is what allows the shared factor to cancel.

EM-U3B-Q03MCQeasy

Let f(x) = (√x − 3)/(x − 9) for x ≠ 9 and f(9) = k. Then f is continuous at x = 9 exactly when k equals

  1. 0
  2. 6
  3. 1/3
  4. 1/6
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Continuity at x = 9 needs limx→9 f(x) = f(9). Here the formula is undefined at x = 9, so the value must be supplied by the limit.

Step by step.

  1. At x = 9 both √x − 3 and x − 9 vanish, so the form is 0/0. Rationalise the numerator by multiplying by (√x + 3)/(√x + 3).
  2. (√x − 3)/(x − 9) = (x − 9)/[(x − 9)(√x + 3)].
  3. Cancel the common factor x − 9: f(x) = 1/(√x + 3) for x ≠ 9.
  4. So limx→9 f(x) = 1/6, and continuity forces k = 1/6.

Answer: 1/6 — option (D).

Common misconception. Applying L'Hôpital's rule to √x − 3 gives 1/(2√x), which is the derivative of the numerator alone; forgetting to differentiate the denominator as well yields 1/3 (option C). Another slip is to use the numerator's constant 3 as the answer (option B).

EM-U3B-Q08MCQmedium

Let f(x) = |x − 2| + |x + 2|. Which statement is TRUE?

  1. f is continuous everywhere and not differentiable only at x = 2
  2. f is discontinuous at x = 2 and at x = −2
  3. f is continuous everywhere and not differentiable at x = 2 and at x = −2
  4. f is continuous and differentiable everywhere
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. A sum of absolute values is continuous everywhere because each absolute value is. It can fail to be differentiable only where an argument vanishes — here at x = 2 and x = −2 — because the slope of |t| changes sign there.

Step by step.

  1. Split the line at the two break points. For x < −2: f(x) = −(x − 2) − (x + 2) = −2x.
  2. For −2 ≤ x ≤ 2: f(x) = −(x − 2) + (x + 2) = 4.
  3. For x > 2: f(x) = (x − 2) + (x + 2) = 2x.
  4. At x = 2 the left derivative is 0 (from the middle piece) and the right derivative is 2, so f has a corner there.
  5. At x = −2 the left derivative is −2 and the right derivative is 0, another corner.

Answer: continuous everywhere, not differentiable at x = 2 and x = −2 — option (C).

Common misconception. Assuming a corner at x = 2 only, since that is the more visible kink (option A), or assuming that a continuous piecewise-linear function must be smooth (option D). Two absolute values mean two corners unless the break points coincide.

EM-U3B-Q12MCQhard

Let f(x) = x² sin(1/x) for x ≠ 0 and f(0) = 0. Which statement is TRUE?

  1. f is not continuous at x = 0
  2. f is differentiable at 0 with f′(0) = 0, and f′ is not continuous at 0
  3. f is continuous at 0 but not differentiable at 0
  4. f is differentiable at 0 and f′ is continuous at 0
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Two separate questions are being asked: does the difference quotient converge, and is the resulting derivative function continuous? They can have different answers, and here they do.

Step by step.

  1. Continuity: |f(x)| ≤ x² → 0, since sin(1/x) is bounded and x² → 0. So f(x) → 0 = f(0), ruling out option (A).
  2. Differentiability at 0: f′(0) = limh→0 [h² sin(1/h) − 0]/h = limh→0 h sin(1/h) = 0, so f′(0) = 0.
  3. For x ≠ 0, the product and chain rules give f′(x) = 2x sin(1/x) − cos(1/x).
  4. The term cos(1/x) oscillates between −1 and 1 without settling, so limx→0 f′(x) does not exist and f′ is not continuous at 0. Options (C) and (D) are therefore both wrong.

Answer: differentiable at 0 with f′(0) = 0, and f′ not continuous at 0 — option (B).

Common misconception. Believing that a derivative, once it exists, must be continuous. Differentiation improves regularity at the single point but not in a neighbourhood: x² sin(1/x) is the standard example of a differentiable function with a discontinuous derivative. Comparing this with x sin(1/x), which is not even differentiable at 0, is the fastest way to see the one-power gap.

EM-U3B-Q17MSQeasy

Each function below is defined to be 0 at the origin and by the stated rule elsewhere. Which of them are continuous at x = 0?

  1. f(x) = sin(1/x) for x ≠ 0
  2. f(x) = x² sin(1/x) for x ≠ 0
  3. f(x) = |x| for x ≠ 0
  4. f(x) = 1/x for x ≠ 0
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Continuity at 0 requires limx→0 f(x) = f(0) = 0. Since all four functions are assigned the value 0 at the origin, each question reduces to whether the limit is 0.

Step by step.

  1. (A) sin(1/x) oscillates between −1 and 1 as x → 0, so no limit exists. Incorrect.
  2. (B) |x² sin(1/x)| ≤ x² → 0 because sin(1/x) is bounded and x² → 0. Limit 0. Correct.
  3. (C) |x| → 0. Limit 0. Correct.
  4. (D) 1/x is unbounded near 0, so the limit is not 0. Incorrect.

Answer: (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Judging continuity from the shape of the graph far from the origin. Option (B) looks wilder than option (A) because it oscillates faster, yet the damping factor x² controls it completely. The squeeze argument, not visual impression, decides.

EM-U3B-Q19MSQmedium

Let f(x) = (x² − 5x + 6)/(x − 2) for x ≠ 2. Which of the following statements are TRUE?

  1. f has a removable discontinuity at x = 2
  2. limx→2 f(x) = −1
  3. f has a vertical asymptote at x = 2
  4. f has a jump discontinuity at x = 2
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A) and (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. At a point where the formula is undefined, classify the discontinuity by the behaviour of the two one-sided limits: a finite common value means removable, two different finite values mean a jump, and divergence means an asymptote.

Step by step.

  1. Factor the numerator: x² − 5x + 6 = (x − 2)(x − 3).
  2. For x ≠ 2, cancel: f(x) = x − 3.
  3. So limx→2 f(x) = 2 − 3 = −1, a finite two-sided limit, which makes the discontinuity removable — (A) and (B) are true.
  4. The denominator vanishes only at x = 2, where the numerator equals 1 − 10 + 6 = −3, not 0, so the cancellation is complete and there is no asymptote — (C) is false.
  5. Both one-sided limits equal −1, so they agree and there is no jump — (D) is false.

Answer: (A) and (B).

Common misconception. Seeing a zero in the denominator and concluding "asymptote" without checking the numerator. A shared factor removes the singularity; the function is the straight line y = x − 3 with a single point deleted, and the graph is a line with a hole, not a wall.

EM-U3B-Q22NATmedium

Let f(x) = x² for x ≤ 1 and f(x) = ax + b for x > 1. If f is differentiable at x = 1, then a + b equals ____.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 1

Detailed solution

Concept. Differentiability at a point implies continuity there, so two conditions must hold: the two pieces must meet, and their slopes must agree.

Step by step.

  1. Continuity at x = 1: limx→1⁻ f(x) = 1 and limx→1⁺ f(x) = a + b, so a + b = 1.
  2. Differentiability at x = 1: the left derivative is 2x evaluated at x = 1, which is 2; the right derivative is a.
  3. Matching slopes gives a = 2, and then a + b = 1 gives b = −1.
  4. Therefore a + b = 2 − 1 = 1.

Answer: 1 (exact integer).

Common misconception. Imposing only continuity and stopping, which leaves b free and makes a + b indeterminate. Continuity alone puts the line through the point (1, 1); it is the slope condition a = 2 that pins the line down. Note also that the reverse implication fails: continuity does not imply differentiability.

EM-U3C-Q03MCQeasy

Let f(x) = ⌊x⌋ be the greatest-integer (floor) function. Which statement is TRUE?

  1. f is discontinuous at every integer and continuous at every non-integer.
  2. f is discontinuous only at x = 0.
  3. f is discontinuous at every half-integer and continuous at every integer.
  4. f is continuous on ℝ.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. f is continuous at a exactly when the left limit, the right limit and f(a) all agree. The floor function is constant on every interval [n, n + 1) and jumps at the right end.

Step by step.

  1. At an integer n: for x slightly below n, ⌊x⌋ = n − 1; for x slightly above n, ⌊x⌋ = n. The two one-sided limits differ, so f is discontinuous at n.
  2. The jump is exactly 1 at every integer, so no integer is exceptional.
  3. If a is not an integer, f equals the constant ⌊a⌋ on the whole interval between the neighbouring integers, hence it is continuous at a.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Judging continuity from a sketch that looks like a staircase everywhere and concluding "continuous except at the origin" (option B). The jump recurs at every integer; the origin is not special.

EM-U3C-Q08MCQmedium

Let f(x) = (e1/x − 1)/(e1/x + 1) for x ≠ 0, and f(0) = 0. The discontinuity of f at x = 0 is

  1. removable
  2. a jump discontinuity
  3. an infinite discontinuity
  4. none — f is continuous at x = 0
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Classify a discontinuity by the two one-sided limits. If both are finite and equal the point is removable; finite and unequal is a jump; if a one-sided limit is infinite the discontinuity is infinite.

Step by step.

  1. As x → 0⁺, 1/x → +∞ so e1/x → +∞. Dividing numerator and denominator by e1/x gives (1 − e−1/x)/(1 + e−1/x) → (1 − 0)/(1 + 0) = 1.
  2. As x → 0⁻, 1/x → −∞ so e1/x → 0, and the quotient tends to (0 − 1)/(0 + 1) = −1.
  3. Both one-sided limits exist and are finite but unequal (1 and −1), so the discontinuity is a jump.

Answer: a jump discontinuity — option (B).

Common misconception. Seeing e1/x and assuming the limit is infinite (option C). The exponential does blow up, but it appears in both numerator and denominator, so the quotient stays bounded; the two sides settle on different values, which is what makes the jump.

EM-U3C-Q13MCQhard

Let f(x) = x² when x is rational and f(x) = 0 when x is irrational. The function f is continuous

  1. only at x = 0
  2. at every rational point and at no irrational point
  3. at no point of ℝ
  4. at every point of ℝ
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Both ℚ and its complement are dense in ℝ. So at a point a the values of f along rational sequences tend to a² and along irrational sequences to 0. Continuity requires the two to agree, and both to equal f(a).

Step by step.

  1. At a ≠ 0: choose rationals qn → a and irrationals rn → a. Then f(qn) → a² ≠ 0 while f(rn) → 0, so the limit does not exist and f is discontinuous at a.
  2. At a = 0: for every x, f(x) is either x² or 0, so 0 ≤ f(x) ≤ x².
  3. By the squeeze principle f(x) → 0 = f(0) as x → 0, so f is continuous at 0.

Answer: only at x = 0 — option (A).

Common misconception. Assuming the function is continuous wherever the formula "looks continuous" — x² on the rationals (option B). A function defined piecewise by dense sets has to be tested with sequences from both sets; here only the one point where the two formulas agree survives.

EM-U3C-Q17MSQmedium

Which of the following functions have a jump discontinuity at the stated point?

  1. f(x) = |x − 3|/(x − 3) at x = 3
  2. f(x) = ⌊x⌋ at x = 2
  3. f(x) = 1/(x − 1)² at x = 1
  4. f(x) = (sin x)/x at x = 0
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A) and (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. A jump discontinuity means both one-sided limits exist and are finite but different. Infinite one-sided limits give an infinite discontinuity; equal finite one-sided limits give a removable one.

Step by step.

  1. Option (A). For x > 3 the quotient is +1, for x < 3 it is −1. Both finite, unequal — a jump.
  2. Option (B). limx→2⁻ ⌊x⌋ = 1 and limx→2⁺ ⌊x⌋ = 2. Both finite, unequal — a jump.
  3. Option (C). 1/(x − 1)² → +∞ from both sides, so the discontinuity is infinite, not a jump.
  4. Option (D). (sin x)/x → 1 from both sides, so the discontinuity is removable.

Answer: Options (A) and (B).

Common misconception. Calling every discontinuity a "jump". The three types are distinguished by the one-sided limits: finite and unequal (jump), finite and equal (removable), or unbounded (infinite).

EM-U3C-Q22NATeasy

Let f(x) = (x³ − 8)/(x − 2) for x ≠ 2 and f(2) = k. Then f is continuous at x = 2 exactly when k equals ____.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 12

Detailed solution

Concept. Continuity at x = 2 requires f(2) = limx→2 f(x). The limit is found by removing the common factor that makes both numerator and denominator vanish.

Step by step.

  1. Factor the difference of cubes: x³ − 8 = (x − 2)(x² + 2x + 4).
  2. Cancel x − 2 for x ≠ 2: f(x) = x² + 2x + 4.
  3. Let x → 2: the limit is 4 + 4 + 4 = 12.
  4. So k = 12 gives continuity.

Answer: 12 (exact integer).

Common misconception. Differentiating instead of factoring and reporting 3·2²/1 = 12 — the right number here only by coincidence — or substituting x = 2 into the uncancelled expression and declaring the function undefined. The factor must be cancelled first.

EM-U3C-Q23NATeasy

Let f(x) = (√(x + 4) − 2)/x for x ≠ 0 and f(0) = k. Then f is continuous at x = 0 exactly when k equals ____.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 0.25

Detailed solution

Concept. The numerator and denominator both vanish at x = 0, so the radical is removed by multiplying by the conjugate.

Step by step.

  1. Multiply numerator and denominator by √(x + 4) + 2: f(x) = [(x + 4) − 4]/[x(√(x + 4) + 2)].
  2. Simplify: f(x) = x/[x(√(x + 4) + 2)] = 1/(√(x + 4) + 2) for x ≠ 0.
  3. Let x → 0: the limit is 1/(2 + 2) = 1/4 = 0.25.

Answer: 0.25 (exact decimal).

Common misconception. Reporting 0 because "the numerator vanishes", or 4 from the denominator alone. Rationalising shows the quotient has a finite nonzero limit.

Mean value theorems 18
EM-U3-Q04MCQeasy

For f(x) = x² on the interval [0, 2], the point c whose existence is asserted by Lagrange's mean value theorem is

  1. 1/2
  2. 1
  3. 3/2
  4. √2
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Lagrange's mean value theorem asserts a point c ∈ (a, b) with f′(c) = (f(b) − f(a))/(b − a), the slope of the chord.

Step by step.

  1. The chord slope is (f(2) − f(0))/(2 − 0) = (4 − 0)/2 = 2.
  2. Set f′(c) = 2c = 2, giving c = 1.
  3. Check 1 ∈ (0, 2), so the asserted point exists and is unique here.

Answer: 1 — option (B).

Common misconception. Confusing the mean value theorem with the midpoint of the interval, or with the point where f attains its average value (f(0) + f(2))/2 = 2 — which would be c = √2, option (D). The theorem equates derivatives, not function values.

EM-U3-Q09MCQmedium

The number of points c in (−√3, √3) at which f′(c) = 0 for f(x) = x³ − 3x is

  1. 0
  2. 1
  3. 3
  4. 2
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Rolle's theorem applies on [−√3, √3] because f is a polynomial (hence continuous and differentiable) and the endpoint values agree. Rolle guarantees at least one stationary point; counting them needs the derivative solved explicitly.

Step by step.

  1. f(−√3) = −3√3 + 3√3 = 0 and f(√3) = 3√3 − 3√3 = 0, so Rolle's hypotheses hold.
  2. f′(x) = 3x² − 3 = 3(x − 1)(x + 1), vanishing at x = ±1.
  3. Both −1 and 1 lie inside (−√3, √3) since √3 ≈ 1.732, so there are exactly two such points.

Answer: 2 — option (D).

Common misconception. Answering 1 from Rolle's theorem as if it guaranteed exactly one point — it guarantees at least one. Answering 3 by also counting the endpoints ±√3, where the derivative is 3, not 0.

EM-U3-Q14MCQhard

Apply Cauchy's mean value theorem to f(x) = x² and g(x) = x³ on [1, 2]. The point c whose existence it asserts is

  1. 3/2
  2. 4/3
  3. 7/3
  4. 14/9
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Cauchy's mean value theorem asserts c ∈ (a, b) with f′(c)/g′(c) = (f(b) − f(a))/(g(b) − g(a)), a ratio of chord slopes rather than a single chord slope. It requires g′(x) ≠ 0 on (a, b).

Step by step.

  1. Chord ratio: (4 − 1)/(8 − 1) = 3/7.
  2. Derivatives: f′(x) = 2x, g′(x) = 3x², so the ratio is 2/(3x).
  3. Solve 2/(3c) = 3/7: 14 = 9c, so c = 14/9 ≈ 1.556.
  4. Check 14/9 ∈ (1, 2), as the theorem promises.

Answer: 14/9 — option (D).

Common misconception. Applying Lagrange's theorem to each function separately and averaging, which gives 3/2 — the midpoint of the x² interval, option (A). Cauchy's theorem couples the two functions through a ratio of derivatives at one common point.

EM-U3-Q18MSQeasy

Which of the following statements about the mean value theorems are TRUE?

  1. Rolle's theorem is the special case of Lagrange's mean value theorem in which f(a) = f(b).
  2. Lagrange's theorem requires continuity on [a, b] and differentiability on (a, b).
  3. Lagrange's theorem requires differentiability at the endpoints a and b.
  4. If f′(x) = 0 at every point of an interval, then f is constant on that interval.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Rolle's theorem is Lagrange's with equal endpoint values, and the zero-derivative corollary is what makes the mean value theorem the main tool for proving identities between functions.

Step by step.

  1. (A) With f(a) = f(b) the chord slope is 0, so Lagrange gives f′(c) = 0 — exactly Rolle.
  2. (B) These are the correct hypotheses: continuity on the closed interval, differentiability on the open one.
  3. (C) False: the theorem needs no one-sided derivative at the endpoints, only continuity there.
  4. (D) Apply Lagrange on any subinterval [x₁, x₂]: f(x₂) − f(x₁) = f′(c)(x₂ − x₁) = 0.

Answer: (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Upgrading the hypothesis to differentiability on [a, b] (option C). Requiring a derivative at the endpoints would exclude functions like √x on [0, 1], for which the theorem still holds.

EM-U3-Q20MSQmedium

Let f(x) = x(x − 1)(x − 2) on [0, 2]. Which of the following are TRUE?

  1. Rolle's theorem applies to f on [0, 2].
  2. f′(c) = 0 at exactly two points of (0, 2).
  3. The conclusion of Rolle's theorem is attained at exactly one point of (0, 2).
  4. There is a point c in (2, 3) with f′(c) = 0.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A) and (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Rolle needs f continuous on [0, 2], differentiable on (0, 2) and f(0) = f(2). The number of stationary points is found by solving f′(x) = 0.

Step by step.

  1. f is a polynomial, and f(0) = 0 = f(2), so Rolle applies: (A) is true.
  2. f(x) = x³ − 3x² + 2x, so f′(x) = 3x² − 6x + 2.
  3. Roots: x = (6 ± √(36 − 24))/6 = 1 ± √3/3, that is ≈ 0.423 and ≈ 1.577, both in (0, 2). So (B) is true and (C) is false.
  4. Both roots lie below 2, so (D) is false.

Answer: (A) and (B).

Common misconception. Reading Rolle's theorem as 'exactly one stationary point'. It guarantees at least one; here the cubic has two, and only a computation of the roots shows that both lie inside the interval.

EM-U3-Q23NATmedium

For f(x) = x² + 2x on [0, 4], let c be the point whose existence Lagrange's mean value theorem asserts. Then c equals ____.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 2

Detailed solution

Concept. Lagrange's theorem gives f′(c) = (f(b) − f(a))/(b − a) for some c strictly inside the interval; for a quadratic the equation is linear in c, so the point is unique.

Step by step.

  1. Chord slope: (f(4) − f(0))/(4 − 0) = (24 − 0)/4 = 6.
  2. f′(x) = 2x + 2, so solve 2c + 2 = 6.
  3. c = 2, and 2 ∈ (0, 4) as required.

Answer: 2

Common misconception. Reporting the midpoint of the interval instead of solving f′(c) = chord slope. For a quadratic the two coincide, which is exactly why the error goes unnoticed here; for f(x) = x³ on [0, 4] the theorem gives c = 4/√3 ≈ 2.31, not the midpoint 2. Always solve the equation the theorem states.

EM-U3B-Q04MCQeasy

Rolle's theorem applies to f(x) = x² − 4x + 1 on the interval [1, 3]. The point c it guarantees is

  1. 1
  2. 3/2
  3. 2
  4. 5/2
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Rolle's theorem: if f is continuous on [a, b], differentiable on (a, b) and f(a) = f(b), then some c in (a, b) has f′(c) = 0.

Step by step.

  1. f is a polynomial, hence continuous on [1, 3] and differentiable on (1, 3).
  2. f(1) = 1 − 4 + 1 = −2 and f(3) = 9 − 12 + 1 = −2, so the equal-endpoint hypothesis holds.
  3. Solve f′(c) = 0: 2c − 4 = 0 gives c = 2.
  4. c = 2 lies in (1, 3), so it is the required point.

Answer: 2 — option (C).

Common misconception. Taking the midpoint of the interval, 2, by coincidence is correct here, but the midpoint is not what the theorem asserts — the point comes from solving f′(c) = 0. Choosing the endpoint value 1 (option A) or half the coefficient sum (option B) is guesswork; always differentiate.

EM-U3B-Q09MCQmedium

Rolle's theorem cannot be applied to f(x) = x2/3 on the interval [−1, 1] because one of its hypotheses fails. Which hypothesis fails?

  1. f is not defined at x = 0
  2. f(−1) ≠ f(1)
  3. f is not continuous on [−1, 1]
  4. f is not differentiable on (−1, 1)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Rolle's theorem has three hypotheses: continuity on [a, b], differentiability on (a, b) and equality of the endpoint values. Any one of them failing blocks the theorem.

Step by step.

  1. f is defined at x = 0, so option (A) is false.
  2. f(−1) = 1 and f(1) = 1, so the endpoints agree and option (B) is false.
  3. f is continuous on [−1, 1] — the cube-root power is continuous everywhere — so option (C) is false.
  4. f′(x) = (2/3)x−1/3 for x ≠ 0, and |f′(x)| → ∞ as x → 0, so the derivative is unbounded at 0 and f is not differentiable at x = 0. That is a point of the open interval (−1, 1), so differentiability fails there and option (D) is correct.

Answer: f is not differentiable on (−1, 1) — option (D).

Common misconception. Confusing continuity with differentiability and declaring the function "well behaved". A vertical tangent at an interior point is exactly the kind of defect Rolle's theorem excludes, and it is the standard counterexample that shows continuity on the closed interval alone is not enough.

EM-U3B-Q13MCQhard

Let f be differentiable on ℝ with f(0) = 0 and 1 ≤ f′(x) ≤ 2 for every x. Which conclusion is valid?

  1. f(3) ≥ 6
  2. f(3) = 3
  3. f(3) ≤ 3
  4. 3 ≤ f(3) ≤ 6
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Lagrange's mean value theorem converts a bound on the derivative into a bound on the increment: on [0, 3] there is a c with f(3) − f(0) = 3 f′(c).

Step by step.

  1. f is differentiable, hence continuous, so the theorem applies on [0, 3].
  2. There is a c in (0, 3) with f(3) = 3 f′(c).
  3. Since 1 ≤ f′(c) ≤ 2, multiplying by 3 gives 3 ≤ f(3) ≤ 6.
  4. And f(0) = 0, so f(3) = 3 f′(c). Both bounds are attained by the linear functions f(x) = x and f(x) = 2x, so neither inequality can be tightened.

Answer: 3 ≤ f(3) ≤ 6 — option (D).

Common misconception. Assuming a single value, f(3) = 3 (option B), or using only one side of the derivative bound, which yields f(3) ≥ 6 (option A) or f(3) ≤ 3 (option C). A derivative bound of the form 1 ≤ f′ ≤ 2 is a two-sided constraint and must produce a two-sided conclusion.

EM-U3B-Q18MSQeasy

Let f(x) = x² on the interval [−1, 1]. Which of the following statements are TRUE?

  1. f(−1) = f(1)
  2. Rolle's theorem applies to f on [−1, 1]
  3. there is exactly one point c in (−1, 1) with f′(c) = 0
  4. f′(c) = 0 at c = 1
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Rolle's theorem asserts the existence of at least one interior point with zero derivative. Counting the points needs the derivative solved separately.

Step by step.

  1. f(−1) = 1 and f(1) = 1, so (A) is true.
  2. f is a polynomial, hence continuous on [−1, 1] and differentiable on (−1, 1), and the endpoints agree; all three hypotheses hold, so (B) is true.
  3. f′(x) = 2x = 0 gives x = 0, the only solution in (−1, 1). So (C) is true.
  4. At c = 1, f′(1) = 2, not 0, so (D) is false.

Answer: (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Reading the conclusion of Rolle's theorem as "at the midpoint", or assuming the point it asserts must be an endpoint. The theorem says nothing about where the point is; only solving f′(c) = 0 locates it, and the endpoint c = 1 is never admissible because the point must lie in the open interval.

EM-U3B-Q20MSQmedium

For which of the following does Lagrange's mean value theorem apply on the stated interval?

  1. f(x) = |x| on [−1, 1]
  2. f(x) = x³ on [−1, 1]
  3. f(x) = 1/x on [1, 2]
  4. f(x) = √x on [0, 1]
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (B), (C) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Lagrange's theorem asks for continuity on the closed interval [a, b] and differentiability on the open interval (a, b). Differentiability at the endpoints is not required.

Step by step.

  1. (A) |x| is continuous on [−1, 1] but has a corner at x = 0, which lies inside (−1, 1). Differentiability fails. Incorrect.
  2. (B) x³ is a polynomial: continuous on [−1, 1] and differentiable on (−1, 1). Correct.
  3. (C) 1/x is continuous on [1, 2] and differentiable on (1, 2), since x is never 0 there. Correct.
  4. (D) √x is continuous on [0, 1] and differentiable on (0, 1), where f′(x) = 1/(2√x). The infinite slope at the endpoint x = 0 is harmless because 0 is an endpoint. Correct.

Answer: (B), (C) and (D).

Common misconception. Rejecting option (D) because √x is not differentiable at 0. That is exactly the point of the open interval in the hypothesis: the theorem needs a tangent line only strictly between the endpoints, so a vertical tangent at an endpoint does not disqualify the function. Option (A), whose defect is interior, is the one that genuinely fails.

EM-U3B-Q23NATmedium

Let f(x) = x³ − 3x² + 2x on the interval [0, 3]. Lagrange's mean value theorem guarantees a point c in (0, 3) with f′(c) equal to the chord slope. The largest such c equals ____.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 2

Detailed solution

Concept. The chord slope on [0, 3] is (f(3) − f(0))/3. The theorem asserts that f′ attains that value somewhere strictly inside the interval; finding where means solving a polynomial equation and discarding any root outside (0, 3).

Step by step.

  1. f(0) = 0 and f(3) = 27 − 27 + 6 = 6, so the chord slope is 6/3 = 2.
  2. f′(x) = 3x² − 6x + 2, so the equation f′(c) = 2 becomes 3c² − 6c + 2 = 2.
  3. That is 3c² − 6c = 3c(c − 2) = 0, whose roots are c = 0 and c = 2.
  4. c = 0 is an endpoint and is excluded; c = 2 lies in (0, 3) and is admissible.
  5. The only admissible point is c = 2, so the largest one is 2.

Answer: 2 (exact integer).

Common misconception. Accepting the root c = 0 because it satisfies f′(0) = 2. The theorem places the point in the open interval, so endpoint roots must be discarded — and here discarding it changes the answer from "two points" to one.

EM-U3C-Q04MCQeasy

For f(x) = x³ on the interval [0, 3], the point c whose existence Lagrange's mean value theorem asserts is

  1. √3
  2. 3/2
  3. √2
  4. 2
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Lagrange's mean value theorem: if f is continuous on [a, b] and differentiable on (a, b), there is c ∈ (a, b) with f′(c) = (f(b) − f(a))/(b − a).

Step by step.

  1. f(3) = 27 and f(0) = 0, so the chord slope is 27/3 = 9.
  2. f′(x) = 3x², so solve 3c² = 9, giving c² = 3.
  3. c = ±√3; only c = √3 ≈ 1.732 lies in (0, 3), so that is the point the theorem guarantees.

Answer: √3 — option (A).

Common misconception. Answering the midpoint 3/2 (option B). For a quadratic the guaranteed point is always the midpoint, but for x³ the tangent is parallel to the chord at √3 ≈ 1.732, left of the midpoint.

EM-U3C-Q09MCQmedium

For f(x) = ln x on the interval [1, e], the point c whose existence Lagrange's mean value theorem asserts is

  1. e − 1
  2. (e + 1)/2
  3. e/2
  4. √e
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. ln x is continuous on [1, e] and differentiable on (1, e) with f′(x) = 1/x, so the theorem applies and the guaranteed point solves 1/c = chord slope.

Step by step.

  1. Chord slope: (ln e − ln 1)/(e − 1) = 1/(e − 1).
  2. Solve 1/c = 1/(e − 1), giving c = e − 1 ≈ 1.718.
  3. Check the interval: 1 < e − 1 < e since e ≈ 2.718, so the point is admissible.

Answer: e − 1 — option (A).

Common misconception. Taking the midpoint (e + 1)/2 (option B) or the geometric mean √e (option D). The guaranteed point depends on the shape of f; for the logarithm it sits well to the left of the midpoint.

EM-U3C-Q10MCQmedium

Let f be differentiable on ℝ with |f′(x)| ≤ 3 for every x, and let f(0) = 1. Which conclusion is guaranteed?

  1. −5 ≤ f(2) ≤ 7
  2. f(2) = 7
  3. |f(2)| ≤ 6
  4. f(2) ≥ 7
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. A bound on the derivative becomes a bound on the function through the mean value theorem: f(2) − f(0) = f′(c) · 2 for some c ∈ (0, 2).

Step by step.

  1. By the mean value theorem, f(2) − 1 = 2 f′(c) for some c ∈ (0, 2).
  2. Taking absolute values, |f(2) − 1| = 2|f′(c)| ≤ 6.
  3. That inequality is exactly −5 ≤ f(2) ≤ 7.
  4. Both endpoints are attainable — for example f(x) = 3x + 1 gives f(2) = 7 and f(x) = −3x + 1 gives f(2) = −5 — so no tighter bound is forced.

Answer: −5 ≤ f(2) ≤ 7 — option (A).

Common misconception. Reporting only f(2) = 7 (option B), which mistakes a bound for an attained value, or |f(2)| ≤ 6 (option C), which forgets that the bound is on the change in f, not on f itself. With f(0) = 1 the interval is centred at 1, not at 0.

EM-U3C-Q14MCQhard

Let f be twice differentiable on ℝ with f(0) = 0, f(1) = 2 and f(2) = 0. Then there exists c ∈ (0, 2) such that

  1. f″(c) < 0
  2. f″(c) > 0
  3. f″(c) = 0
  4. f″(c) = 2
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Apply the mean value theorem twice — first to f on the two halves to locate slopes of f, then to f′ on the interval between those two points to read off a value of f″.

Step by step.

  1. On [0, 1]: slope (2 − 0)/1 = 2, so there is c1 ∈ (0, 1) with f′(c1) = 2.
  2. On [1, 2]: slope (0 − 2)/1 = −2, so there is c2 ∈ (1, 2) with f′(c2) = −2.
  3. Since c1 < c2, apply the mean value theorem to f′ on [c1, c2]: there is c ∈ (c1, c2) with f″(c) = (−2 − 2)/(c2 − c1) = −4/(c2 − c1).
  4. The denominator is positive, so f″(c) < 0. The value is negative but its size is not determined, so nothing stronger can be asserted.

Answer: f″(c) < 0 — option (A).

Common misconception. Reading the "rise then fall" shape as a statement about the second derivative being positive (option B), or assuming the second derivative must vanish somewhere (option C). Rolle's theorem applied to f′ needs f′(c1) = f′(c2), which is false here; the two slopes are 2 and −2.

EM-U3C-Q18MSQmedium

Let f be differentiable on ℝ with f(1) = 3 and f(4) = 9. Which of the following must be TRUE?

  1. There is c ∈ (1, 4) with f′(c) = 2.
  2. f′(x) = 2 for every x ∈ (1, 4).
  3. There is c ∈ (1, 4) with f(c) = 5.
  4. f is bounded on [1, 4].
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Differentiability implies continuity, so the extreme value theorem and the intermediate value theorem are available on [1, 4]; the mean value theorem converts the two endpoint values into a statement about f′.

Step by step.

  1. Option (A). The chord slope is (9 − 3)/(4 − 1) = 2, so the mean value theorem gives f′(c) = 2 for some c ∈ (1, 4) — TRUE.
  2. Option (B). The theorem locates one point with that slope; it says nothing about the rest of the interval. For instance f(x) = x² − 5x + 7 also satisfies the two endpoint conditions but has f′(x) = 2x − 5 ≠ 2 in general — FALSE.
  3. Option (C). f is continuous and 3 < 5 < 9, so the intermediate value theorem gives f(c) = 5 — TRUE.
  4. Option (D). f is continuous on the closed bounded interval [1, 4], hence bounded there — TRUE.

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D).

Common misconception. Reading the mean value theorem backwards as a statement about the whole interval (option B). It asserts existence of at least one point with the chord slope; many different functions share the same two endpoint values.

EM-U3C-Q25NATeasy

Let f(x) = x² − 4x + 1 on the interval [0, 6]. Lagrange's mean value theorem guarantees a point c in (0, 6) with f′(c) equal to the chord slope. The sum of all such points is ____.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 3

Detailed solution

Concept. The theorem guarantees at least one interior point whose tangent is parallel to the chord; here the equation that determines those points is linear in c, so there is exactly one.

Step by step.

  1. f(0) = 1 and f(6) = 36 − 24 + 1 = 13, so the chord slope is (13 − 1)/6 = 2.
  2. f′(x) = 2x − 4, so solve 2c − 4 = 2, giving c = 3.
  3. 3 ∈ (0, 6), so it is the only admissible point and the sum is 3.

Answer: 3 (exact integer).

Common misconception. Summing the roots of f′(x) = 0 instead of the roots of f′(x) = chord slope, which gives the vertex x = 2 rather than the point the theorem asserts. The chord slope must be computed from the two endpoints first.

Partial derivatives 18
EM-U3-Q05MCQeasy

For f(x, y) = x²y + y³, the mixed partial derivative ∂²f/∂x∂y equals

  1. 2x
  2. 2y
  3. 2x + 3y²
  4. 6y
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Mixed partials are computed by differentiating in either order; by Clairaut's theorem the two orders agree whenever the second partials are continuous, which they are for a polynomial.

Step by step.

  1. Differentiate with respect to x first: ∂f/∂x = 2xy.
  2. Now differentiate that with respect to y: ∂/∂y (2xy) = 2x.
  3. Reversing the order gives the same answer: ∂f/∂y = x² + 3y² and ∂/∂x (x² + 3y²) = 2x.

Answer: 2x — option (A).

Common misconception. Reporting ∂²f/∂x² = 2y (option B) or ∂²f/∂y² = 6y (option D), which are the pure second partials. The notation ∂²f/∂x∂y here means differentiate in x then y, and the mixed derivative of a polynomial is generally of lower degree than either pure one.

EM-U3-Q06MCQeasy

The tangent plane to the surface z = x² + y² at the point (1, 1, 2) is

  1. z = x + y
  2. z = 2x + 2y
  3. z = 2x + 2y − 2
  4. z = 2x + 2y + 2
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. The tangent plane to z = f(x, y) at (a, b, f(a, b)) is z = f(a, b) + fx(a, b)(x − a) + fy(a, b)(y − b), a first-order Taylor expansion.

Step by step.

  1. Here fx = 2x and fy = 2y, so at (1, 1) both slopes equal 2, and f(1, 1) = 2.
  2. Substitute: z = 2 + 2(x − 1) + 2(y − 1).
  3. Expand: z = 2x + 2y − 2.

Answer: z = 2x + 2y − 2 — option (C).

Common misconception. Dropping the constant: writing the plane as z = 2x + 2y (option B) ignores that the plane must pass through (1, 1, 2) — it would pass through the origin instead. Always substitute the point back to check.

EM-U3-Q10MCQmedium

For f(x, y) = x³y² − 2xy, the value of fxx at (1, 1) is

  1. 2
  2. 4
  3. 6
  4. 0
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. A pure second partial fxx differentiates twice with respect to x, treating y as a constant throughout.

Step by step.

  1. fx = 3x²y² − 2y.
  2. fxx = 6xy².
  3. At (1, 1): 6 · 1 · 1 = 6.

Answer: 6 — option (C).

Common misconception. Reporting the mixed partial fxy = 6x²y − 2, which equals 4 at (1, 1) — option (B) — or the pure fyy = 2x³, which equals 2 — option (A). fxx differentiates twice in x, and the −2xy term drops out after the first differentiation.

EM-U3-Q11MCQmedium

Let w = x²y with x = 2t and y = t². Then dw/dt at t = 1 equals

  1. 8
  2. 16
  3. 12
  4. 24
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. The chain rule for a composite of several variables: dw/dt = (∂w/∂x)(dx/dt) + (∂w/∂y)(dy/dt). Substituting first and differentiating the resulting one-variable function is an equally valid check.

Step by step.

  1. Substituting: w = (2t)²(t²) = 4t⁴, so dw/dt = 16t³ and at t = 1 this is 16.
  2. Chain rule check: ∂w/∂x = 2xy, dx/dt = 2, ∂w/∂y = x², dy/dt = 2t.
  3. So dw/dt = 4xy + 2tx²; at t = 1, x = 2 and y = 1, giving 8 + 8 = 16.

Answer: 16 — option (B).

Common misconception. Keeping only one branch of the chain rule, which gives 8 (the x branch alone) — option (A). Every intermediate variable that depends on t contributes a term.

EM-U3-Q15MCQhard

For f(x, y) = x³ + y³ − 3xy, which statement about the critical points is TRUE?

  1. (0, 0) is a saddle point and (1, 1) is a local minimum.
  2. (0, 0) and (1, 1) are both saddle points.
  3. (0, 0) is a local minimum and (1, 1) is a saddle point.
  4. (1, 1) is a local maximum.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Solve fx = fy = 0, then classify each critical point with the discriminant D = fxxfyy − fxy²: D < 0 is a saddle, D > 0 with fxx > 0 a minimum.

Step by step.

  1. fx = 3x² − 3y = 0 gives y = x²; fy = 3y² − 3x = 0 gives x = y².
  2. Combining, x = x⁴, so x = 0 or x = 1 (the roots of x³ = 1 are only x = 1 in the reals). The critical points are (0, 0) and (1, 1).
  3. fxx = 6x, fyy = 6y, fxy = −3, so D = 36xy − 9.
  4. At (0, 0): D = −9 < 0, a saddle. At (1, 1): D = 27 > 0 and fxx = 6 > 0, a local minimum.

Answer: saddle at (0, 0), local minimum at (1, 1) — option (A).

Common misconception. Classifying from the Hessian signs alone without the discriminant, which mislabels (0, 0) as a minimum because fyy = 0 is not negative. Also common: discarding (1, 1) as a maximum by analogy with x³, which has no critical point at all.

EM-U3-Q25NAThard

For f(x, y) = x²y³, the value of ∂⁴f/∂x²∂y² at (1, 1) is ____.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 12

Detailed solution

Concept. For a product xmyn the mixed partial of order r in x and s in y is [m!/(m − r)!] · [n!/(n − s)!] · xm−r yn−s, and the order of differentiation does not matter for a polynomial.

Step by step.

  1. Differentiate twice in y: ∂f/∂y = 3x²y², then ∂²f/∂y² = 6x²y.
  2. Then twice in x: ∂/∂x (6x²y) = 12xy, then ∂²/∂x² (6x²y) = 12y.
  3. At (1, 1): 12 · 1 = 12. Shortcut: 2!/(0!) · 3!/(1!) · x⁰y¹ = 2 · 6 · y = 12y.

Answer: 12

Common misconception. Counting the derivative as ∂²f/∂x∂y (order two) rather than order four, which gives 6xy² = 6 at (1, 1). The symbol ∂x² means 'twice in x', and ∂y² means 'twice in y', so four differentiations are performed in all.

EM-U3B-Q05MCQeasy

For f(x, y) = x³ + 2xy², the value of ∂f/∂y at the point (1, 2) is

  1. 4
  2. 8
  3. 16
  4. 2
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. The partial derivative ∂f/∂y differentiates with respect to y while holding x constant, so x is treated as a constant coefficient.

Step by step.

  1. Differentiate term by term in y: the term x³ contributes 0, and 2xy² contributes 2x · 2y = 4xy.
  2. So ∂f/∂y = 4xy.
  3. At (1, 2): 4 · 1 · 2 = 8.

Answer: 8 — option (B).

Common misconception. Differentiating with respect to x instead gives 3x² + 2y², which is 7 at (1, 2) and matches no option; using 2y² alone as the answer gives 4 (option A), and squaring the coefficient gives 16 (option C). The factor 2 in 2xy² and the exponent 2 in y both belong in the answer.

EM-U3B-Q06MCQeasy

The gradient of f(x, y) = x² + 3y² at the point (2, 1) is

  1. (4, 6)
  2. (2, 3)
  3. (4, 3)
  4. (2, 6)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. The gradient is the vector of first partial derivatives, ∇f = (fx, fy), evaluated at the stated point.

Step by step.

  1. fx = 2x and fy = 6y.
  2. At (2, 1): fx = 4 and fy = 6.
  3. So ∇f(2, 1) = (4, 6).

Answer: (4, 6) — option (A).

Common misconception. Reporting the partials at the wrong point, or dropping the factor 3 from 3y², gives (4, 3) — option (C). Halving both components gives (2, 3) (option B), which is the gradient of x² + y² rather than of x² + 3y².

EM-U3B-Q10MCQmedium

Let z = x² + y², where x = r cos θ and y = r sin θ. Then ∂z/∂r equals

  1. 2r
  2. 2r²
  3. 2r(cos θ + sin θ)
  4. 0
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Two routes give the same answer: substitute first and differentiate, or apply the chain rule ∂z/∂r = zx xr + zy yr.

Step by step.

  1. Substituting: z = r²cos²θ + r²sin²θ = r², so ∂z/∂r = 2r.
  2. By the chain rule: zx = 2x and zy = 2y, so ∂z/∂r = 2x cos θ + 2y sin θ.
  3. Using x = r cos θ and y = r sin θ this is 2r cos²θ + 2r sin²θ = 2r.
  4. Both routes give ∂z/∂r = 2r.

Answer: 2r — option (A).

Common misconception. Adding the two chain-rule terms without using the trigonometric identity gives 2r(cos θ + sin θ) — option (C) — which is not a function of r alone. Reporting r and then forgetting the factor 2 gives 2r² (option B), and treating x and y as independent of r gives 0 (option D).

EM-U3B-Q14MCQhard

For f(x, y) = 4xy − x⁴ − y⁴, which statement about the critical points is TRUE?

  1. (0, 0) is a local minimum
  2. (1, 1) is a saddle point and (0, 0) is a local maximum
  3. both (0, 0) and (1, 1) are saddle points
  4. (0, 0) is a saddle point and (1, 1) is a local maximum
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Critical points solve fx = fy = 0. Each is classified by the discriminant D = fxxfyy − fxy²: a minimum if D > 0 and fxx > 0, a maximum if D > 0 and fxx < 0, and a saddle if D < 0.

Step by step.

  1. fx = 4y − 4x³ and fy = 4x − 4y³.
  2. Setting both to zero gives y = x³ and x = y³, hence x = x⁹, so x ∈ {0, 1, −1}. The critical points are (0, 0), (1, 1) and (−1, −1).
  3. Second derivatives: fxx = −12x², fyy = −12y², fxy = 4.
  4. At (0, 0): D = 0 · 0 − 16 = −16 < 0, so the point is a saddle.
  5. At (1, 1): D = 144 − 16 = 128 > 0 and fxx = −12 < 0, so it is a local maximum; the same holds at (−1, −1) by symmetry.

Answer: (0, 0) is a saddle and (1, 1) is a local maximum — option (D).

Common misconception. Reading the signs of fxx and fyy separately instead of forming D, which makes both points look like maxima. When D = 0 the second-derivative test is inconclusive and the discriminant is the deciding quantity; a zero discriminant is the case that actually fails, not a negative one.

EM-U3B-Q15MCQhard

For f(x, y) = x² + 3xy + 2y², the value of x fx + y fy at the point (1, 2) is

  1. 15
  2. 19
  3. 30
  4. 45
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. f is a homogeneous polynomial of degree 2, so Euler's theorem gives x fx + y fy = 2f. The expression can be evaluated directly as a check.

Step by step.

  1. f(1, 2) = 1 + 6 + 8 = 15, so f.
  2. Euler: x fx + y fy = 2 · 15 = 30.
  3. Direct check: fx = 2x + 3y = 8 and fy = 3x + 4y = 11, so at (1, 2) the value is 1 · 8 + 2 · 11 = 30.
  4. Both routes give 30.

Answer: 30 — option (C).

Common misconception. Reporting f(1, 2) itself, which is 15 (option A), instead of the Euler expression 2f(1, 2). A second slip is to forget the factor 2 in 3xy and obtain 19 (option B), or to double once more and reach 45 (option D).

EM-U3B-Q25NAThard

For f(x, y, z) = x³y²z, the value of ∂⁴f/∂x²∂y∂z at the point (1, 2, 1) is ____.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 24

Detailed solution

Concept. For a polynomial, mixed partial derivatives commute, so the order of differentiation does not matter; the exponents are simply decremented once per differentiation in that variable.

Step by step.

  1. Differentiate once in z: fz = x³y².
  2. Once in y: fzy = 2x³y.
  3. Once in x: fzyx = 6x²y.
  4. Twice in x: fzyxx = 12xy, then fzyxxx = 12y.
  5. At (1, 2, 1): 12 · 1 · 2 = 24.

Answer: 24 (exact integer).

Common misconception. Dividing by the exponents or subtracting them, as if differentiating were division; and forgetting that x is differentiated twice, which halves the result to 12. The exponents 3, 2 and 1 are decremented once per differentiation, so f loses two powers of x.

EM-U3C-Q05MCQeasy

The gradient of f(x, y) = x²y + xy² at the point (1, 1) is

  1. (3, 3)
  2. (2, 2)
  3. (3, 2)
  4. (1, 1)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. The gradient is the vector of first partial derivatives, ∇f = (fx, fy), evaluated at the stated point. Each partial is computed with the other variable held constant.

Step by step.

  1. Holding y constant: fx = 2xy + y², so fx(1, 1) = 2 + 1 = 3.
  2. Holding x constant: fy = x² + 2xy, so fy(1, 1) = 1 + 2 = 3.
  3. Hence ∇f(1, 1) = (3, 3).

Answer: (3, 3) — option (A).

Common misconception. Swapping the roles and reporting (fy, fx), or dropping the cross term xy² while differentiating with respect to x and getting (2, 2) (option B). The mixed monomial contributes to both partials.

EM-U3C-Q11MCQmedium

Let u = xy. The value of ∂u/∂y at the point (2, 3) is

  1. 8 ln 2
  2. 12
  3. 8
  4. 12 ln 2
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. When differentiating with respect to the exponent, xy is an exponential function of y with base x: ∂(ay)/∂y = ay ln a.

Step by step.

  1. ∂u/∂y = xy ln x.
  2. At (x, y) = (2, 3): 2³ ln 2 = 8 ln 2.
  3. Numerically 8 × 0.693 ≈ 5.545, which is neither 8 nor 12.

Answer: 8 ln 2 — option (A).

Common misconception. Using the power rule y xy−1, which is the derivative with respect to x and gives 3 · 2² = 12 (option B), or ignoring the logarithmic factor and giving 8 (option C). The variable being differentiated decides which rule applies.

EM-U3C-Q15MCQhard

Let f(x, y) = xy(x² − y²)/(x² + y²) for (x, y) ≠ (0, 0), and f(0, 0) = 0. Then fxy(0, 0) equals

  1. −1
  2. 1
  3. 0
  4. It does not exist
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. A mixed partial at the origin must be computed from its definition: fxy(0, 0) = limk→0 [fx(0, k) − fx(0, 0)]/k. That requires fx(0, y), which is itself a difference quotient in x. This function is the standard counterexample showing that fxy ≠ fyx is possible when the mixed partials are not continuous.

Step by step.

  1. For y ≠ 0: f(h, y)/h = y(h² − y²)/(h² + y²), so fx(0, y) = limh→0 y(h² − y²)/(h² + y²) = −y.
  2. Also fx(0, 0) = limh→0 f(h, 0)/h = 0.
  3. Therefore fxy(0, 0) = limk→0 (−k − 0)/k = −1.
  4. Reversing the order gives fy(x, 0) = x and fyx(0, 0) = +1, confirming the two mixed partials differ.

Answer: −1 — option (A).

Common misconception. Invoking Clairaut's theorem and reporting 1 (option B) or assuming both orders agree and giving 0 (option C). Clairaut guarantees equality only when the mixed partials are continuous at the point, and here they are not; the two orders differ by sign.

EM-U3C-Q19MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about partial derivatives are TRUE?

  1. If both partial derivatives of f exist at (a, b), then f is continuous at (a, b).
  2. If f is differentiable at (a, b), then both partial derivatives exist at (a, b).
  3. If both partial derivatives exist and are continuous in a neighbourhood of (a, b), then f is differentiable at (a, b).
  4. If fxy and fyx both exist at (a, b), then they are equal there.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. For functions of two variables, existence of the partials is weaker than continuity, and continuity of the partials is what upgrades "partials exist" to "differentiable". Differentiability is the strong hypothesis from which the partials follow.

Step by step.

  1. Option (A). FALSE. Take f(x, y) = xy/(x² + y²) away from the origin and f(0, 0) = 0. Both partials exist at the origin (each is 0 along an axis), yet f is unbounded near the origin and so not continuous there.
  2. Option (B). TRUE. Differentiability means f(a + h, b + k) − f(a, b) = Ah + Bk + o(√(h² + k²)), and taking k = 0 shows fx(a, b) = A exists (similarly fy).
  3. Option (C). TRUE. This is the standard sufficient condition: continuous partials on a neighbourhood give the linear approximation above.
  4. Option (D). FALSE. Existence of both mixed partials does not force equality; continuity of them does (Clairaut). The function xy(x² − y²)/(x² + y²) has fxy(0, 0) = −1 but fyx(0, 0) = +1.

Answer: Options (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Transferring the one-variable intuition — "derivative exists implies continuity" — to partial derivatives (option A). In two variables the two partials probe only two directions, and behaviour off those directions is unconstrained.

EM-U3C-Q20MSQhard

Let f(x, y) be differentiable at the point P and let u be a unit vector. Which of the following statements are TRUE?

  1. The directional derivative of f at P in the direction u equals ∇f(P) · u.
  2. The largest value the directional derivative can take at P is |∇f(P)|.
  3. ∇f(P) is orthogonal to the level curve of f through P.
  4. If ∇f(P) = 0, then P is a local extremum of f.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Differentiability at P gives the linear approximation f(P + tu) = f(P) + t ∇f(P) · u + o(t), and every statement about directional derivatives follows from that one line.

Step by step.

  1. Option (A). TRUE by the definition of the directional derivative, Duf(P) = limt→0 [f(P + tu) − f(P)]/t = ∇f(P) · u.
  2. Option (B). TRUE. ∇f(P) · u = |∇f(P)| cos θ with θ the angle between the two vectors; the maximum is |∇f(P)|, attained when u points along the gradient.
  3. Option (C). TRUE. Along a level curve f is constant, so Duf = ∇f · u = 0 for every tangent direction u.
  4. Option (D). FALSE. A vanishing gradient is only a necessary condition. f(x, y) = x² − y² has ∇f(0, 0) = 0, but the origin is a saddle point: f > 0 along the x-axis and f < 0 along the y-axis.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Treating "stationary point" and "extremum" as synonyms (option D). In one variable a zero derivative leaves only a few possibilities; in two variables the saddle is a genuine third case, detected by the discriminant D = fxxfyy − fxy² being negative.

EM-U3C-Q24NATeasy

Let f(x, y) = exy. The value of fxy at the point (0, 1) is ____.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 1

Detailed solution

Concept. Differentiate once with respect to x and then once with respect to y, applying the product and chain rules. For this smooth function the order does not matter.

Step by step.

  1. fx = y exy.
  2. fxy = ∂/∂y [y exy] = exy + xy exy = exy(1 + xy).
  3. At (0, 1): e⁰(1 + 0) = 1.

Answer: 1 (exact integer).

Common misconception. Dropping the product-rule term and answering exy = 1 for the wrong reason, or multiplying the two partials fxfy. The mixed partial is a derivative of a derivative, not a product of derivatives.

Unit 4 — Calculus: Maxima, Minima & Integrals 75
Single-variable extrema 22
EM-U4-Q01MCQeasy

The function f(x) = x³ − 3x has critical points at x = −1 and x = 1. Which one of the following classifies them correctly?

  1. Local maximum at x = −1, local minimum at x = 1.
  2. Local minimum at x = −1, local maximum at x = 1.
  3. Local maximum at both points.
  4. Neither point is an extremum; both are points of inflection.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. The sign of the second derivative at a stationary point decides between a local maximum and a local minimum.

Step by step.

  1. Differentiate: f′(x) = 3x² − 3 = 3(x − 1)(x + 1), so the stationary points are x = −1 and x = 1.
  2. Differentiate again: f″(x) = 6x.
  3. At x = −1: f″(−1) = −6 < 0, so the curve is concave down and the point is a local maximum.
  4. At x = 1: f″(1) = 6 > 0, so the curve is concave up and the point is a local minimum.
  5. Check the values: f(−1) = 2 and f(1) = −2, consistent with the classification.

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Applying the first-derivative test sloppily and assuming the left critical point is always the minimum. The test is about the sign of f″ at each point, not about position.

EM-U4-Q02MCQeasy

A function f is twice differentiable at x = a, with f′(a) = 0 and f″(a) . Then at x = a, f has

  1. a local maximum.
  2. a local minimum.
  3. a point of inflection.
  4. no extremum, since only the first derivative is known to vanish.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. The second-derivative test: at a stationary point, negative second derivative means concave down, hence a local maximum.

Step by step.

  1. A vanishing first derivative makes x = a a stationary point, so the tangent is horizontal there.
  2. f″(a) < 0 means f′ is decreasing through a, so f′ changes from positive to negative.
  3. Values of f therefore rise to f(a) and fall after it: a local maximum.

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Confusing concave down with a minimum because the curve 'opens downward' and the point 'looks low'. Concave down at a stationary point is always a maximum.

EM-U4-Q03NATmedium

The largest value attained by f(x) = x³ − 3x on the closed interval [−2, 2] is ______. (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Answer: 2

Detailed solution

Concept. On a closed interval the extreme values are attained at critical points inside the interval or at the endpoints, so all candidates must be evaluated.

Step by step.

  1. Critical points from f′(x) = 3x² − 3 = 0: x = −1 and x = 1, both inside [−2, 2].
  2. Evaluate at every candidate: f(−2) = −8 + 6 = −2, f(−1) = −1 + 3 = 2, f(1) = 1 − 3 = −2, f(2) = 8 − 6 = 2.
  3. The largest of {−2, 2, −2, 2} is 2.
  4. It is attained twice: at the interior point x = −1 and at the endpoint x = 2.

Answer: 2

Common misconception. Reporting only the interior maximum and ignoring the endpoints, or assuming the maximum must be unique. Here the endpoint ties with the interior critical point.

EM-U4-Q04MSQmedium

Let f be continuous on the closed interval [a, b] and differentiable on (a, b). Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Any extreme value of f on [a, b] is attained either at a critical point in (a, b) or at an endpoint.
  2. If f′(c) = 0 for some c ∈ (a, b), then f has a local extremum at c.
  3. f always attains both a maximum and a minimum on [a, b].
  4. If f′(x) > 0 for every x in (a, b), then the maximum of f on [a, b] is f(b).
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. The extreme value theorem guarantees attainment; Fermat's theorem says an interior extremum forces a zero derivative; monotonicity locates the extremes at the endpoints.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true: an interior extremum of a differentiable function forces f′ = 0, and if there is none inside, the extremum sits at an endpoint.
  2. (B) is false: f(x) = x³ has f′(0) = 0, but x = 0 is neither a maximum nor a minimum, because f′ does not change sign there.
  3. (C) is true by the extreme value theorem, since a continuous function on a closed bounded interval attains its bounds.
  4. (D) is true: a strictly increasing function on [a, b] has its largest value at the right endpoint.
  5. So (A), (C) and (D) hold.

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D)

Common misconception. Option (B) is the standard trap: a vanishing derivative is necessary for an interior extremum but not sufficient. Always check the sign change of f′.

EM-U4-Q05MCQeasy

The minimum value of f(x) = x² − 4x + 7 over all real x is

  1. 3
  2. 2
  3. 4
  4. 7
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. A quadratic with a positive leading coefficient is a parabola opening upward; its vertex gives the global minimum.

Step by step.

  1. Complete the square: x² − 4x + 7 = (x − 2)² + 3.
  2. The squared term is never negative, so f(x) ≥ 3 for all x.
  3. Equality holds at x = 2, so the minimum value is 3.
  4. Check with the derivative: f′(x) = 2x − 4 = 0 gives x = 2 and f(2) = 3.

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Reading the constant term 7 as the minimum, or reporting the location x = 2 instead of the value 3.

EM-U4-Q06MSQmedium

Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. f(x) = x³ has f′(0) = 0, and x = 0 is not a local extremum of f.
  2. If f″ changes sign at c, then c is a point of inflection.
  3. If f″(c) = 0, then c is a point of inflection.
  4. If f has a local maximum at c and f′(c) exists, then f′(c) = 0.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Concavity is read from the sign of the second derivative; a change of sign is what makes an inflection point, not a single zero value.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true: f′(x) = 3x² vanishes at 0 and is positive on both sides, so f is increasing throughout and 0 is not an extremum.
  2. (B) is true: the sign of f″ is exactly the concavity, and a change of concavity at a point of the curve is an inflection point.
  3. (C) is false: f(x) = x⁴ has f″(0) = 0 but f″(x) = 12x² ≥ 0 everywhere, so the curve is concave up on both sides of 0 and 0 is a minimum, not an inflection point.
  4. (D) is true by Fermat's theorem: at an interior local extremum of a differentiable function the tangent is horizontal.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Common misconception. Option (C) is the usual error — treating f″(c) = 0 as sufficient. x⁴ at the origin is the standard counterexample.

EM-U4-Q07MCQhard

Let f(x) = x⁴ − 4x³. Which one of the following is TRUE?

  1. Local minimum at x = 3, and points of inflection at x = 0 and x = 2.
  2. Local maximum at x = 0 and local minimum at x = 3.
  3. Local minima at x = 0 and x = 3.
  4. f has no local extremum.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Stationary points come from f′ = 0; their type comes from the sign of f″, and an inflection point needs a sign change of f″.

Step by step.

  1. f′(x) = 4x³ − 12x² = 4x²(x − 3), so the stationary points are x = 0 (double root) and x = 3.
  2. f′(x) has the sign of x − 3 away from 0: negative for x < 3 and positive for x > 3, so f decreases to x = 3 and increases after it — a local minimum there.
  3. At x = 0 the derivative does not change sign, so 0 is not an extremum.
  4. f″(x) = 12x² − 24x = 12x(x − 2), which changes sign at x = 0 and at x = 2.
  5. Hence the points of inflection are x = 0 and x = 2, and the only extremum is the minimum at x = 3.

Answer: Option (A)
