Common misconception. Treating every root of f′ = 0 as an extremum, so the double root at 0 is mistaken for a maximum or a minimum. A repeated root of the derivative with no sign change gives a flat inflection point.

EM-U4-Q26MCQeasy

The function f(x) = x³ − 6x² + 9x has critical points at x = 1 and x = 3. Which one of the following classifies them correctly?

  1. Local maximum at x = 1, local minimum at x = 3.
  2. Local minimum at x = 1, local maximum at x = 3.
  3. Local maximum at both points.
  4. Local minimum at both points.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. A critical point is a solution of f′(x) = 0. The second-derivative test reads the sign of f″ there: f″ < 0 means the curve is concave down, so the point is a local maximum; f″ > 0 means concave up, so a local minimum.

Step by step.

  1. f′(x) = 3x² − 12x + 9 = 3(x − 1)(x − 3), so the critical points are x = 1 and x = 3.
  2. f″(x) = 6x − 12.
  3. At x = 1: f″(1) = −6 < 0, so x = 1 is a local maximum.
  4. At x = 3: f″(3) = 6 > 0, so x = 3 is a local minimum.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Reading the second-derivative test backwards, or using the sign of f′ at the point instead of the sign change of f′ around it. At a critical point f′ is zero by definition, so its value there carries no information; only f″ or a sign change of f′ does.

EM-U4-Q27MCQeasy

The minimum value of f(x) = x² + 6x + 2 over all real x is

  1. 2
  2. −9
  3. −7
  4. 7
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Completing the square turns a quadratic into a(x − h)² + k. For a > 0 the squared term is never negative, so the least value is k, attained at x = h.

Step by step.

  1. x² + 6x + 2 = (x² + 6x + 9) − 9 + 2 = (x + 3)² − 7.
  2. Since (x + 3)² ≥ 0 for every real x, f(x) ≥ −7.
  3. Equality holds at x = −3, so the minimum value is −7.

Answer: Option (C) — −7.

Common misconception. Quoting the constant term 2 as the minimum, or forgetting to subtract (b/2)² = 9 after adding it. The vertex ordinate is c − b²/4a, not c.

EM-U4-Q28MCQmedium

Let f(x) = x³ − 3x² + 3x − 2. Which one of the following is TRUE?

  1. f has a local maximum at x = 1.
  2. f has a local minimum at x = 1.
  3. f′(1) = 0, yet f has neither a local maximum nor a local minimum at x = 1.
  4. f has a local maximum at x = 1 and a local minimum at x = 3.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. A vanishing first derivative is necessary but not sufficient for an extremum. The point is a genuine extremum only when f′ changes sign there; if f′ keeps its sign, the point is a horizontal point of inflection.

Step by step.

  1. f′(x) = 3x² − 6x + 3 = 3(x − 1)², which vanishes only at x = 1.
  2. For every x ≠ 1, 3(x − 1)² > 0. So f′ ≥ 0 on both sides of x = 1 and does not change sign.
  3. With no sign change of f′, f is increasing across x = 1; the point is a point of inflection, not an extremum.
  4. The second derivative f″(x) = 6(x − 1) gives f″(1) = 0, so the second-derivative test is inconclusive — it is the sign-change argument that settles the question.

Answer: Option (C).

Common misconception. Concluding "extremum" from f′(1) = 0 alone. A repeated root of f′ (here of multiplicity two) produces a horizontal inflection. Also note that a zero second derivative is not evidence of an inflection — it only means the second-derivative test fails.

EM-U4-Q29MCQmedium

The function f(x) = x³ − 3x² is concave upward on

  1. (−∞, 1)
  2. (1, ∞)
  3. (−1, 1)
  4. (−∞, ∞)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Concavity is decided by the sign of the second derivative on an interval: f″ > 0 gives concave up (convex), f″ < 0 gives concave down.

Step by step.

  1. f′(x) = 3x² − 6x and f″(x) = 6x − 6.
  2. Solve 6x − 6 > 0, giving x > 1.
  3. So f is concave upward exactly on (1, ∞), and concave downward on (−∞, 1).

Answer: Option (B) — (1, ∞).

Common misconception. Reading concavity off f′, or taking the sign interval of f′ (which describes where f rises and falls) instead of that of f″. Increasing and concave upward are independent properties.

EM-U4-Q30MCQmedium

Let f(x) = 2x³ − 3x² − 12x + 5. At which value of x does f have a local maximum?

  1. x = 2
  2. x = 1
  3. x = −2
  4. x = −1
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Solve f′(x) = 0 for the critical points, then classify each one with the sign of f″: negative gives a local maximum, positive a local minimum.

Step by step.

  1. f′(x) = 6x² − 6x − 12 = 6(x² − x − 2) = 6(x − 2)(x + 1), so the critical points are x = 2 and x = −1.
  2. f″(x) = 12x − 6.
  3. f″(−1) = −18 < 0, so x = −1 is a local maximum.
  4. f″(2) = 18 > 0, so x = 2 is a local minimum.

Answer: Option (D) — x = −1.

Common misconception. Stopping at the critical points and reporting the local minimum instead, or assuming the left-hand critical point is always the minimum. Which one is the maximum depends on the sign of f″, so it has to be computed.

EM-U4-Q31MSQeasy

Let f(x) = x³ on the closed interval [−1, 2]. Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. f has no local extremum in the open interval (−1, 2).
  2. The maximum value of f on [−1, 2] is 8.
  3. The minimum value of f on [−1, 2] is 0.
  4. f has a local minimum at x = 0 because f′(0) = 0.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A) and (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. On a closed interval the extreme values are attained either at a critical point inside or at an endpoint. A zero derivative is only a candidate: f(x) = x³ shows a critical point with no extremum.

Step by step.

  1. f′(x) = 3x² vanishes only at x = 0, and 3x² > 0 on both sides of 0. With no sign change, f is increasing throughout (−1, 2), so it has no local extremum there. Statement (A) is true.
  2. Increasing on the whole interval means the extreme values sit at the endpoints: f(−1) = −1 and f(2) = 8. The maximum is 8, so (B) is true.
  3. The minimum is f(−1) = −1, not 0, so (C) is false.
  4. Since f increases through x = 0, there is no local minimum there; the vanishing derivative is necessary, not sufficient. (D) is false.

Answer: Options (A) and (B).

Common misconception. Treating f′(0) = 0 as proof of an extremum, and forgetting the endpoints when the interval is closed. The minimum of x³ on [−1, 2] is at the left endpoint, not at the critical point.

EM-U4-Q32MCQhard

Let f(x) = x³ + ax² + bx + c have a local maximum at x = −1 and a local minimum at x = 3. Then a + b equals

  1. −12
  2. 12
  3. −6
  4. 6
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. If a cubic has critical points at p and q, then f′(x) = 3(x − p)(x − q). Matching coefficients of f′(x) = 3x² + 2ax + b determines a and b.

Step by step.

  1. f′(x) = 3x² + 2ax + b must vanish at x = −1 and x = 3, so f′(x) = 3(x + 1)(x − 3) = 3x² − 6x − 9.
  2. Comparing the linear terms: 2a = −6, so a = −3.
  3. Comparing the constants: b = −9.
  4. Hence a + b = −3 − 9 = −12. (Consistency check: f″(x) = 6x − 6 gives f″(−1) = −12 < 0, a maximum, and f″(3) = 12 > 0, a minimum, as required.)

Answer: Option (A) — −12.

Common misconception. Setting f′(−1) = 0 and f′(3) = 0 but then solving carelessly: the coefficient of x² in f′ is 2a, not a, and the constant term is b only after the leading 3 is distributed. Dropping that factor gives a = −6, b = −9.

EM-U4-Q33NAThard

The maximum value attained by f(x) = x³ − 12x on the closed interval [−3, 3] is ______. (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Answer: 16

Detailed solution

Concept. For a continuous function on a closed interval, the maximum is attained at a critical point inside the interval or at an endpoint. All candidates must be evaluated.

Step by step.

  1. f′(x) = 3x² − 12 = 3(x − 2)(x + 2), so the critical points are x = −2 and x = 2, both inside [−3, 3].
  2. Evaluate at the critical points: f(−2) = −8 + 24 = 16 and f(2) = 8 − 24 = −16.
  3. Evaluate at the endpoints: f(−3) = −27 + 36 = 9 and f(3) = 27 − 36 = −9.
  4. The largest of {16, −16, 9, −9} is 16.

Answer: 16 (an integer; tolerance 10⁻⁹).

Common misconception. Reporting the local maximum of 16 without checking the endpoints — here that is right, but the endpoint f(−3) = 9 is close enough that a different interval would flip the answer. The opposite error is to give 27 from x³ alone, ignoring the −12x term.

EM-U4C-Q01MCQeasy

For x > 0, the function f(x) = x + 4/x attains its minimum value at exactly one point of its domain. That minimum value is

  1. 4
  2. 2
  3. 2√2
  4. The function has no minimum on x > 0
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. A differentiable function on an interval is minimised where its derivative vanishes and changes sign from negative to positive. The arithmetic–geometric mean inequality gives the same answer without any differentiation.

Step by step.

  1. Differentiate: f′(x) = 1 − 4/x².
  2. Set f′(x) = 0: x² = 4, so x = 2 on the stated domain x > 0.
  3. The second derivative is f″(x) = 8/x³ > 0 for x > 0, so x = 2 gives a minimum.
  4. f(2) = 2 + 2 = 4. As x → 0⁺ and as x → ∞, f(x) → ∞, so 4 is the global minimum, not merely a local one.
  5. One-line check by AM–GM: x + 4/x ≥ 2√(x · 4/x) = 4, with equality exactly when x = 4/x, that is x = 2.

Answer: 4 — option (A).

Common misconception. Reporting 2, the point where the minimum occurs, instead of 4, the minimum value. A second slip is to conclude that no minimum exists because f grows without bound — but it does so only as x → 0⁺ and as x → ∞, so a minimum is attained in between.

EM-U4C-Q02MCQeasy

For x ≥ 0, the maximum value of f(x) = x²e−x is

  1. 4e−2
  2. 2e−2
  3. 4e−1
  4. e−2
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. A product of a polynomial and an exponential is differentiated by the product rule. The exponential factor is never zero, so the critical points come from the polynomial factor alone; the stated domain is what decides whether a maximum exists at all.

Step by step.

  1. f′(x) = 2x e−x − x² e−x = x(2 − x) e−x.
  2. Since e−x > 0 for every x, f′(x) = 0 only at x = 0 and x = 2.
  3. f(0) = 0 and f(2) = 4e−2 ≈ 0.541. As x → ∞, f(x) → 0, so x = 2 gives the maximum on [0, ∞).
  4. The domain matters: on the whole real line x²e−x → ∞ as x → −∞, so no maximum exists there.

Answer: 4e−2 — option (A).

Common misconception. Dropping the exponential factor and answering 4, or differentiating incorrectly to x(1 − x) and using x = 1. The other trap is forgetting the stated domain: x ≥ 0 is exactly what makes the maximum exist.

EM-U4C-Q03NATeasy

The number of local extrema of f(x) = |x² − 4| on the whole real line is ______.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 3

Detailed solution

Concept. An absolute value changes formula where its argument vanishes. The candidate extrema are therefore the zeros of the derivative on each smooth piece, together with the corner points where the derivative does not exist.

Step by step.

  1. Split the line: f(x) = 4 − x² on [−2, 2] and f(x) = x² − 4 outside it.
  2. On (−2, 2), f′(x) = −2x, which vanishes only at x = 0; there f(0) = 4, a local maximum.
  3. Outside [−2, 2], f′(x) = 2x is never zero, so there is no further stationary point.
  4. At x = ±2 the graph has corners: f = 0 there and f > 0 on both sides of each, so both points are local minima.
  5. The local extrema are at x = −2, x = 0 and x = 2: three in all.

Answer: 3 (exact integer).

Common misconception. Counting only the two corners, or only the stationary point at 0. A related error is to differentiate |x² − 4| formally as 2x and to call x = ±2 stationary points — the derivative does not exist there, which is precisely why they are extrema.

EM-U4C-Q04MCQmedium

Let f(x) = x⁴. Which one of the following statements about the point x = 0 is TRUE?

  1. f′(0) = 0 and f″(0) = 0, and 0 is a strict local minimum.
  2. f′(0) = 0 and f″(0) = 0, and 0 is not a local extremum.
  3. f′(0) = 0 and f″(0) = 12 > 0, so the second-derivative test gives a local minimum.
  4. f″(0) = 0 forces an inflection point at x = 0.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. The second-derivative test is a sufficient test only. When f′(c) = 0 and f″(c) = 0 it is inconclusive, and the definition of a local minimum has to be applied directly.

Step by step.

  1. f′(x) = 4x³ and f″(x) = 12x², so f′(0) = 0 and f″(0) = 0.
  2. The second-derivative test therefore says nothing at x = 0; it neither confirms nor excludes an extremum.
  3. Go back to the definition: f(x) = x⁴ ≥ 0 = f(0) for every real x, so 0 is a strict minimum — in fact the global one.
  4. f″(x) = 12x² ≥ 0 on both sides of 0, so the sign of concavity never changes and there is no inflection point.

Answer: f′(0) = 0 and f″(0) = 0, and 0 is a strict local minimum — option (A).

Common misconception. Concluding "not an extremum" or "an inflection point" from f″(c) = 0. That value makes the test inconclusive and nothing more: x⁴ has a minimum, x³ has an inflection and −x⁴ has a maximum, all with f′(0) = f″(0) = 0.

EM-U4C-Q05MSQmedium

Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. A function that is continuous on an open interval need not attain a maximum on that interval.
  2. If f′(c) = 0 and f″(c) = 0, then c is not a local extremum of f.
  3. If f is differentiable on ℝ and f′(x) ≠ 0 for every real x, then f is strictly monotone on ℝ.
  4. A differentiable function can have a local maximum at c without f″(c) < 0.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Extreme-value questions depend on the domain, and stationarity questions depend on which derivatives actually exist. Every option here has to be checked against a definition or a theorem rather than against intuition.

Step by step.

  1. (A) TRUE. f(x) = x is continuous on (0, 1) and unbounded there, so it attains no maximum; the extreme-value theorem needs a closed bounded interval.
  2. (B) FALSE. f(x) = x⁴ has f′(0) = f″(0) = 0 and a strict local minimum at 0, as Question 4 shows.
  3. (C) TRUE. A derivative has the intermediate-value property (Darboux), so f′ cannot change sign without passing through 0. Since f′ never vanishes it keeps one sign throughout ℝ, and f is strictly monotone.
  4. (D) TRUE. f(x) = −x⁴ has a local maximum at 0 with f″(0) = 0, and for f(x) = −x4/3 the second derivative does not even exist at 0.

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D).

Common misconception. Treating f″(c) = 0 as a disqualification in (B), and assuming that a local maximum must have f″ < 0 in (D). Only the sufficient direction of the second-derivative test is valid; neither of its conclusions can be reversed.

EM-U4C-Q06MCQmedium

A rectangle is inscribed in a circle of radius 5. The largest possible area of the rectangle is

  1. 25
  2. 50
  3. 50√2
  4. 100
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. A constrained optimisation in one variable: express the area in terms of one half-side, use the constraint to eliminate the other, and differentiate on the admissible interval. The arithmetic–geometric mean inequality gives the same maximum in one step.

Step by step.

  1. Let the rectangle have sides 2x and 2y. Its diagonal is a diameter of the circle, so x² + y² = 25.
  2. The area is A = 4xy, so maximising A means maximising xy subject to x² + y² = 25.
  3. AM–GM: xy ≤ (x² + y²)/2 = 25/2, with equality when x = y. Hence A ≤ 4 · 25/2 = 50.
  4. The bound is attained by the square of diagonal 10, whose side is 5√2 and whose area is (5√2)² = 50.
  5. Calculus check: A(x) = 4x√(25 − x²) has A′(x) = 0 at x² = 25/2, giving A = 50 again.

Answer: 50 — option (B).

Common misconception. Maximising the diagonal, which is fixed at 10, or its square, 100. Another slip is to use x² + y² = 5 and lose the radius, which returns 25. The largest inscribed rectangle is always the square, and its area is half the square of the diagonal.

EM-U4C-Q07MSQhard

Let f(x) = x1/x for x > 0. Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. f attains its maximum at x = e.
  2. f is increasing on (0, e) and decreasing on (e, ∞).
  3. f(x) → e as x → ∞.
  4. The maximum value of f is e1/e.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. A variable exponent is handled by taking logarithms: ln f = (ln x)/x, whose derivative decides where f grows and where it decays. Because ln is increasing, f and ln f have their maxima at the same point.

Step by step.

  1. ln f(x) = (ln x)/x for x > 0.
  2. Differentiate: (ln f)′ = (1 − ln x)/x².
  3. This vanishes only at x = e; it is positive for x < e and negative for x > e, so f increases on (0, e) and decreases on (e, ∞).
  4. Hence the maximum is attained at x = e, with value f(e) = e1/e ≈ 1.4447.
  5. As x → ∞, (ln x)/x → 0, so f(x) = e(ln x)/x → e⁰ = 1. The function therefore approaches 1, not e.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Option (C) is the trap: the limit is 1, and e is where the maximum is attained, not what the function tends to. A second error is to differentiate x1/x with the power rule, which is valid only for a constant exponent.

Multivariable extrema & saddle points 17
EM-U4-Q08MCQeasy

At a stationary point of f(x, y) let D = fxx fyy − (fxy)². If D > 0 and fxx > 0, then the point is

  1. a local minimum.
  2. a local maximum.
  3. a saddle point.
  4. undetermined; a higher-order test is needed.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. The second-derivative test for two variables uses the discriminant D to decide between an extremum and a saddle, and the sign of fxx to decide which extremum.

Step by step.

  1. D > 0 means the quadratic form of second derivatives is definite, so the point is a strict extremum.
  2. The sign of fxx gives the type: positive means the surface curves upward in every direction.
  3. Hence the point is a local minimum; fxx < 0 with D > 0 would give a maximum, and D < 0 a saddle point.
  4. D = 0 is the case in which the test is inconclusive.

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Swapping the roles: believing D > 0 alone identifies a minimum. It only says 'extremum'; the sign of fxx decides which.

EM-U4-Q09NATeasy

The minimum value of f(x, y) = x² + y² − 4x − 6y + 13 is ______. (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Answer: 0

Detailed solution

Concept. Completing squares turns a quadratic in two variables into a sum of squares plus a constant, which exposes the minimum immediately.

Step by step.

  1. Group the terms: (x² − 4x) + (y² − 6y) + 13.
  2. Complete each square: x² − 4x = (x − 2)² − 4 and y² − 6y = (y − 3)² − 9.
  3. So f = (x − 2)² + (y − 3)² + (−4 − 9 + 13) = (x − 2)² + (y − 3)².
  4. A sum of squares is never negative, so f ≥ 0, with equality exactly at (2, 3).
  5. Check with the gradient: fx = 2x − 4 = 0 and fy = 2y − 6 = 0 give the same point.

Answer: 0

Common misconception. Answering 13 by reading off the constant term, or 4 and 9 by forgetting that the completed squares bring their own negative constants.

EM-U4-Q10MCQmedium

For f(x, y) = x² − y² the second-derivative test at the origin gives

  1. a local minimum.
  2. a local maximum.
  3. a saddle point.
  4. an inconclusive result, since D = 0.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. The sign of D = fxxfyy − (fxy)² at a stationary point decides between an extremum and a saddle.

Step by step.

  1. fx = 2x and fy = −2y both vanish at the origin, so it is a stationary point.
  2. fxx = 2, fyy = −2, fxy = 0.
  3. So D = (2)(−2) − 0² = −4 < 0.
  4. A negative discriminant means the surface rises in one direction and falls in another: a saddle point.
  5. Directly: along y = 0 the function is x² (increasing away from 0) and along x = 0 it is −y² (decreasing).

Answer: Option (C)

Common misconception. Marking 'inconclusive' because one second derivative is negative. Inconclusive is D = 0, not D < 0.

EM-U4-Q11MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about the second-derivative test for f(x, y) are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. D at a stationary point means a saddle point.
  2. D > 0 together with fxx means a local maximum.
  3. D = 0 means the point is certainly not an extremum.
  4. The test is applied only at points where both first partial derivatives vanish.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. The test is a statement about the quadratic form of second derivatives at a stationary point; its degenerate case D = 0 carries no information.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true: with D < 0 the second-order form is indefinite, so the surface goes both up and down through the point.
  2. (B) is true: definite with a negative fxx means the surface curves downward in every direction.
  3. (C) is false: D = 0 leaves the test inconclusive. For f = x⁴ + y⁴ the origin has D = 0 and is a minimum; for f = x⁴ − y⁴ the origin has D = 0 and is a saddle point.
  4. (D) is true: away from a stationary point the first-order terms dominate, so the second-order test says nothing.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Common misconception. Option (C) is the standard over-reading of the degenerate case. D = 0 is not a verdict.

EM-U4-Q12NAThard

For f(x, y) = x³ + y³ − 3xy, the number of stationary points that are saddle points is ______. (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Answer: 1

Detailed solution

Concept. Find every stationary point from the two first-order conditions, then apply the second-derivative test at each one separately.

Step by step.

  1. fx = 3x² − 3y = 0 gives y = x²; fy = 3y² − 3x = 0 gives x = y².
  2. Substituting: x = (x²)² = x⁴, so x(x³ − 1) = 0 and x = 0 or x = 1.
  3. The stationary points are (0, 0) and (1, 1).
  4. Second derivatives: fxx = 6x, fyy = 6y, fxy = −3, so D = 36xy − 9.
  5. At (0, 0): D = −9 < 0, a saddle point. At (1, 1): D = 27 > 0 and fxx = 6 > 0, a local minimum.
  6. Exactly one of the two stationary points is a saddle point.

Answer: 1

Common misconception. Counting both stationary points, or stopping after (0, 0). The test must be applied at each point separately, and it can give a different verdict at each.

EM-U4-Q13MCQhard

The maximum value of x + y subject to the constraint x² + y² = 1 is

  1. 1
  2. √2
  3. 2
  4. 2√2
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. A constrained extremum is found with Lagrange multipliers, or directly from the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality.

Step by step.

  1. Lagrange: ∇(x + y) = λ∇(x² + y²) gives 1 = 2λx and 1 = 2λy, so x = y.
  2. The constraint then reads 2x² = 1, so x = y = ±1/√2.
  3. At (1/√2, 1/√2) the value is 2/√2 = √2; at the opposite point it is −√2.
  4. The maximum is therefore √2 ≈ 1.414.
  5. Shortcut: by Cauchy–Schwarz, (x + y)² ≤ 2(x² + y²) = 2, so |x + y| ≤ √2, with equality at x = y.

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Answering 2 by taking the maximum of x² + y² instead of x + y, or 1 by assuming the maximum is attained at (1, 0).

EM-U4-Q34MCQeasy

At a stationary point of f(x, y), let D = fxx·fyy − (fxy)². If D < 0, then the stationary point is

  1. a local minimum.
  2. a local maximum.
  3. a saddle point.
  4. undetermined; further tests are needed.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. The second-derivative test for two variables uses D = fxxfyy − (fxy)². D > 0 with fxx > 0 is a minimum, D > 0 with fxx < 0 a maximum, D < 0 a saddle point, and D = 0 is the inconclusive case.

Step by step.

  1. D is the determinant of the Hessian matrix at the stationary point.
  2. When D < 0 the Hessian has eigenvalues of opposite signs, so the surface curves up in one direction and down in another.
  3. Such a point is therefore neither a maximum nor a minimum: it is a saddle point.
  4. Only D = 0 leaves the test inconclusive, not D < 0.

Answer: Option (C) — a saddle point.

Common misconception. Swapping the roles of D < 0 and D = 0. D < 0 is a definite verdict (saddle), while D = 0 is the only case needing a separate argument.

EM-U4-Q35NATeasy

The minimum value of f(x, y) = x² + y² + 2x − 4y + 7 is ______. (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Answer: 2

Detailed solution

Concept. Group the terms into two perfect squares, one in x and one in y. The minimum is the constant left over, because both squares are non-negative.

Step by step.

  1. x² + 2x = (x + 1)² − 1 and y² − 4y = (y − 2)² − 4.
  2. So f(x, y) = (x + 1)² + (y − 2)² − 1 − 4 + 7 = (x + 1)² + (y − 2)² + 2.
  3. Both squares are ≥ 0, so f(x, y) ≥ 2, with equality at (−1, 2).
  4. Hence the minimum value is 2.

Answer: 2 (an integer; tolerance 10⁻⁹).

Common misconception. Reporting 7 (the constant term) or 0 (the value at the origin, f(0, 0) = 7 is not even the minimum). The vertex of the paraboloid is at (−1, 2), not at the origin.

EM-U4-Q36MCQmedium

For f(x, y) = x² + xy + y², the stationary point at the origin is

  1. a local maximum.
  2. a local minimum.
  3. a saddle point.
  4. a point where the second-derivative test is inconclusive.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Compute the three second partials at the stationary point, form D = fxxfyy − (fxy)², and classify by the sign of D and of fxx.

Step by step.

  1. fx = 2x + y and fy = x + 2y, both zero only at (0, 0), so the origin is the only stationary point.
  2. fxx = 2, fyy = 2, fxy = 1.
  3. D = (2)(2) − (1)² = 3 > 0.
  4. Since D > 0 and fxx = 2 > 0, the origin is a local minimum.

Answer: Option (B) — a local minimum.

Common misconception. Forgetting the mixed term when forming D: using D = fxxfyy = 4 happens to give the same verdict here, but dropping (fxy)² changes the classification whenever fxy is large, e.g. for f = x² + 6xy + y² where D = 1 − 36 < 0 (a saddle).

EM-U4-Q37MSQmedium

Consider minimising f(x, y) = x² + y² subject to the constraint x + y = 4. Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. The constrained minimum value of f is 8.
  2. The stationary point of the Lagrangian is (x, y) = (2, 2).
  3. The constraint set is unbounded, so f has no minimum on it.
  4. At the constrained minimum, ∇f is parallel to ∇g, where g(x, y) = x + y.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Lagrange multipliers locate constrained extrema where ∇f = λ∇g. The condition says the two gradients are parallel, i.e. the level curves are tangent at the optimum.

Step by step.

  1. With g(x, y) = x + y − 4, the Lagrangian is L = x² + y² − λ(x + y − 4).
  2. ∂L/∂x = 2x − λ = 0 and ∂L/∂y = 2y − λ = 0 give x = y = λ/2.
  3. The constraint then gives 2x = 4, so x = y = 2 and λ = 4. Statement (B) is true.
  4. The constrained value is f(2, 2) = 4 + 4 = 8. Statement (A) is true.
  5. At that point ∇f = (4, 4) and ∇g = (1, 1), so ∇f = 4∇g: parallel. Statement (D) is true.
  6. The line x + y = 4 is unbounded, but f grows without limit along it while still attaining the value 8 at (2, 2). Unboundedness of the constraint set does not preclude a minimum, so (C) is false.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Inferring "no minimum" from an unbounded constraint set. Along x + y = 4, f = x² + (4 − x)² is a quadratic in x and so has a finite minimum. The other trap is substituting the constraint and forgetting that the answer is still a constrained value.

EM-U4-Q38MCQhard

For f(x, y) = x³ + y³ − 3xy, the stationary points (0, 0) and (1, 1) are respectively

  1. a local minimum and a saddle point.
  2. a saddle point and a local minimum.
  3. a saddle point and a local maximum.
  4. a local maximum and a local minimum.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Stationary points solve fx = fy = 0. Each is then classified by D = fxxfyy − (fxy)² together with the sign of fxx.

Step by step.

  1. fx = 3x² − 3y and fy = 3y² − 3x. Setting both to zero gives y = x² and x = y², hence x = x⁴, so x = 0 or x = 1, giving (0, 0) and (1, 1).
  2. fxx = 6x, fyy = 6y, fxy = −3, so D = 36xy − 9.
  3. At (0, 0): D = −9 < 0, a saddle point.
  4. At (1, 1): D = 27 > 0 and fxx = 6 > 0, a local minimum.

Answer: Option (B).

Common misconception. Assuming symmetry makes both stationary points the same type, or evaluating D at only one of them. Here D changes sign between the two points, so they must be classified separately.

EM-U4C-Q08MCQeasy

How many stationary points does f(x, y) = x² − 2xy + y² + 2x have?

  1. Exactly one
  2. Exactly two
  3. None
  4. Infinitely many
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Stationary points solve the two first-order equations simultaneously. A pair of linear equations can be inconsistent, in which case the function has no stationary point at all — even when it looks like a harmless quadratic.

Step by step.

  1. fx = 2x − 2y + 2 and fy = −2x + 2y.
  2. fx = 0 gives y = x + 1; fy = 0 gives y = x.
  3. The two requirements contradict each other, so the system has no solution and the function has no stationary point.
  4. Completing the square shows why: f = (x − y)² + 2x, which tends to −∞ along the line y = x as x → −∞. A function unbounded below along a line needs no interior critical point.

Answer: None — option (C).

Common misconception. Assuming that a quadratic in two variables must have a stationary point and answering "exactly one". "Infinitely many" tempts anyone who solves a single equation, or who sees the perfect square (x − y)² and concludes that the flat direction gives a whole line of critical points — the extra term 2x destroys that.

EM-U4C-Q09NATmedium

For f(x, y) = x³ + y³ − 3x − 3y, the number of stationary points that are saddle points is ______.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 2

Detailed solution

Concept. For a twice differentiable function of two variables the second-derivative test uses D = fxxfyy − (fxy)²: D < 0 gives a saddle point, while D > 0 gives a local extremum whose type is fixed by the sign of fxx.

Step by step.

  1. fx = 3x² − 3 = 0 gives x = ±1; fy = 3y² − 3 = 0 gives y = ±1.
  2. The four stationary points are (1, 1), (1, −1), (−1, 1) and (−1, −1).
  3. fxx = 6x, fyy = 6y, fxy = 0, so D = 36xy.
  4. At (1, 1): D = 36 > 0 and fxx = 6 > 0, a local minimum. At (−1, −1): D = 36 > 0 and fxx = −6 < 0, a local maximum.
  5. At (1, −1) and (−1, 1): D = −36 < 0, so both are saddle points.

Answer: 2 (exact integer).

Common misconception. Counting all four stationary points as extrema because each satisfies both first-order conditions — stationarity is not extremality. Another slip is to look only at the sign of fxx, which calls all four points extrema and returns 0.

EM-U4C-Q10MCQmedium

The maximum value of x²y subject to the constraint x² + y² = 3 is

  1. 9/4
  2. 2
  3. 3
  4. 3/2
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Constrained optimisation by Lagrange multipliers: at an extremum of f on the level curve g = 3 the two gradients are parallel. The multiplier equations have several branches, and every branch must be evaluated before the maximum is declared.

Step by step.

  1. ∇f = (2xy, x²) and ∇g = (2x, 2y), so the Lagrange equations are 2xy = 2λx and x² = 2λy.
  2. If x ≠ 0, the first equation gives λ = y, and the second then gives x² = 2y².
  3. Substituting into x² + y² = 3: 3y² = 3, so y = ±1 and x² = 2.
  4. The objective takes the value x²y = 2 · 1 = 2 at y = 1 and −2 at y = −1.
  5. If x = 0 then y² = 3 and x²y = 0, which is neither the largest nor the smallest value. The maximum is therefore 2.

Answer: 2 — option (B).

Common misconception. 9/4 is the maximum of x²y² and 3/2 is its square root; both come from squaring the objective, which silently discards the sign of y. The value 3 comes from ignoring the constraint altogether.

EM-U4C-Q11MSQmedium

Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. If f is differentiable and has a local extremum at an interior point (x₀, y₀), then ∇f(x₀, y₀) = 0.
  2. The Lagrange condition ∇f = λ∇g is necessary but not sufficient for a constrained extremum.
  3. If ∇f(x₀, y₀) = 0 and D = fxxfyy − (fxy)² > 0 at that point, then (x₀, y₀) is a local extremum.
  4. If ∇f(x₀, y₀) = 0 and D = 0 at that point, then (x₀, y₀) is a saddle point.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Stationarity is necessary for an interior extremum but not sufficient. The second-order behaviour, read from the Hessian through D, decides the type of a stationary point, and the degenerate case D = 0 decides nothing at all.

Step by step.

  1. (A) TRUE — this is Fermat's theorem in two variables: at an interior extremum every first partial derivative vanishes.
  2. (B) TRUE — ∇f = λ∇g holds at constrained minima and constrained maxima alike, and also at points that are neither, so it cannot by itself certify an extremum.
  3. (C) TRUE — D > 0 means the Hessian is definite at the point, so the quadratic form is positive definite (local minimum) or negative definite (local maximum) near it.
  4. (D) FALSE — D = 0 is the inconclusive case. For f = x⁴ + y⁴ the origin has D = 0 and is a strict minimum; for f = x³ + y³ it has D = 0 and is neither a maximum nor a minimum.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Treating D = 0 as a saddle point. The saddle case is D < 0, where the quadratic form is indefinite; when D = 0 the second-order information is simply insufficient and higher-order terms have to be examined.

EM-U4C-Q12MCQhard

The maximum value of f(x, y) = (x² + y²)e−(x²+y²) over the whole plane is

  1. 1
  2. 1/(2e)
  3. 4/e²
  4. 1/e
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. The function depends on x and y only through t = x² + y², so the two-variable problem collapses to maximising g(t) = te−t on [0, ∞).

Step by step.

  1. Put t = x² + y² ≥ 0. Then f = g(t) = te−t, and every t ≥ 0 is attained by some point of the plane.
  2. g′(t) = e−t(1 − t), which is positive for t < 1 and negative for t > 1.
  3. So g increases up to t = 1 and decreases afterwards, and the maximum is g(1) = 1/e.
  4. The maximum is attained on the whole circle x² + y² = 1, where f = 1/e ≈ 0.368.

Answer: 1/e — option (D).

Common misconception. 4/e² is the maximum of t²e−t and 1/(2e) that of te−2t; both come from mis-copying the exponent. The value 1 is not attained here at all, since g(0) = 0.

EM-U4C-Q13MCQhard

The minimum distance from the origin to a point of the surface xyz = 8 with x, y, z > 0 is

  1. 2
  2. 4
  3. 2√3
  4. 8
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Minimising a distance means minimising its square, which removes the square root. With the symmetric constraint xyz = 8 the Lagrange equations are symmetric, and either Lagrange multipliers or AM–GM identifies the closest point.

Step by step.

  1. Minimise x² + y² + z² subject to xyz = 8, then take the square root.
  2. Lagrange: 2x = λyz, 2y = λxz, 2z = λxy.
  3. Multiplying the first equation by x gives 2x² = λxyz = 8λ, and similarly 2y² = 2z² = 8λ. Hence x² = y² = z², and positivity forces x = y = z.
  4. Then x³ = 8, so x = y = z = 2 and x² + y² + z² = 12.
  5. The distance is √12 = 2√3 ≈ 3.46. AM–GM confirms it: x² + y² + z² ≥ 3(x²y²z²)1/3 = 3 · 4 = 12, with equality at x = y = z.

Answer: 2√3 — option (C).

Common misconception. Answering 12, the minimum of the squared distance, instead of its square root; or minimising x + y + z, whose minimum is 6. The value 8 in the options is the constraint itself, not a distance.

Definite integrals 20
EM-U4-Q14MCQeasy

∫01 x² dx equals

  1. 1/3
  2. 1/2
  3. 1
  4. 2/3
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. The fundamental theorem of calculus: integrate the power function and substitute the limits.

Step by step.

  1. An antiderivative of x² is x³/3.
  2. Evaluate between 0 and 1: 1/3 − 0 = 1/3.

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Using x³ as the antiderivative and answering 1/3 for the wrong reason, or treating the integral as the average value 1/2.

EM-U4-Q15MCQeasy

∫0π/2 sin x dx equals

  1. 0
  2. 1
  3. π/2
  4. 2
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. The antiderivative of sin x is −cos x; evaluate it at the two limits.

Step by step.

  1. An antiderivative of sin x is −cos x.
  2. Evaluate: −cos(π/2) − (−cos 0) = −0 + 1 = 1.

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Dropping the minus sign and getting −1, or integrating over [0, π] by habit and answering 2.

EM-U4-Q16MCQeasy

∫−11 x³ dx equals

  1. 0
  2. 1/2
  3. 1/4
  4. 2
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. An odd integrand integrated over an interval symmetric about the origin gives zero, because the two halves cancel.

Step by step.

  1. x³ is odd: (−x)³ = −x³, and the interval [−1, 1] is symmetric about 0.
  2. Hence ∫−11 x³ dx = 0.
  3. Check directly: x⁴/4 evaluated at 1 and −1 gives 1/4 − 1/4 = 0.

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Answering 1/2 by integrating only over [0, 1] and forgetting that the negative half cancels it.

EM-U4-Q17NATmedium

∫01 x ex dx equals ______. (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Answer: 1

Detailed solution

Concept. Integration by parts with u = x and dv = exdx reduces the integrand to a pure exponential.

Step by step.

  1. Take u = x, dv = exdx, so du = dx and v = ex.
  2. By parts: ∫ x ex dx = x ex − ∫ ex dx = x ex − ex.
  3. Evaluate between 0 and 1: (e − e) − (0 − 1) = 0 + 1 = 1.
  4. The value is exactly 1, independent of e cancelling out.

Answer: 1

Common misconception. Answering e − 1 by keeping only the x ex boundary term and dropping −∫exdx. The remaining integral must be evaluated too.

EM-U4-Q18MSQmedium

Let f and g be continuous on [a, b]. Which of the following are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. ∫ab (f + g) = ∫ab f + ∫ab g.
  2. ∫ab f = ∫ac f + ∫cb f for every c ∈ [a, b].
  3. If f is odd, then ∫−aa f = 0.
  4. ∫ab f(x) dx = f(b) − f(a) for every continuous f.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Linearity, additivity over subintervals and the odd-function symmetry are properties of the integral; the last statement confuses the integral with the antiderivative.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true: the integral is linear in the integrand.
  2. (B) is true: splitting the interval at an interior point is valid for continuous functions.
  3. (C) is true: the substitution x → −x maps one half of a symmetric interval onto the other with a sign change.
  4. (D) is false: ∫ab f = F(b) − F(a) where F is an antiderivative of f, not f itself. For f(x) = x² on [0, 1] the integral is 1/3, while f(1) − f(0) = 1.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Common misconception. Option (D) is the classic confusion between f and its antiderivative F in the fundamental theorem.

EM-U4-Q19MCQhard

Which one of the following improper integrals diverges?

  1. ∫1∞ x−2 dx
  2. ∫1∞ x−1 dx
  3. ∫0∞ e−x dx
  4. ∫01 x−1/2 dx
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. For ∫1∞ x−p dx the integral converges exactly when p > 1; at the origin the threshold is p < 1.

Step by step.

  1. (A): p = 2 > 1, so it converges, to 1.
  2. (B): p = 1, the boundary case; the antiderivative is ln x, which tends to ∞, so the integral diverges.
  3. (C): ∫0∞ e−x dx = 1, convergent.
  4. (D): the integrand behaves like x−1/2 at 0 with exponent less than 1, and the value is 2, so it converges.
  5. Only (B) diverges.

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Assuming every integrand that tends to 0 at infinity has a finite integral. 1/x → 0 but its integral still diverges, because the tail decays too slowly.

EM-U4-Q39MCQeasy

∫₀¹ (3x² + 2x) dx equals

  1. 1
  2. 3
  3. 2
  4. 4
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. The fundamental theorem of calculus: find an antiderivative and evaluate it at the upper limit minus its value at the lower limit.

Step by step.

  1. An antiderivative of 3x² + 2x is x³ + x².
  2. Evaluate: [x³ + x²]01 = (1 + 1) − (0 + 0).
  3. So the integral equals 2.

Answer: Option (C) — 2.

Common misconception. Integrating term by term but forgetting the second limit: using only the value at x = 1 gives 2 here by luck, but the same slip on ∫01 (3x² + 2x) dx over a shifted interval such as [1, 2] gives 12 instead of 10. Always subtract the lower-limit value.

EM-U4-Q40MCQeasy

∫₀^(π/2) cos x dx equals

  1. 0
  2. 1/2
  3. 2
  4. 1
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. An antiderivative of cos x is sin x; evaluate it at the limits.

Step by step.

  1. ∫ cos x dx = sin x.
  2. So the definite integral is sin(π/2) − sin 0.
  3. sin(π/2) = 1 and sin 0 = 0, giving 1.

Answer: Option (D) — 1.

Common misconception. Using the derivative instead of the antiderivative (writing −sin x), which gives −1, or evaluating cos at the limits rather than sin.

EM-U4-Q41NATeasy

∫₀² |x − 1| dx = ______. (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Answer: 1

Detailed solution

Concept. The absolute value changes formula at x = 1, so split the integral there and use additivity over subintervals.

Step by step.

  1. On [0, 1], |x − 1| = 1 − x, so ∫01 (1 − x) dx = [x − x²/2]01 = 1/2.
  2. On [1, 2], |x − 1| = x − 1, so ∫12 (x − 1) dx = [x²/2 − x]12 = (2 − 2) − (1/2 − 1) = 1/2.
  3. Adding, the integral is 1/2 + 1/2 = 1. (Geometrically: two right triangles of area 1/2 each.)

Answer: 1 (an integer; tolerance 10⁻⁹).

Common misconception. Integrating x − 1 across the whole interval without splitting, which gives 0 and treats the area below the axis as negative — impossible for an absolute value.

EM-U4-Q42MCQmedium

∫₀^π x sin x dx equals

  1. 0
  2. π
  3. π/2
  4. 2π
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Integration by parts, ∫u dv = uv − ∫v du, with u = x and dv = sin x dx — choose u so that differentiating it simplifies the integrand.

Step by step.

  1. Take u = x, dv = sin x dx, so du = dx and v = −cos x.
  2. ∫ x sin x dx = −x cos x + ∫ cos x dx = −x cos x + sin x.
  3. Evaluate from 0 to π: at π, −π cos π + sin π = π + 0 = π; at 0, 0 + 0 = 0.
  4. Hence the integral is π.

Answer: Option (B) — π.

Common misconception. Concluding 0 from the oddness of sin x. The factor x makes the integrand positive on all of (0, π), so the integral cannot vanish. Odd-symmetry shortcuts need a symmetric interval, and [0, π] is not symmetric about the origin.

EM-U4-Q43MSQmedium

Let f and g be continuous on [a, b]. Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. ∫ₐᵇ f(x) dx = −∫_bᵃ f(x) dx
  2. If f(x) ≥ 0 on [a, b], then ∫ₐᵇ f(x) dx ≥ 0
  3. ∫ₐᵇ f(x)g(x) dx = (∫ₐᵇ f(x) dx)(∫ₐᵇ g(x) dx)
  4. |∫ₐᵇ f(x) dx| ≤ ∫ₐᵇ |f(x)| dx
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. The definite integral is linear and order-preserving; it is not multiplicative. The absolute-value estimate follows from −|f| ≤ f ≤ |f|.

Step by step.

  1. Reversing the limits reverses the orientation, so ∫ab f = F(b) − F(a) = −(F(a) − F(b)) = −∫ba f. Statement (A) is true.
  2. If f ≥ 0 on [a, b], every Riemann sum is a sum of non-negative terms, so the limit ∫ab f is non-negative. Statement (B) is true.
  3. The integral is linear, not multiplicative: with f = g = x on [0, 1], ∫ f g = 1/3 while (∫ f)(∫ g) = 1/4. Statement (C) is false.
  4. Since −|f| ≤ f ≤ |f|, monotonicity gives −∫|f| ≤ ∫f ≤ ∫|f|, that is |∫f| ≤ ∫|f|. Statement (D) is true.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Assuming the integral is multiplicative over products, as it is additive. For f = g = x on [0, 1], ∫ f g = 1/3 but (∫ f)(∫ g) = 1/4. In (D) equality holds only when f keeps one sign.

EM-U4-Q44MSQhard

Which of the following improper integrals converge? (Select all that apply.)

  1. ∫₁^∞ x^(−2) dx
  2. ∫₁^∞ x^(−1/2) dx
  3. ∫₀¹ x^(−1/2) dx
  4. ∫₁^∞ x^(−1) dx
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. The p-integrals: ∫1∞ x−p dx converges exactly when p > 1, and ∫01 x−p dx converges exactly when p < 1.

Step by step.

  1. ∫1∞ x−2 dx = [−x−1]1∞ = 0 + 1 = 1, finite: converges. (A) converges.
  2. ∫1∞ x−1/2 dx = [2√x]1∞ → ∞: diverges. (B) diverges.
  3. ∫01 x−1/2 dx = [2√x]01 = 2, finite: converges. (C) converges.
  4. ∫1∞ x−1 dx = [ln x]1∞ → ∞: diverges. (D) diverges.

Answer: Options (A) and (C).

Common misconception. Applying the same exponent rule at both ends. For the infinite range the integrand must decay faster than 1/x (p > 1), whereas near 0 the singularity must be milder than 1/x (p < 1). A single exponent can therefore converge at one end and diverge at the other.

EM-U4-Q45NAThard

The value of ∫₀^(ln 2) e^(2x) dx is ______. (Enter a decimal, e.g. 0.75.)

Show answer & solution

Answer: 1.5

Detailed solution

Concept. An antiderivative of ekx is ekx/k. The upper limit ln 2 makes e2·ln 2 = 4.

Step by step.

  1. ∫ e2x dx = e2x/2.
  2. At the upper limit: e2 ln 2/2 = (eln 2)²/2 = 2²/2 = 2.
  3. At the lower limit: e0/2 = 1/2.
  4. Hence the value is 2 − 1/2 = 3/2 = 1.5.

Answer: 1.5 (tolerance 10⁻⁹).

Common misconception. Using eln 2 = 2 but forgetting to square it for e2 ln 2, which gives 1 instead of 1.5; or omitting the factor 1/2 from the antiderivative, which gives 3.

EM-U4C-Q14MCQeasy

The value of ∫₁e ln x dx is

  1. e − 1
  2. e
  3. 0
  4. 1
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Integration by parts with the logarithm as the part to be differentiated: ∫ ln x dx = x ln x − x. The definite integral is then evaluated at the two limits.

Step by step.

  1. Take u = ln x and dv = dx, so du = dx/x and v = x.
  2. ∫ ln x dx = x ln x − ∫ x · (1/x) dx = x ln x − x.
  3. At the upper limit x = e: e · 1 − e = 0.
  4. At the lower limit x = 1: 1 · 0 − 1 = −1.
  5. The value is 0 − (−1) = 1.

Answer: 1 — option (D).

Common misconception. Forgetting the −x term leaves x ln x from 1 to e, which is e — the distractor. Reporting e − 1 comes from integrating the constant 1 instead of ln x, and 0 from cancelling the two limits without evaluating them.

EM-U4C-Q15MCQeasy

The value of ∫₀π/2 cos²x dx is

  1. π/4
  2. π/2
  3. 1
  4. 2
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. A definite integral of an even power of a trigonometric function is handled with the double-angle identity, which turns cos²x into a constant plus a term that integrates to zero over a whole number of half-periods.

Step by step.

  1. Use cos²x = (1 + cos 2x)/2.
  2. ∫₀π/2 cos²x dx = ½∫₀π/2 dx + ½∫₀π/2 cos 2x dx.
  3. The first piece is ½ · π/2 = π/4.
  4. The second piece is ½ · [sin 2x / 2]₀π/2 = ½ · (0 − 0) = 0.
  5. The value is π/4 ≈ 0.785.

Answer: π/4 — option (A).

Common misconception. π/2 comes from forgetting the factor ½ in the identity, and 1 from integrating cos x instead of cos²x. A useful check: over [0, π/2], cos²x averages to ½, so the integral must be half the length of the interval.

EM-U4C-Q16NATeasy

The value of ∫₁9 dx/√x is ______.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 4

Detailed solution

Concept. A power of x integrates by raising the exponent by one and dividing by the new exponent: ∫xndx = xn+1/(n+1), which for n = −½ gives 2√x.

Step by step.

  1. Write the integrand as x−1/2.
  2. The antiderivative is x1/2/(½) = 2√x.
  3. Evaluate: 2√9 − 2√1 = 6 − 2 = 4.

Answer: 4 (exact integer).

Common misconception. Dropping the factor 2 and answering 3 − 1 = 2, the classic error of integrating x−1/2 as √x. The rule adds one to the exponent and divides by the new exponent; here the new exponent is ½, so the division doubles the result.

EM-U4C-Q17MCQmedium

The value of limn→∞ (1/n)(√(1/n) + √(2/n) + … + √(n/n)) is

  1. 1/2
  2. 3/4
  3. 2/3
  4. 1
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. A limit of a sum of the form (1/n)Σ f(k/n) is a right-endpoint Riemann sum for ∫₀¹ f(x) dx, so the sum converges to that integral.

Step by step.

  1. Write the sum as (1/n) Σk=1n √(k/n), a Riemann sum with Δx = 1/n and xk = k/n.
  2. The limit is therefore ∫₀¹ √x dx.
  3. ∫₀¹ x1/2 dx = [2/3 x3/2]₀¹ = 2/3.
  4. Numerical check at n = 4: (1/4)(0.5 + 0.7071 + 0.8660 + 1) ≈ 0.768, which lies above 2/3 ≈ 0.667 and falls towards it.

Answer: 2/3 — option (C).

Common misconception. 1/2 comes from reading the summand as k/n and integrating x; 3/4 from integrating x3/2; and 1 from treating the sum as n terms of size 1/n with summand 1. The square root has to survive into the integral.

EM-U4C-Q18MSQmedium

Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. If f is continuous and odd on [−a, a], then ∫−aa f(x) dx = 0.
  2. ∫ab f(x)g(x) dx = (∫ab f(x) dx)(∫ab g(x) dx).
  3. If f is continuous with f ≥ 0 on [a, b] and ∫ab f = 0, then f is identically zero on [a, b].
  4. The additivity rule ∫ab f = ∫ac f + ∫cb f holds only when f is continuous on [a, b].
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. The integral is linear and order-preserving, but it is not multiplicative, and additivity does not require continuity. Each option tests one of those distinctions.

Step by step.

  1. (A) TRUE — substitute x = −t in ∫−a⁰ f to get −∫₀a f, which cancels the other half exactly.
  2. (B) FALSE — take f = g = x on [0, 1]: the left side is ∫₀¹ x² = 1/3 while the right side is (1/2)(1/2) = 1/4.
  3. (C) TRUE — if f(x₀) > 0 at some point of [a, b], continuity keeps f > f(x₀)/2 on a small interval, and the integral is at least the area of that rectangle, which is positive.
  4. (D) FALSE — additivity holds whenever all three integrals exist, for instance for piecewise continuous functions; continuity is sufficient but not necessary.

Answer: Options (A) and (C).

Common misconception. Option (B) transplants the product rule to integrals, which is simply false. Option (D) overstates a hypothesis. A related trap is to think that ∫f = 0 with f ≥ 0 allows f to be positive at isolated points — for a continuous function it does not.

EM-U4C-Q19MCQhard

The improper integral ∫₀¹ ln x dx is such that

  1. it equals 1
  2. it equals −1
  3. it equals 0
  4. it diverges to −∞
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. An improper integral with a singularity at an endpoint is defined as a limit of proper integrals. A logarithm that tends to −∞ can still be integrable, because ∫ ln x dx = x ln x − x and ε ln ε → 0.

Step by step.

  1. The integrand is unbounded at 0, so by definition ∫₀¹ ln x dx = limε→0⁺ ∫ε¹ ln x dx.
  2. The antiderivative is x ln x − x.
  3. At the upper limit: 1 · 0 − 1 = −1.
  4. At the lower limit: ε ln ε − ε → 0 − 0 = 0, since ε ln ε → 0 as ε → 0⁺.
  5. The limit exists and equals −1 − 0 = −1, so the integral converges.

Answer: it equals −1 — option (B).

Common misconception. Concluding "diverges to −∞" because the integrand does: an unbounded integrand may still have a finite integral. The decisive step is ε ln ε → 0, the same limit that makes ∫₀¹ x ln x dx converge.

EM-U4C-Q20NAThard

The value of ∫₀¹ x ln x dx is ______.

Show answer & solution

Answer: −0.25

Detailed solution

Concept. Integration by parts with the logarithm differentiated and the power integrated. The endpoint x = 0 is a limit rather than a substitution, and the term x² ln x vanishes there.

Step by step.

  1. Take u = ln x and dv = x dx, so du = dx/x and v = x²/2.
  2. ∫ x ln x dx = (x²/2) ln x − ∫ (x²/2)(1/x) dx = (x²/2) ln x − x²/4.
  3. At x = 1: 0 − 1/4 = −1/4.
  4. As x → 0⁺: x² ln x → 0 and x²/4 → 0, so the lower limit contributes 0.
  5. The value is −1/4 = −0.25.

Answer: −0.25 (exact decimal).

Common misconception. Reporting +0.25 by losing the sign of the −x²/4 term, or declaring divergence because ln x → −∞. The integral is negative because ln x < 0 throughout (0, 1), which is a quick sanity check on the sign.

Vector calculus basics 16
EM-U4-Q20MCQeasy

The gradient of f(x, y) = x²y is

  1. (2xy, x²)
  2. (x², 2xy)
  3. (2x, 2y)
  4. (2xy, 2xy)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. The gradient collects the partial derivatives in the order of the variables.

Step by step.

  1. ∂f/∂x = 2xy, treating y as a constant.
  2. ∂f/∂y = x², treating x as a constant.
  3. So ∇f = (2xy, x²).

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Swapping the components, which would be the gradient of x y². Keep the order of the variables straight.

EM-U4-Q21MCQeasy

The divergence of F(x, y, z) = (x, y, z) is

  1. 0
  2. 1
  3. 3
  4. x + y + z
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Divergence is the sum of the partial derivatives of each component with respect to its own variable.

Step by step.

  1. ∂/∂x (x) = 1, ∂/∂y (y) = 1, ∂/∂z (z) = 1.
  2. Sum: 1 + 1 + 1 = 3.
  3. Geometrically the field is radially outward with constant divergence, so a unit cube gains volume at rate 3.

Answer: Option (C)

Common misconception. Answering x + y + z by differentiating each component with respect to the wrong variable, or 0 by confusing divergence with curl.

EM-U4-Q22NATmedium

The directional derivative of f(x, y) = x² + y² at the point (1, 1) in the direction of the vector (3, 4) is ______. (Enter an exact decimal value.)

Show answer & solution

Answer: 2.8

Detailed solution

Concept. The directional derivative in a unit direction u is ∇f · u; the direction vector must be normalised first.

Step by step.

  1. ∇f = (2x, 2y), so at (1, 1) we have ∇f = (2, 2).
  2. The direction (3, 4) has length √(9 + 16) = 5, so the unit vector is u = (3/5, 4/5).
  3. Then Duf = (2, 2) · (3/5, 4/5) = 6/5 + 8/5 = 14/5 = 2.8.
  4. The maximum possible directional derivative at that point is |∇f| = 2√2 ≈ 2.83, and 2.8 is just below it, as it must be.

Answer: 2.8

Common misconception. Forgetting to normalise and answering (2, 2) · (3, 4) = 14. The direction vector must be a unit vector, otherwise the result is not a rate per unit distance.

EM-U4-Q23MCQmedium

The divergence of F(x, y, z) = (x², y², z²) at the point (1, 1, 1) is

  1. 3
  2. 6
  3. 9
  4. 0
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Compute the divergence as a function first, then substitute the point.

Step by step.

  1. ∇ · F = ∂(x²)/∂x + ∂(y²)/∂y + ∂(z²)/∂z = 2x + 2y + 2z.
  2. At (1, 1, 1): 2 + 2 + 2 = 6.
  3. Note that the field is not the identity field, so the answer is not the constant 3 of the previous case.

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Answering 3 by remembering the divergence of (x, y, z), or 9 by evaluating (x + y + z)² at the point.

EM-U4-Q24MSQmedium

Which of the following statements are TRUE for a twice continuously differentiable scalar field f and vector field F? (Select all that apply.)

  1. curl(∇f) = 0.
  2. ∇ · (curl F) = 0.
  3. ∇f points in the direction in which f increases fastest.
  4. A vector field with zero curl is conservative on every domain.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. The two identities 'curl of a gradient is zero' and 'divergence of a curl is zero' follow from the equality of mixed partial derivatives; conservativeness additionally needs a simply connected domain.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true: the components of curl(∇f) are differences of mixed partials such as fxy − fyx, which vanish by continuity of the second derivatives.
  2. (B) is true: ∇ · (curl F) expands into terms that cancel pairwise in the same way.
  3. (C) is true: the directional derivative is ∇f · u, maximised when u is the unit vector along ∇f.
  4. (D) is false: the standard counterexample is F = (−y, x, 0)/(x² + y²) on the plane with the origin removed. Its curl is zero, yet its circulation around the origin is 2π, so no potential exists on that domain.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Common misconception. Option (D) drops the hypothesis 'simply connected'. Zero curl guarantees a potential only on a domain with no holes.

EM-U4-Q25MCQhard

For F(x, y, z) = (y, z, x), curl F is

  1. (1, 1, 1)
  2. (−1, −1, −1)
  3. (0, 0, 0)
  4. (z, x, y)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. The curl is (∂R/∂y − ∂Q/∂z, ∂P/∂z − ∂R/∂x, ∂Q/∂x − ∂P/∂y) for F = (P, Q, R); each component differentiates the 'wrong' pair of variables.

Step by step.

  1. Here P = y, Q = z, R = x.
  2. First component: ∂R/∂y − ∂Q/∂z = 0 − 1 = −1.
  3. Second component: ∂P/∂z − ∂R/∂x = 0 − 1 = −1.
  4. Third component: ∂Q/∂x − ∂P/∂y = 0 − 1 = −1.
  5. So curl F = (−1, −1, −1), a constant field.

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Answering (0, 0, 0) by differentiating each component with respect to its own variable, which is divergence, not curl. Curl always pairs different variables.

EM-U4-Q46MCQeasy

The gradient of f(x, y, z) = xyz is

  1. (yz, xz, xy)
  2. (x, y, z)
  3. (xy, yz, zx)
  4. (z, x, y)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. The gradient of a scalar field is the vector of its partial derivatives, ∇f = (fx, fy, fz).

Step by step.

  1. fx = yz, treating y and z as constants.
  2. fy = xz and fz = xy, by the same argument.
  3. So ∇f = (yz, xz, xy).

Answer: Option (A) — (yz, xz, xy).

Common misconception. Differentiating with respect to each variable but keeping the same variable in every component, which produces (x, y, z) — that is the gradient of (x² + y² + z²)/2, not of xyz.

EM-U4-Q47MCQmedium

The directional derivative of f(x, y) = xy at the point (2, 3) in the direction of the vector (1, 1) is

  1. 5
  2. 5/√2
  3. 5√2
  4. 1/√2
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. The directional derivative in a direction is ∇f · u, where u must be a unit vector. The raw direction vector has to be normalised first.

Step by step.

  1. ∇f = (y, x), so at (2, 3) we have ∇f = (3, 2).
  2. Normalise: ‖(1, 1)‖ = √2, so u = (1, 1)/√2.
  3. ∇f · u = (3 + 2)/√2 = 5/√2 ≈ 3.54.

Answer: Option (B) — 5/√2.

Common misconception. Dotting with the unnormalised vector (1, 1) and answering 5. That number is the rate of change per unit of the vector (1, 1), which has length √2, so it is not a derivative with respect to distance.

EM-U4-Q48MCQmedium

For F(x, y, z) = (y², z², x²), the value of curl F at the point (1, 1, 1) is

  1. (2, 2, 2)
  2. (0, 0, 0)
  3. (−2, −2, −2)
  4. (1, 1, 1)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. For F = (P, Q, R), curl F = (Ry − Qz, Pz − Rx, Qx − Py). Each component mixes two different variables.

Step by step.

  1. Here P = y², Q = z², R = x².
  2. First component: Ry − Qz = 0 − 2z = −2z.
  3. Second component: Pz − Rx = 0 − 2x = −2x.
  4. Third component: Qx − Py = 0 − 2y = −2y.
  5. So curl F = (−2z, −2x, −2y), which at (1, 1, 1) is (−2, −2, −2).

Answer: Option (C) — (−2, −2, −2).

Common misconception. Computing the divergence instead, 0 + 0 + 0 = 0, and answering (0, 0, 0). Divergence differentiates each component with respect to its own variable and returns a scalar; curl returns a vector. Also watch the sign order: Ry − Qz, not Qz − Ry.

EM-U4-Q49MSQeasy

Which of the following statements are TRUE for a twice continuously differentiable scalar field f and vector field F? (Select all that apply.)

  1. ∇ · (∇ × F) = 0
  2. ∇ × (∇f) = 0
  3. The gradient of a scalar field is a vector field.
  4. The divergence of a vector field is a vector field.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Gradient raises a scalar to a vector, divergence lowers a vector to a scalar and curl keeps a vector a vector. The two composite identities hold because mixed partial derivatives commute.

Step by step.

  1. ∇ × F is a vector field, and its divergence is ∂x(Ry − Qz) + ∂y(Pz − Rx) + ∂z(Qx − Py), in which every pair of mixed partials cancels. So (A) is true.
  2. ∇f is a vector field whose curl has components like fzy − fyz = 0 by equality of mixed partials. So (B) is true.
  3. The gradient of a scalar field has one component per coordinate, so it is a vector field. (C) is true.
  4. The divergence Px + Qy + Rz is a single number at each point: a scalar field. (D) is false.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Believing the divergence is a vector — it is the scalar trace of the Jacobian. The mirror error is to think ∇ × (∇f) = ∇(∇ · f); the latter is undefined, since f is a scalar.

EM-U4-Q50NATmedium

The divergence of F(x, y, z) = (x²y, yz², zx²) at the point (1, 1, 1) is ______. (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Answer: 4

Detailed solution

Concept. div F = Px + Qy + Rz for F = (P, Q, R): differentiate each component with respect to its own variable and add.

Step by step.

  1. P = x²y, so Px = 2xy.
  2. Q = yz², so Qy = z².
  3. R = zx², so Rz = x².
  4. At (1, 1, 1): 2 + 1 + 1 = 4.

Answer: 4 (an integer; tolerance 10⁻⁹).

Common misconception. Differentiating every component with respect to x instead of each with respect to its own variable. At (1, 1, 1) that slip also gives 2 + 0 + 2 = 4, so this point is a weak test; the general expression div F = 2xy + z² + x² is what has to be right. The other frequent error is returning a vector, (2, 1, 1), forgetting that divergence produces a scalar.

EM-U4C-Q21MCQeasy

For f(x, y) = x² + y², the largest possible value of the directional derivative of f at the point (3, 4) is

  1. 6
  2. 8
  3. 14
  4. 10
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. The directional derivative of f at a point in a unit direction u is ∇f · u. Over all unit vectors this dot product is largest when u points along ∇f, and the maximum value is |∇f|.

Step by step.

  1. ∇f = (2x, 2y), so ∇f(3, 4) = (6, 8).
  2. For a unit vector u, Cauchy–Schwarz gives Duf = ∇f · u ≤ |∇f||u| = |∇f|.
  3. |∇f| = √(36 + 64) = 10, attained in the direction (3/5, 4/5).

Answer: 10 — option (D).

Common misconception. 14 comes from adding the two components instead of taking the norm, and 6 or 8 from quoting a single partial derivative. A further trap is to use the direction vector without normalising it: the directional derivative is defined only for a unit vector.

EM-U4C-Q22NATeasy

For F(x, y, z) = (x²y, y²z, z²x), the value of div F at the point (1, 1, 1) is ______.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 6

Detailed solution

Concept. The divergence of F = (F₁, F₂, F₃) is ∂F₁/∂x + ∂F₂/∂y + ∂F₃/∂z: each component is differentiated with respect to its own variable, and the three results are added as scalars.

Step by step.

  1. ∂/∂x (x²y) = 2xy.
  2. ∂/∂y (y²z) = 2yz.
  3. ∂/∂z (z²x) = 2zx.
  4. div F = 2xy + 2yz + 2zx, which at (1, 1, 1) is 2 + 2 + 2 = 6.

Answer: 6 (exact integer).

Common misconception. Differentiating each component with respect to all three variables, which computes a Jacobian or a gradient rather than a divergence, or differentiating x²y with respect to y by mistake. Divergence is a scalar: if the working produces a vector, the wrong operator has been applied.

EM-U4C-Q23MCQmedium

Let F(x, y, z) = (2xy + z, x², x). Which one of the following statements is TRUE?

  1. curl F = (1, 1, 0), so F is not conservative.
  2. F is conservative, with potential φ(x, y, z) = x²y + xz.
  3. F is not conservative, because ∂F₁/∂x = 2y is not zero.
  4. F is solenoidal, because div F = 0 everywhere.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. A field on a simply connected region is conservative exactly when its curl vanishes; for F = (P, Q, R) the curl is (Ry − Qz, Pz − Rx, Qx − Py). A potential is then recovered by integrating the components.

Step by step.

  1. Here P = 2xy + z, Q = x² and R = x.
  2. curl F = (0 − 0, 1 − 1, 2x − 2x) = (0, 0, 0).
  3. ℝ³ is simply connected and F is continuously differentiable, so a potential exists.
  4. From φx = 2xy + z we get φ = x²y + xz + h(y, z). Then φy = x² forces hy = 0 and φz = x forces hz = 0, so φ = x²y + xz up to an additive constant.
  5. Check the other claims: div F = 2y + 0 + 0 = 2y, which is not identically zero, so F is not solenoidal.

Answer: F is conservative, with potential φ(x, y, z) = x²y + xz — option (B).

Common misconception. Reading a non-zero ∂P/∂x as evidence against conservativeness — the test is the curl, not the individual partial derivatives. Equally, a vanishing curl guarantees a potential only on a simply connected domain; on the punctured plane the classic vortex field has zero curl and no global potential.

EM-U4C-Q24MSQmedium

Which of the following statements are TRUE for sufficiently smooth fields? (Select all that apply.)

  1. ∇ · (∇f) = 0 for every twice continuously differentiable scalar field f.
  2. ∇ × (∇f) = 0 for every twice continuously differentiable scalar field f.
  3. ∇ · (∇ × F) = 0 for every twice continuously differentiable vector field F.
  4. If curl F = 0 throughout a simply connected region, then F = ∇φ for some scalar field φ on that region.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (B), (C) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Two second-order identities hold for every sufficiently smooth field — the curl of a gradient and the divergence of a curl both vanish — while the divergence of a gradient is the Laplacian, which is not generally zero. The converse direction, curl-free implying a potential, needs simple connectivity.

Step by step.

  1. (A) FALSE — ∇ · ∇f = ∇²f is the Laplacian. For f = x² + y² it equals 4, not 0; only harmonic functions satisfy ∇²f = 0.
  2. (B) TRUE — ∇ × ∇f = 0, because each component is a difference of mixed partials that are equal by Clairaut's theorem.
  3. (C) TRUE — ∇ · (∇ × F) = 0 identically; every term cancels against another mixed partial.
  4. (D) TRUE — this is the Poincaré lemma: on a simply connected region a curl-free C¹ field is a gradient field.

Answer: Options (B), (C) and (D).

Common misconception. Option (A) is the standard confusion between the two vanishing identities and the Laplacian. A second trap is to state (D) without the simple-connectivity hypothesis: on a domain with a hole a curl-free field can fail to have a potential.

EM-U4C-Q25MCQeasy

Which one of the following scalar fields is harmonic, that is, satisfies ∇²f = 0 everywhere?

  1. f = x² + y²
  2. f = x³ + y³
  3. f = x² − y²
  4. f = x²y²
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. A scalar field is harmonic when its Laplacian ∇²f = fxx + fyy vanishes identically. For polynomials this is a direct computation, and harmonicity has nothing to do with having a minimum.

Step by step.

  1. For f = x² + y²: fxx = 2, fyy = 2, so ∇²f = 4 ≠ 0.
  2. For f = x³ + y³: fxx = 6x, fyy = 6y, so ∇²f = 6(x + y) ≠ 0.
  3. For f = x² − y²: fxx = 2, fyy = −2, so ∇²f = 0 — harmonic.
  4. For f = x²y²: fxx = 2y², fyy = 2x², so ∇²f = 2(x² + y²) ≠ 0.

Answer: f = x² − y² — option (C).

Common misconception. Assuming every quadratic is harmonic, or that a harmonic function must have a minimum. x² − y² is harmonic and has a saddle point at the origin: a non-constant harmonic function can never have an interior maximum or minimum.

Unit 5 — Continuous Probability & Distributions 50
Random variables, CDF & PDF 10
EM-U5-Q01MCQeasy

A continuous random variable X has probability density function f(x) = kx for 0 ≤ x ≤ 4 and f(x) = 0 otherwise. The value of k is

  1. 1/8
  2. 1/4
  3. 1/2
  4. 1/16
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. A probability density function must integrate to 1 over its whole support. The constant is fixed by that single condition, not by the value of f at any one point.

Step by step.

  1. Set the total probability to one: ∫04 kx dx = 1.
  2. Integrate: ∫04 kx dx = k[x²/2]04 = k · 8.
  3. So 8k = 1, giving k = 1/8.

Answer: Option (A) — k = 1/8.

Common misconception. Reading the constant off the value of the density at the right endpoint, f(4) = 4k = 1, which gives k = 1/4. A density is a height, not a probability; only its integral is constrained to be 1.

EM-U5-Q02MCQeasy

A random variable X has cumulative distribution function F(x) = 1 − e−3x for x ≥ 0 and F(x) = 0 for x < 0. The probability density function of X is

  1. 3e−3x for x ≥ 0, and 0 for x < 0
  2. e−3x for x ≥ 0, and 0 for x < 0
  3. 3e−3x for all real x
  4. 1 − 3e−3x for x ≥ 0, and 0 for x < 0
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. For an absolutely continuous random variable the density is the derivative of the distribution function: f(x) = F′(x) wherever F is differentiable.

Step by step.

  1. Differentiate F(x) = 1 − e−3x with respect to x: the constant 1 dies and the exponential brings down the inner factor −3.
  2. So f(x) = −(−3)e−3x = 3e−3x for x ≥ 0.
  3. For x < 0, F is constant, so f(x) = 0 there; the density is never negative.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Dropping the chain-rule factor and answering e−3x, or extending 3e−3x to the whole real line. The density must vanish where the distribution function is flat.

EM-U5-Q03MCQmedium

A continuous random variable X has density f(x) = 2x for 0 ≤ x ≤ 1 and f(x) = 0 otherwise. Then P(X ≤ 1/2) equals

  1. 1/2
  2. 1/4
  3. 1/8
  4. 3/4
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. For a continuous random variable, a probability is the area under the density: P(X ≤ a) = ∫−∞a f(x) dx = F(a).

Step by step.

  1. Integrate the density from 0 to 1/2: ∫01/2 2x dx = [x²]01/2.
  2. Substitute the limits: (1/2)² − 0 = 1/4.
  3. So P(X ≤ 1/2) = 1/4, which is also F(1/2) = (1/2)² for this distribution.

Answer: Option (B) — 1/4.

Common misconception. Substituting the endpoint into the density instead of integrating, which gives f(1/2) = 1 and a nonsensical probability. A density value is not a probability; only the accumulated area is.

EM-U5-Q04MSQmedium

Let X be a random variable with cumulative distribution function F. Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. F is non-decreasing on ℝ.
  2. F is right-continuous.
  3. P(a < X ≤ b) = F(b) − F(a) for all a < b.
  4. F is continuous for every random variable.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. The three universal properties of a distribution function are monotonicity, right-continuity and the limit values F(−∞) = 0, F(+∞) = 1; interval probabilities are read as differences of F.

Step by step.

  1. Monotone: if a < b then {X ≤ a} ⊆ {X ≤ b}, so F(a) ≤ F(b). Statement (A) is true.
  2. Right-continuity, F(x) = F(x⁺), holds for every distribution function; it is the convention that makes P(X ≤ x) = F(x) rather than P(X < x). Statement (B) is true.
  3. Splitting the event: P(X ≤ b) = P(X ≤ a) + P(a < X ≤ b), so P(a < X ≤ b) = F(b) − F(a). Statement (C) is true.
  4. Statement (D) is false: a discrete variable such as a fair die has F with jumps of size 1/6 at 1, 2, …, 6.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Assuming every distribution function is continuous. Continuity of F is exactly what distinguishes an absolutely continuous variable from one with atoms, and the die is the standard counterexample.

EM-U5-Q05NAThard

A continuous random variable X has density f(x) = cx² for 0 ≤ x ≤ 3 and f(x) = 0 otherwise. The expected value E[X] is ______.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 2.25

Detailed solution

Concept. Two steps in order: normalise the density to find c, then compute E[X] = ∫ x f(x) dx over the support.

Step by step.

  1. Normalise: ∫03 cx² dx = c[x³/3]03 = 9c = 1, so c = 1/9.
  2. Then E[X] = ∫03 x · (x²/9) dx = (1/9)∫03 x³ dx.
  3. Integrate: (1/9)[x⁴/4]03 = (1/9)(81/4) = 9/4 = 2.25.

Answer: 2.25 (that is 9/4).

Common misconception. Using the midpoint 1.5 of the support, which is the mean only for a uniform density. The density here increases with x, so the mass sits to the right of the midpoint and E[X] > 1.5.

EM-U5-Q26MCQeasy

A continuous random variable X has density f(x) = k(1 − x) for 0 ≤ x ≤ 1 and f(x) = 0 otherwise. Then k equals

  1. 1/2
  2. 1
  3. 2
  4. 4
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. A density must integrate to 1 over its whole support, and that single condition fixes the normalising constant.

Step by step.

  1. Set the total area to one: ∫₀¹ k(1 − x) dx = 1.
  2. Integrate the bracket: ∫₀¹ (1 − x) dx = [x − x²/2]₀¹ = 1/2.
  3. So k/2 = 1, giving k = 2.

Answer: Option (C) — k = 2.

Common misconception. Reading the constant off one ordinate, f(0) = k = 1. A density is a height, not a probability; only its integral is pinned to 1.

EM-U5-Q27MCQeasy

A random variable X has distribution function F(x) = x³ for 0 ≤ x ≤ 1. Then P(0.5 < X ≤ 0.8) equals

  1. 0.512
  2. 0.387
  3. 0.125
  4. 0.216
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. For a continuous variable the probability of an interval is the difference of the distribution function at the endpoints.

Step by step.

  1. P(0.5 < X ≤ 0.8) = F(0.8) − F(0.5).
  2. F(0.8) = 0.8³ = 0.512 and F(0.5) = 0.5³ = 0.125.
  3. 0.512 − 0.125 = 0.387.

Answer: Option (B) — 0.387.

Common misconception. Reporting F(0.8) = 0.512 and forgetting the lower endpoint, or differentiating and using the density instead of the distribution function.

EM-U5-Q28MCQmedium

A random variable X has density f(x) = x/2 for 0 ≤ x ≤ 2 and f(x) = 0 otherwise. Then P(X ≤ 1 | X ≤ 1.5) equals

  1. 1/4
  2. 1/2
  3. 2/3
  4. 4/9
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Conditioning on an event of positive probability rescales probability: P(A | B) = P(A ∩ B)/P(B). For nested intervals the intersection is the smaller interval.

Step by step.

  1. Here F(x) = x²/4 on [0,2].
  2. P(X ≤ 1) = 1/4 and P(X ≤ 1.5) = 2.25/4 = 9/16.
  3. P(X ≤ 1 | X ≤ 1.5) = (1/4)/(9/16) = 4/9 ≈ 0.444.

Answer: Option (D) — 4/9.

Common misconception. Dividing by the length of the whole support instead of by P(X ≤ 1.5), which returns 1/4 and silently ignores the conditioning.

EM-U5-Q29MSQmedium

Let X be a continuous random variable with density f and distribution function F. Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. E[X] = ∫ x f(x) dx taken over the whole real line.
  2. If f(x) = 0 on an interval [a, b], then P(a ≤ X ≤ b) = 0.
  3. F is differentiable at every real x, with F′(x) = f(x) at every x.
  4. f(x) ≥ 0 for all x, and f integrates to 1.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. A density is non-negative and integrates to one; probability is the integral of the density. The identity F′ = f holds almost everywhere, not at every point.

Step by step.

  1. A is the definition of expectation for an absolutely continuous law.
  2. B holds because the probability of the interval is the integral of f over it, and the integrand is identically zero there.
  3. C fails at corners: F(x) = 2x on [0, 1/2] and F(x) = 2 − 2x on (1/2, 1] is a valid distribution function that is not differentiable at 1/2.
  4. D is exactly the pair of defining properties of a density.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Assuming every distribution function is differentiable with derivative equal to the density at every point. A density is only determined up to sets of measure zero.

EM-U5-Q30NAThard

A continuous random variable X has density f(x) = c x²(1 − x) for 0 ≤ x ≤ 1 and f(x) = 0 otherwise. The expected value E[X] is ______.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 0.6

Detailed solution

Concept. Normalise the density first, then take the first moment E[X] = ∫ x f(x) dx.

Step by step.

  1. ∫₀¹ c(x² − x³) dx = c(1/3 − 1/4) = c/12 = 1, so c = 12.
  2. E[X] = 12 ∫₀¹ (x³ − x⁴) dx = 12(1/4 − 1/5).
  3. 12 · (1/20) = 0.6.

Answer: 0.6 (exact).

Common misconception. Taking the moment with an un-normalised constant, or integrating x²(1 − x) and reporting 1/20 = 0.05, which is the normalising integral and not a moment.

Uniform distribution 10
EM-U5-Q06MCQeasy

If X is uniformly distributed on [2, 10], then Var(X) equals

  1. 64
  2. 16/3
  3. 8/3
  4. 4
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. For X ∼ U(a, b): E[X] = (a + b)/2 and Var(X) = (b − a)²/12.

Step by step.

  1. The length of the support is b − a = 10 − 2 = 8.
  2. So Var(X) = 8²/12 = 64/12.
  3. Simplify: 64/12 = 16/3 ≈ 5.33.

Answer: Option (B) — 16/3.

Common misconception. Using (b − a)²/4 or the whole 64 without dividing by 12. The 12 comes from integrating (x − μ)² across the interval; the variance of U(0,1) is the 1/12 that anchors the formula.

EM-U5-Q07MCQeasy

If X is uniformly distributed on [0, 5], then P(X > 3) equals

  1. 3/5
  2. 1/5
  3. 2/5
  4. 1/2
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. For a uniform distribution, probability is proportional to length: P(X ∈ A) = |A ∩ [a, b]| / (b − a).

Step by step.

  1. The interval of interest is (3, 5], of length 5 − 3 = 2.
  2. The whole support has length 5, and the density is the constant 1/5.
  3. So P(X > 3) = 2 · (1/5) = 2/5.

Answer: Option (C) — 2/5.

Common misconception. Reading 3/5, the position of the cut-off rather than the length beyond it. The probability is the length of the part of the support that satisfies the condition, divided by the total length.

EM-U5-Q08MCQmedium

A random variable X is uniform on [a, b] with E[X] = 5 and Var(X) = 3. Then a equals

  1. 1
  2. 3
  3. 4
  4. 2
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. The mean and variance of U(a, b) give two equations in the two unknowns a + b and b − a; solve for a and b separately.

Step by step.

  1. Mean: (a + b)/2 = 5, so a + b = 10.
  2. Variance: (b − a)²/12 = 3, so (b − a)² = 36 and, since b > a, b − a = 6.
  3. Add and subtract: b = (10 + 6)/2 = 8 and a = (10 − 6)/2 = 2.

Answer: Option (D) — a = 2.

Common misconception. Taking the square root as b − a = ±18, or forgetting that the variance fixes the width while the mean fixes the centre. Both are needed; one alone leaves a one-parameter family.

EM-U5-Q09MSQmedium

Let X be uniformly distributed on [0, 1]. Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. E[X] = 1/2.
  2. Var(X) = 1/12.
  3. P(X = 1/2) = 1/2.
  4. E[X²] = 1/3.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. For U(0,1) the density is the constant 1 on the unit interval, so moments are plain integrals ∫01 xⁿ dx = 1/(n+1), and any single point carries probability zero.

Step by step.

  1. E[X] = ∫01 x dx = 1/2, so (A) is true.
  2. E[X²] = ∫01 x² dx = 1/3, hence Var(X) = 1/3 − (1/2)² = 1/3 − 1/4 = 1/12. (B) and (D) are true.
  3. A single point has zero probability under a continuous density, so P(X = 1/2) = 0, not 1/2. (C) is false.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Confusing the value of the density at a point with the probability of that point. For U(0,1) the density at 1/2 is indeed 1, but P(X = 1/2) = 0 because a point has no length.

EM-U5-Q10NAThard

Let X and Y be independent random variables, each uniform on [0, 1]. Then P(|X − Y| ≤ 1/2) is ______.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 0.75

Detailed solution

Concept. The joint density of two independent U(0,1) variables is the constant 1 on the unit square, so a probability is the area of the region inside the square.

Step by step.

  1. Work in the unit square of the (x, y) plane. The condition |x − y| ≤ 1/2 is the band between the lines y = x − 1/2 and y = x + 1/2.
  2. The complement consists of the two corner triangles where y > x + 1/2 and where y < x − 1/2; each has legs 1/2, so each has area (1/2)(1/2)(1/2) = 1/8.
