  3. Total excluded area 2 · 1/8 = 1/4, so the required area is 1 − 1/4 = 3/4 = 0.75.

Answer: 0.75.

Common misconception. Computing P(X − Y ≤ 1/2) and forgetting the absolute value, which removes only one of the two corner triangles and gives 0.875. The band is symmetric about the diagonal, so both corners must go.

EM-U5-Q31MCQeasy

If X is uniformly distributed on [−2, 6], then P(X < 1) equals

  1. 1/8
  2. 5/8
  3. 3/8
  4. 1/2
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. For a uniform law on [a, b] probability is length over total length.

Step by step.

  1. Total length = 6 − (−2) = 8.
  2. Favourable length = 1 − (−2) = 3.
  3. P(X < 1) = 3/8.

Answer: Option (C) — 3/8.

Common misconception. Measuring the favourable part from 0 instead of from the left endpoint a = −2, which gives 1/8.

EM-U5-Q32MCQeasy

If X is uniformly distributed on [0, 10], then P(X > 7 | X > 4) equals

  1. 3/10
  2. 2/5
  3. 7/10
  4. 1/2
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Conditioning a uniform law on a sub-interval leaves it uniform on that sub-interval, so the conditional probability is again a length ratio.

Step by step.

  1. P(X > 4) = 6/10.
  2. P(X > 7) = 3/10.
  3. P(X > 7 | X > 4) = (3/10)/(6/10) = 1/2.

Answer: Option (D) — 1/2.

Common misconception. Answering 3/10, the unconditional probability, which ignores that the variable is already known to exceed 4.

EM-U5-Q33MCQmedium

A random variable X is uniform on [a, b] with E[X] = 4 and standard deviation √3. Then b equals

  1. 6
  2. 10
  3. 7
  4. 4
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. E[X] = (a + b)/2 and Var(X) = (b − a)²/12 recover the endpoints from a stated mean and variance.

Step by step.

  1. Var(X) = 3, so (b − a)²/12 = 3 and (b − a)² = 36, giving b − a = 6.
  2. (a + b)/2 = 4, so a + b = 8.
  3. Adding the two relations, 2b = 14, so b = 7 and a = 1.

Answer: Option (C) — b = 7.

Common misconception. Using the standard deviation 3 as the variance, which gives (b − a)² = 3 and a non-integer span; or keeping both roots of (b − a)² = 36 and getting the endpoints in the wrong order.

EM-U5-Q34MSQmedium

Let X be uniform on [a, b] with a < b. Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. E[X] = (a + b)/2.
  2. Var(X) = (b − a)²/12.
  3. The density is 1/(b − a) on [a, b] and 0 outside it.
  4. P(X = a) = 1/(b − a).
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. The uniform law has constant density 1/(b − a) on its support, mean at the midpoint and variance (b − a)²/12; single points carry probability zero.

Step by step.

  1. A and B are the standard mean and variance of U[a, b].
  2. C is the density itself: constant on the support and zero outside.
  3. D fails because X is continuous, so P(X = a) = 0 for every a.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Reading the density height 1/(b − a) as the probability of the point a. A density is a rate per unit length, not a mass.

EM-U5-Q35NAThard

Let X and Y be independent random variables, each uniform on [0, 1]. Then E[min(X, Y)] is ______.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 0.33

Detailed solution

Concept. For independent uniforms the joint density is 1 on the unit square, so E[min(X, Y)] = ∫₀¹ P(min(X, Y) > t) dt.

Step by step.

  1. P(min(X, Y) > t) = P(X > t)P(Y > t) = (1 − t)² for 0 ≤ t ≤ 1.
  2. E[min(X, Y)] = ∫₀¹ (1 − t)² dt.
  3. = [−(1 − t)³/3]₀¹ = 1/3 ≈ 0.33.

Answer: 0.33 (exactly 1/3).

Common misconception. Squaring the mean, (1/2)² = 0.25, as if E[min] equalled (E[X])². The minimum of two variables is not the square of the mean.

Exponential distribution 10
EM-U5-Q11MCQeasy

If X is exponentially distributed with rate λ = 2, that is f(x) = 2e−2x for x ≥ 0, then P(X > 1/2) equals

  1. 1 − e−1
  2. e−2
  3. 1/2
  4. e−1
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. For an exponential variable the survival function is P(X > t) = e−λt, so the integral never has to be redone.

Step by step.

  1. Use the survival form: P(X > t) = ∫t∞ λe−λx dx = e−λt.
  2. Substitute λ = 2 and t = 1/2: the exponent is −2 · 1/2 = −1.
  3. So P(X > 1/2) = e−1 ≈ 0.3679.

Answer: Option (D) — e−1.

Common misconception. Reporting the complement 1 − e−1, which is P(X ≤ 1/2). The survival function carries no leading 1 −; the minus sign belongs to the exponent.

EM-U5-Q12MCQeasy

An exponentially distributed random variable X has mean 4. The standard deviation of X is

  1. 2
  2. 4
  3. 16
  4. 1/4
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. For X ∼ Exp(λ): E[X] = 1/λ and Var(X) = 1/λ², so the standard deviation equals the mean.

Step by step.

  1. The mean 4 fixes the rate: 1/λ = 4, so λ = 1/4.
  2. Then Var(X) = 1/λ² = 16.
  3. The standard deviation is √16 = 4, the same number as the mean.

Answer: Option (B) — 4.

Common misconception. Stopping at the variance 16 and reporting it as the standard deviation. The exponential is the continuous distribution whose standard deviation equals its mean, which makes the two easy to confuse.

EM-U5-Q13MCQmedium

If X is exponentially distributed with rate λ = 1, then P(X > 5 | X > 3) equals

  1. e−5
  2. 1 − e−2
  3. e−2
  4. e−3
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. The exponential distribution is memoryless: P(X > s + t | X > s) = P(X > t). Having already survived s units of time tells you nothing about the remaining life.

Step by step.

  1. By definition P(X > 5 | X > 3) = P(X > 5) / P(X > 3) because {X > 5} ⊂ {X > 3}.
  2. With λ = 1: P(X > 5) = e−5 and P(X > 3) = e−3.
  3. Divide: e−5/e−3 = e−2, which is P(X > 2) as memorylessness predicts.

Answer: Option (C) — e−2.

Common misconception. Answering P(X > 5) = e−5 by ignoring the conditioning, or e−3 by reusing the conditioning time. The conditional probability depends only on the gap 5 − 3 = 2.

EM-U5-Q14MSQmedium

Let X be exponentially distributed with rate λ > 0. Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. X is memoryless: P(X > s + t | X > s) = P(X > t).
  2. Var(X) = 1/λ.
  3. P(X > 2/λ) = e−2.
  4. The density f is strictly decreasing on [0, ∞).
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. The exponential law has survival e−λt, constant hazard λ, variance 1/λ², and is the canonical memoryless continuous law.

Step by step.

  1. Memorylessness follows from e−λ(s+t)/e−λs = e−λt. (A) is true.
  2. The variance is 1/λ², not 1/λ (that is the mean). (B) is false.
  3. Put t = 2/λ in the survival function: e−λ · 2/λ = e−2. (C) is true.
  4. The density λe−λx is a positive constant times a decreasing exponential, hence strictly decreasing. (D) is true.

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D).

Common misconception. Mixing up 1/λ and 1/λ². The mean and the variance of an exponential differ in units, so a variance can never be 1/λ once λ carries the unit of inverse time.

EM-U5-Q15NAThard

An exponentially distributed random variable has mean 4. Its median is ______. (Give the answer correct to two decimal places.)

Show answer & solution

Answer: 2.77

Detailed solution

Concept. The median m solves F(m) = 1/2, that is 1 − e−λm = 1/2, giving m = −ln(1/2)/λ = (ln 2)/λ.

Step by step.

  1. Mean 4 means λ = 1/4.
  2. Set e−m/4 = 1/2, so −m/4 = −ln 2 and m = 4 ln 2.
  3. Numerically 4 × 0.693147 = 2.772589, which is 2.77 to two places.

Answer: 2.77 (exactly 4 ln 2).

Common misconception. Answering 4 and treating the median as the mean. For a right-skewed law the median lies below the mean, and here it is about 69\% of it; only for a symmetric law do the two coincide.

EM-U5-Q36MCQeasy

An exponentially distributed random variable X has mean 5. Then P(X > 10) equals

  1. e⁻¹
  2. e⁻²
  3. 1 − e⁻²
  4. e⁻¹⁰
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. For an exponential law the survival function is the compact form P(X > t) = e^(−λt), with λ = 1/mean.

Step by step.

  1. Mean 5 gives λ = 1/5.
  2. P(X > 10) = e^(−10/5) = e⁻².
  3. Numerically e⁻² ≈ 0.1353.

Answer: Option (B) — e⁻².

Common misconception. Using λ = 5 straight from the mean, which gives e⁻⁵⁰, or answering 1 − e⁻², which is the probability of the complementary event.

EM-U5-Q37MCQeasy

An exponentially distributed random variable X has rate λ = 0.5. Then Var(X) equals

  1. 2
  2. 1/4
  3. 0.5
  4. 4
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. An exponential law has mean 1/λ and variance 1/λ², so its standard deviation equals its mean.

Step by step.

  1. λ = 0.5.
  2. Var(X) = 1/λ² = 1/(0.5)².
  3. = 4.

Answer: Option (D) — 4.

Common misconception. Reporting 1/λ = 2, which is both the mean and the standard deviation, but not the variance.

EM-U5-Q38MCQmedium

An exponentially distributed random variable X has mean 2. Then P(X ≤ 4) equals

  1. 1 − e⁻²
  2. 1 − e⁻⁴
  3. e⁻²
  4. 1/2
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Integrate the density from 0 to the point, or use the distribution function F(t) = 1 − e^(−λt).

Step by step.

  1. Mean 2 gives λ = 1/2.
  2. P(X ≤ 4) = 1 − e^(−4 · 1/2).
  3. = 1 − e⁻² ≈ 0.8647.

Answer: Option (A) — 1 − e⁻².

Common misconception. Writing e⁻² and dropping the complement, which answers P(X > 4) instead of P(X ≤ 4).

EM-U5-Q39MSQmedium

Let X be exponentially distributed with rate λ > 0. Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. The median of X is (ln 2)/λ.
  2. Var(X) = 1/λ².
  3. P(X > t) = 1 − e^(−λt) for t ≥ 0.
  4. Doubling λ halves the mean.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. The exponential law has mean 1/λ, variance 1/λ², survival function e^(−λt) and median (ln 2)/λ, which lies below the mean because the density is skewed to the right.

Step by step.

  1. A: solve 1 − e^(−λm) = 1/2 to obtain m = (ln 2)/λ.
  2. B: Var(X) = 1/λ² by the standard moments.
  3. C fails: P(X > t) = e^(−λt), whereas 1 − e^(−λt) is the distribution function F(t).
  4. D: the mean 1/λ is inversely proportional to λ.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Confusing P(X > t) with F(t), and assuming the median equals the mean, which fails for every skewed law.

EM-U5-Q40NAThard

Let X and Y be independent exponential random variables with means 3 and 6 respectively. Then P(X < Y) is ______.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 0.67

Detailed solution

Concept. For independent exponential variables the smaller rate wins: P(X < Y) = λ_X/(λ_X + λ_Y).

Step by step.

  1. The rates are λ_X = 1/3 and λ_Y = 1/6.
  2. P(X < Y) = ∫ (1/3)e^(−x/3) · e^(−x/6) dx over [0, ∞).
  3. The integrand is (1/3)e^(−x/2), whose integral is (1/3)·2 = 2/3 ≈ 0.67.

Answer: 0.67 (exactly 2/3).

Common misconception. Putting the means in the ratio, 3/(3 + 6) = 1/3, instead of the rates. The comparison is between rates, and the smaller rate belongs to the larger mean.

Normal distribution 10
EM-U5-Q16MCQeasy

If X ∼ N(μ, σ²), then P(X < μ) equals

  1. 0
  2. 1/2
  3. 1
  4. 1/4
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. The normal density is symmetric about μ, so the mean is also the median and the distribution function satisfies F(μ) = 1/2.

Step by step.

  1. Standardise: P(X < μ) = P((X − μ)/σ < 0) = Φ(0).
  2. The standard normal density φ is even, so the area to the left of 0 equals the area to the right.
  3. The total area is 1, hence Φ(0) = 1/2.

Answer: Option (B) — 1/2.

Common misconception. Assuming P(X < μ) = 0 because the density is largest at μ. A point carries no probability; the density value at the mean is irrelevant to P(X < μ).

EM-U5-Q17MCQeasy

For a standard normal variable Z, the probability P(|Z| < 1) is closest to

  1. 0.6827
  2. 0.9545
  3. 0.3413
  4. 0.5000
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. The empirical rule for the normal law: about 68.27\% of the mass lies within one standard deviation of the mean, 95.45\% within two and 99.73\% within three.

Step by step.

  1. Write P(|Z| < 1) = P(−1 < Z < 1) = Φ(1) − Φ(−1).
  2. By symmetry Φ(−1) = 1 − Φ(1), so the probability is 2Φ(1) − 1.
  3. With Φ(1) = 0.8413: 2(0.8413) − 1 = 0.6827.

Answer: Option (A) — 0.6827.

Common misconception. Answering 0.3413, which is the one-sided P(0 < Z < 1). The absolute value makes the interval two-sided, so the one-sided tail area must be doubled.

EM-U5-Q18MCQmedium

If X ∼ N(10, 4), where the second parameter is the variance, then P(X > 14) is closest to

  1. 0.9772
  2. 0.1587
  3. 0.0455
  4. 0.0228
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Standardise with the standard deviation, Z = (X − μ)/σ, where σ is the square root of the stated variance.

Step by step.

  1. Here μ = 10 and σ² = 4, so σ = 2.
  2. Standardise: P(X > 14) = P(Z > (14 − 10)/2) = P(Z > 2).
  3. The upper tail beyond two standard deviations is 1 − 0.9772 = 0.0228.

Answer: Option (D) — 0.0228.

Common misconception. Dividing by the variance 4 instead of the standard deviation 2, which gives P(Z > 1) = 0.1587. The standard normal scale is in standard deviations, never in variances.

EM-U5-Q19MSQmedium

Let X ∼ N(2, 9) and Y ∼ N(3, 16) be independent, with the second parameter the variance in each case. Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. X + Y ∼ N(5, 25).
  2. X − Y ∼ N(−1, 25).
  3. 2X ∼ N(4, 18).
  4. P(X < 2) = 1/2.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Independent normal variables are closed under linear combinations: means add with the coefficients, variances add with the squares of the coefficients. Scaling a single variable scales the variance by the square of the factor.

Step by step.

  1. Means: E[X + Y] = 2 + 3 = 5. Variances: independence gives Var(X + Y) = 9 + 16 = 25. (A) is true.
  2. For a difference the variances still add: Var(X − Y) = 9 + 16 = 25, and the mean is 2 − 3 = −1. (B) is true.
  3. Scaling: E[2X] = 4 but Var(2X) = 4 · 9 = 36, so 2X ∼ N(4, 36), not N(4, 18). (C) is false.
  4. The mean of X is its median, so P(X < 2) = 1/2. (D) is true.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Subtracting variances when forming X − Y, which would give N(−1, −7), or adding 18 instead of 36 when doubling X. Variances always add under independence and always scale by the square.

EM-U5-Q20NAThard

Let X ∼ N(50, 100), with variance 100. If P(X < c) = 0.9772, then c equals ______. (Use z = 2.00 for the cumulative probability 0.9772.)

Show answer & solution

Answer: 70

Detailed solution

Concept. Invert the standardisation: if P(Z < z) = p then x = μ + zσ. The table is always consulted on the standard normal scale.

Step by step.

  1. The variance is 100, so σ = 10 and μ = 50.
  2. The stated probability corresponds to z = 2.00.
  3. Convert back: c = μ + zσ = 50 + 2(10) = 70.

Answer: 70.

Common misconception. Using σ = 100 and getting c = 250, or reading 0.9772 as a z-value directly. The second parameter of N(μ, σ²) is the variance, so the standard deviation must be extracted first.

EM-U5-Q41MCQeasy

For a standard normal variable Z, the probability P(Z > 1.96) is closest to

  1. 0.0500
  2. 0.4750
  3. 0.0250
  4. 0.9750
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. The tabulated fact P(|Z| < 1.96) = 0.95 splits the remaining 0.05 equally between the two tails.

Step by step.

  1. The central 95% of the standard normal lies within ±1.96.
  2. The two tails share the remaining 5%.
  3. P(Z > 1.96) = 0.025.

Answer: Option (C) — 0.025.

Common misconception. Reporting 0.05, the total two-tail area, or 0.975, which is the cumulative probability Φ(1.96).

EM-U5-Q42MCQeasy

A random variable X is normal with mean 20 and variance σ², where P(X < 14) = 0.0228. Then σ equals

  1. 2
  2. 3
  3. 4
  4. 6
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Standardise and invert: z = (x − μ)/σ, so a known tail probability pins down σ.

Step by step.

  1. P(X < 14) = 0.0228 corresponds to z = −2.
  2. −2 = (14 − 20)/σ = −6/σ.
  3. σ = 3.

Answer: Option (B) — σ = 3.

Common misconception. Answering 9, which is the variance. The notation N(20, σ²) names the variance, so the spread in the units of X is σ.

EM-U5-Q43MCQmedium

If X is normal with mean 100 and variance 25, then P(95 < X < 110) is closest to

  1. 0.7745
  2. 0.1359
  3. 0.8413
  4. 0.8185
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Standardise both endpoints and subtract cumulative probabilities: P(a < X < b) = Φ(z_b) − Φ(z_a).

Step by step.

  1. σ = 5, since the variance is 25.
  2. z₁ = (95 − 100)/5 = −1 and z₂ = (110 − 100)/5 = 2.
  3. Φ(2) − Φ(−1) = 0.9772 − 0.1587 = 0.8185.

Answer: Option (D) — 0.8185.

Common misconception. Using σ = 25, which pushes both endpoints far into the tails, or subtracting the two tail areas instead of adding the two inner pieces.

EM-U5-Q44MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about the normal distribution are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. If X is normal then aX + b is normal for any constants a ≠ 0 and b.
  2. For a normal variable the mean, the median and the mode coincide.
  3. If X and Y are jointly normal with Cov(X, Y) = 0, then X and Y are independent.
  4. Every symmetric distribution is normal.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Normality is preserved by affine maps and by linear combinations of jointly normal variables; for a jointly normal pair, zero covariance is the same as independence.

Step by step.

  1. A: aX + b has mean aμ + b and variance a²σ², and remains normal.
  2. B: the density is symmetric about μ, so the three central measures agree.
  3. C is the special property that separates the joint normal from other joint laws.
  4. D fails: the uniform and the logistic laws are symmetric but not normal.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Extending 'uncorrelated implies independent' beyond the jointly normal case, where it is false, and assuming that symmetry alone forces normality.

EM-U5-Q45NAThard

Let X be normal with mean 50 and variance 16. If P(X > c) = 0.0228, then c equals ______. (Use z = 2.00 for the cumulative probability 0.9772.)

Show answer & solution

Answer: 58

Detailed solution

Concept. Invert a tabulated tail probability with x = μ + zσ, reading z from the cumulative probability.

Step by step.

  1. P(X > c) = 0.0228 gives P(X < c) = 0.9772, so z = 2.
  2. The variance is 16, so σ = 4.
  3. c = 50 + 2(4) = 58.

Answer: 58.

Common misconception. Using z = −2, which gives 42 and answers the lower tail instead; or adding the variance 16 in place of zσ = 8.

Correlation & covariance 10
EM-U5-Q21MCQeasy

For random variables X and Y with finite second moments, which identity is correct?

  1. Cov(X, Y) = E[XY] − E[X]E[Y]
  2. Cov(X, Y) = E[XY] + E[X]E[Y]
  3. Cov(X, Y) = E[X]E[Y] − E[XY]
  4. Cov(X, Y) = E[X²] − (E[X])²
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Covariance is the expected product of the centred variables, Cov(X, Y) = E[(X − E[X])(Y − E[Y])], which expands to the computational form.

Step by step.

  1. Expand the definition: E[(X − μX)(Y − μY)] = E[XY] − μYE[X] − μXE[Y] + μXμY.
  2. Since E[X] = μX and E[Y] = μY, this collapses to E[XY] − μXμY.
  3. So Cov(X, Y) = E[XY] − E[X]E[Y]; the fourth option is Var(X), the covariance of X with itself.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Choosing the last option, which is the variance. Covariance of a variable with itself is its variance, so option (D) is a special case with the wrong label rather than a general identity.

EM-U5-Q22MCQeasy

If X and Y are independent with Var(X) = 4 and Var(Y) = 9, then Var(X + Y) equals

  1. 5
  2. 13
  3. 36
  4. 25
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Independence makes the covariance zero, so the variance of a sum is the sum of the variances; standard deviations, not variances, are what fail to add.

Step by step.

  1. General rule: Var(X + Y) = Var(X) + Var(Y) + 2Cov(X, Y).
  2. Independence implies Cov(X, Y) = 0, so the cross term vanishes.
  3. Hence Var(X + Y) = 4 + 9 = 13.

Answer: Option (B) — 13.

Common misconception. Adding the standard deviations 2 + 3 = 5. Variances add under independence; standard deviations add only in the degenerate case where one variable is a non-negative multiple of the other.

EM-U5-Q23MCQmedium

Random variables X and Y have Var(X) = 4, Var(Y) = 9 and Cov(X, Y) = 3. The correlation coefficient ρ(X, Y) equals

  1. 1/3
  2. 3/4
  3. 1/2
  4. 3
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Correlation is the covariance rescaled by the two standard deviations: ρ(X, Y) = Cov(X, Y) / (σXσY), so it is dimensionless and lies in [−1, 1].

Step by step.

  1. Standard deviations: σX = √4 = 2 and σY = √9 = 3.
  2. Denominator: σXσY = 6.
  3. So ρ = 3/6 = 1/2.

Answer: Option (C) — 1/2.

Common misconception. Dividing by the variances 4 · 9 = 36 to get 1/12, or reporting the covariance 3 itself. Correlation is normalised by standard deviations, which is exactly why it can never exceed 1.

EM-U5-Q24MSQmedium

For random variables X and Y with finite variances, which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. −1 ≤ ρ(X, Y) ≤ 1 always.
  2. ρ(X, Y) = 0 implies that X and Y are independent.
  3. Cov(aX + b, cY + d) = ac · Cov(X, Y).
  4. Replacing X by 3X + 1 and Y by 2Y + 5 leaves ρ unchanged.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Correlation measures linear association only. It is bounded by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, is invariant under affine changes that keep the sign, and vanishes without forcing independence.

Step by step.

  1. Cauchy–Schwarz gives |Cov(X, Y)| ≤ σXσY, hence |ρ| ≤ 1. (A) is true.
  2. Uncorrelated is weaker than independent: with X ∼ U(−1, 1) and Y = X² we get Cov(X, Y) = E[X³] = 0 yet Y is a function of X. (B) is false.
  3. Affine shifts do not move the centred variables and the scalings pull out linearly: Cov(aX + b, cY + d) = ac Cov(X, Y). (C) is true.
  4. Here ac = 6 > 0, so both covariance and the product of standard deviations are multiplied by 6 and the ratio is unchanged. (D) is true.

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D).

Common misconception. Reading zero correlation as independence. It is equivalent only in the joint normal case; in general Y = X² with symmetric X is uncorrelated but completely dependent.

EM-U5-Q25NAThard

Random variables X and Y satisfy E[X] = 2, E[Y] = 3, E[XY] = 10, Var(X) = 4 and Var(Y) = 9. The correlation coefficient ρ(X, Y) is ______. (Give the answer correct to two decimal places.)

Show answer & solution

Answer: 0.67

Detailed solution

Concept. Two steps: recover the covariance from Cov(X, Y) = E[XY] − E[X]E[Y], then divide by the product of the standard deviations.

Step by step.

  1. Covariance: Cov(X, Y) = 10 − (2)(3) = 10 − 6 = 4.
  2. Standard deviations: σX = 2 and σY = 3, so the denominator is 6.
  3. Hence ρ = 4/6 = 2/3 ≈ 0.6667, that is 0.67 to two places.

Answer: 0.67 (exactly 2/3).

Common misconception. Using E[XY] = 10 as the covariance, which ignores the E[X]E[Y] = 6 correction and would give ρ = 10/6 > 1. A correlation above 1 is an immediate signal that the centring step was skipped.

EM-U5-Q46MCQeasy

Let X and Y be independent with Var(X) = 2 and Var(Y) = 3. Then Var(2X − 3Y) equals

  1. 35
  2. 5
  3. 13
  4. 30
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. For independent variables Var(aX + bY) = a²Var(X) + b²Var(Y), whatever the signs of a and b.

Step by step.

  1. Var(2X − 3Y) = 4 Var(X) + 9 Var(Y).
  2. = 4(2) + 9(3).
  3. = 8 + 27 = 35.

Answer: Option (A) — 35.

Common misconception. Subtracting the second term because of the minus sign, giving 8 − 27 or 8 − 9. Variance always adds; the sign disappears when the coefficient is squared.

EM-U5-Q47MCQeasy

For a random variable X with finite second moment, Cov(X, X) equals

  1. 0
  2. E[X²]
  3. Var(X)
  4. 1
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Covariance of a variable with itself is its variance: Cov(X, X) = E[X²] − (E[X])².

Step by step.

  1. Cov(X, X) = E[(X − μ)(X − μ)].
  2. = E[(X − μ)²].
  3. = Var(X).

Answer: Option (C) — Var(X).

Common misconception. Answering E[X²], which is the second moment about the origin, not the centred second moment.

EM-U5-Q48MCQmedium

Random variables X and Y have Var(X) = 9, Var(Y) = 16 and correlation coefficient ρ(X, Y) = 0.5. Then Cov(X, Y) equals

  1. 3
  2. 6
  3. 12
  4. 24
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. ρ = Cov(X, Y)/(σ_X σ_Y), so Cov(X, Y) = ρ σ_X σ_Y — the product of the standard deviations, not of the variances.

Step by step.

  1. σ_X = 3 and σ_Y = 4.
  2. Cov(X, Y) = 0.5 × 3 × 4.
  3. = 6.

Answer: Option (B) — 6.

Common misconception. Multiplying ρ by the variances 9 × 16, which gives 72, or by 9 × 4 and losing the square roots.

EM-U5-Q49MSQmedium

For random variables with finite variances, which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Cov(X, Y) = 0 does not imply that X and Y are independent in general.
  2. Var(X − Y) = Var(X) + Var(Y) − 2Cov(X, Y).
  3. If Y = −2X + 3 then ρ(X, Y) = −1.
  4. ρ(X, Y) = 1 implies Y = aX + b with a < 0.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Zero covariance removes the cross term from the variance of a sum but does not force independence; |ρ| = 1 exactly when the pair is affinely related, and the sign of the slope fixes the sign of ρ.

Step by step.

  1. A: take X uniform on [−1, 1] and Y = X²; then Cov(X, Y) = 0 while the two are dependent.
  2. B is the expansion Var(X − Y) = Var X + Var Y − 2Cov(X, Y).
  3. C: the slope is −2, which is negative, so ρ = −1.
  4. D fails: ρ = 1 forces a positive slope a > 0.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Treating zero correlation as independence, and reading |ρ| = 1 as 'some straight-line relation' while ignoring that the sign of ρ fixes the sign of the slope.

EM-U5-Q50NAThard

Random variables X and Y satisfy Var(X) = 4, Var(Y) = 9 and Cov(X, Y) = −3. The correlation coefficient ρ(X, Y) is ______.

Show answer & solution

Answer: -0.5

Detailed solution

Concept. ρ = Cov(X, Y)/(σ_X σ_Y), with σ the square root of the stated variance; the sign of the covariance carries over to ρ.

Step by step.

  1. σ_X = 2 and σ_Y = 3.
  2. ρ = −3/(2 × 3).
  3. = −0.5.

Answer: -0.5.

Common misconception. Dropping the sign, which loses the direction of the relation, or dividing by the variances 4 × 9 instead of by 2 × 3.

What this test contains

25 questions on Unit 5 (Continuous Probability & Distributions), Set 3 (second pass) over the unit. All five subtopics are covered: random variables, CDF and PDF — normalising a piecewise density, reading an interval off a distribution function, a conditional expectation, a parabolic density checked on four counts, and the median of a skewed density; uniform distribution — a second moment, a percentile, a conditional mean, what an affine map preserves, and the area beyond a diagonal in the unit square; exponential distribution — an interval probability, the effect of a linear rescaling on the variance, minima and sums of independent lifetimes, the four properties that follow from memorylessness, and the expectation of a later failure; normal distribution — the one-sigma band, a left tail, a standardisation against two endpoints, an affine map of a normal variable, and a percentile; and correlation and covariance — a perfect negative linear relation, the bilinearity of covariance, a variance of a sum built from ρ, a full numerical check of one covariance, and a linear combination with a negative correlation. Composition: 15 MCQ, 5 MSQ, 5 NAT; difficulty 10 easy, 10 medium, 5 hard; 60 minutes, 35 marks. MCQ carries 1 mark, MSQ and NAT carry 2 marks, there is no negative marking, and an MSQ scores only when the exact set of correct options is selected. This is a practice marking scheme, not the official GATE scheme.

Concept map used to build the questions

  • A density is a height, a distribution function is accumulated mass. Only the integral of f is fixed at one; intervals are read as differences of F, and a conditional probability renormalises the density onto the conditioning event.
  • Uniform means flat, and flat survives only affine maps. Shifts and scalings keep a uniform variable uniform, squaring and rooting do not; any interval of equal length carries equal mass, so midpoints, percentiles and conditional means are read off directly.
  • The exponential is the memoryless law. Mean and standard deviation are both 1/λ, the hazard rate is constant, minima stay exponential (rates add) while sums become gamma (shapes add), and the tail is a single exponential e−λt.
  • The normal is closed under affine maps and linear combinations. Standardise with σ, never with the variance; the one-sigma, two-sigma and three-sigma bands carry about 68, 95 and 99.7 per cent, and a percentile is read backwards through the table.
  • Covariance is bilinear, correlation is scale-free. Every variance of a sum or difference carries 2ab·Cov; ρ lies in [−1, 1], saturates at ±1 exactly on a straight line, and is untouched by shifts and by positive scalings.

Source categories

  • STANDARD_TEXTBOOK — written from the standard treatment of the topic (densities and distribution functions, the uniform, exponential and normal families, correlation and covariance).
  • ORIGINAL_SYNTHESIS — designed for this series to test a specific trap or a calculation that the textbook examples do not cover.
